3. Common Syntax Errors: 

In this video I'm going to show you some of the most common compile time errors that I see amongst junior developers. If you have some programming experience, feel free to skip this video. So, what are these common errors? The first one is when you declare a variable but you forget to specify it's type. Something like this.

So, what is name here? It's a string. And, we need to specify that here. In some languages like Python, we don't have to specify the data type. So that's the reason a lot of people new to Java forget to specify the data type of their variables.

Now let's revert this back, another error is when you forget to add a semi colon at the end of the statement. Look, here we have a red underline, and it's already telling us that a semi colon is expected. So in Java you should always terminate your statements with a semi colon, unless you're defining a code block. So, here we have a code block, here we have another code block. We don't need to terminate these blocks with a semi colon.

Now, another common error is when you call a method but forget to add parenthesis. Let's say we want to print something on the terminal, so, we call the print line method, but we forget to add parenthesis. So here we have a compilation error, cannot resolve symbol print line. So here we need to add parenthesis because this is a method. Another common issue is when you want to print a string but you forget to add quotes, so here we need to add double quotes, and we cannot use single quotes for strings, single quotes only represent single characters.

So here we need double quotes, another common issue is when you misspell or incorrectly capitalize a variable. For example, here we have this name variable, if we misspell it and forget the e, or incorrectly capitalize it, we're going to get a compilation error. Because as you know, Java is a case sensitive language, so it's sensitive to lower case and upper case letters. So always make sure to spell and capitalize your variables and other identifiers properly. Now let's fix this, here's another common issue.

Let's say we declare a variable called class. Class is a reserved keyword in Java, so we cannot use it as an identifier for a variable or a class or a method name. And finally, another issue that I see quite often amongst beginners is when they use a single equal sign to compare values. For example, let's say we have an integer set to zero. Now I want to compare this with two.

So here we're using two equal signs. But a lot of beginners use a single equal sign for comparison, and this results in a compilation error. Take a look. Java is expecting a boolean expression that produces a boolean value, but we have an assignment statement. So we're assigning two to this variable.

So these are the common issues that I personally have observed a lot amongst beginners. Next we're going to look at debugging Java applications.








4. Debugging Java Applications: 

In this tutorial, I'm going to show you how to debug Java applications. Debugging means finding and removing errors in your program. So here we have this simple Java program, and our main method, we print the start message on the terminal, then we call the print numbers method that I have defined down here, this method should print numbers zero, one, two, three, and four. And finally we print the finish message on the terminal. Now, let me run this program, you only see these two numbers.

These are even numbers. So our program has a bug, we want to find and fix this bug. Let me show you. So, you start by inserting a break point on a statement. So here on the first line of our main method, we can click on this gray area to insert a break point.

We also have a shortcut for this, so if you look at the run menu on the top, down below you should see an item called toggle line break point. Unfortunately, it's not visible in my recording window, but the shortcut on mac is command and f eight. So these shortcuts are very useful in inserting or removing break points, see? Or we can simply click here to remove this breakpoint or add it again. Now we run our program using the debugger, and the execution will stop right here, before this statement.

So this is the benefit of using break points. In a large program, we can put a break point somewhere in the middle of the program where we are suspicious that we have a bug. So Java will execute all statements up to that point. Now, to run our application using the debugger, we go to the run menu, look at this item, debug main. The shortcut on Mac is control n d.

So let's press control n d, okay, our program started, we can see this line is highlighted which means it's not executed yet. Now over here we see a new panel which looks a little bit scary at the beginning, but trust me it's super easy. On the top we have this toolbar, and here we can execute the code line by line. So the first item here is step over, you can see the shortcut is f eight, and with this we can execute the current line. So, let's execute that, beautiful, now we are on line six, if you look at the console or terminal window, you can see the start message was printed here.

Alright, now we are on this line, once again we can step over this line, and if we look at the console we can see these two numbers. So this is where we are having a problem. Instead of seeing the numbers 01234, we only see 02. So we need to investigate the code inside this method. But now we're on this line.

So I'm going to rerun our program, and next time we want to step into this method and see what's going on there. So using this icon, we can rerun our program, as you can see the shortcut is command and r on mac. Alright? Here we are, let's step over this line, now we are on line six, this time we're going to step into this method and see exactly what is happening there. So here we have another icon, step into and the shortcut is f7.

Alright? Here we are, we have a for loop, I is set to zero, as long as I is less than limit, we're incrementing I by two. So this is the reason we have this bug. Instead of displaying all the sequential numbers, we're displaying only the even numbers. But before we fix this, let's look at other tools we have here.

We have this variables window which automatically detects variables that are currently in the scope and shows their value. So here we have limit set to four, as you can see limit is the parameter of this function. And four is the value that we pass here. So this variables window is really helpful, it allows us to look at the value of various variables and see why something is not working. Now currently we haven't executed this line, that's why we don't see I in this window.

Let's step over this line, now you can see I is set to zero, so we are in the first iteration of this loop, let's execute this line as well, back to the console window, so we saw zero, now let's step over this line one more time, we are on line 12 and on the right side you can see the current value of I. So these are some additional hints that IntelliJ adds to our code, it makes it really easy to debug our applications. Now back to the debugger panel, most of the time you see the variables that are important here, but if there is something that is not detected, you can always add a watch. With a watch, you can watch a variable. So here, you can click on this icon, new watch, and you can type the name of a variable, like I.

So as you can see I is set to two. Now as you debug your applications and add more watches, this window might get a little bit too busy, so you can remove the watches that you don't need anymore. Now, we identify the source of the problem, it's this statement over here. Instead of incrementing I by two, we want to increment it by one. So, let's change this to I plus plus Now to see the changes we need to rerun our program, we need to restart it.

So, let's click on this icon, once again we're at the beginning of the main method, as you work on larger programs, you don't want to start from the main method. You want to start from where there's a problem. So, I'm going to remove this breakpoint from here and add another break point to this method, now using this play icon, we can continue executing up to the next break point, which is what we just placed in this method. There you go. So next time we restart our program, the execution starts here.

This is much faster than manually executing all statements up to this point. So once again, let's step over this line, and then print I on the terminal, one more time, now I is one, let's keep going, now I is two, beautiful, it's working properly. Now let's say we're done with this and we don't want to keep executing these two lines because we're in a loop and this is repetitive and time consuming. So if you're done with a method we can always step out and here's the icon for stepping out of a method. The shortcut is shift and f eight.

So, let's click this, now we're back to the main method, so we can go back to the console window, we see all the numbers 0123, four is not printed here because we still have a bug in this method, we should use the less than or equal to operator. So this is how we debug Java applications using IntelliJ. If you use a different code editor, the process is exactly the same, it's just the shortcuts that are different. Pretty much every code editor these days has these tools built into it. So you can step over a method, you can step into it, you can step out and so on.

When you're done debugging, you can click on the stop icon to terminate this session. Now, one last thing before I finish this tutorial. Let's start another debugging session, so here we are inside the print numbers method, we have this window here frames, and this shows all the methods that are called in the reverse order. So let me expand this a little bit, our program started here, in the main method. And on line six, which you can see highlighted over here, we called the print numbers method, so the control moved to the print numbers method, now if this method also called another method, that method would be listed here.

So in this window we can see all the methods that are called in the reverse order. This is also called the call stack, because all the methods that are called are stacked on top of each other. This is really useful in debugging large applications, you can put a break point somewhere in the middle of the code, and look at the frames window to see how you got there. You can see all the methods that are called to get to that point. Very very useful.

So this is all about debugging Java applications, it's a very important skill that every developer must master. I hope you learned a lot and thank you for watching.

package com.codewithmosh; 

public class Main {
	public static void main(String[] args) {
		System.out.println("Start");  
		printNumbers(4); 
		System.out.println("Finish"); 
	}
	
	public static void printNumbers(int limit){
		for(int i = 0; i < limit; i += 2)
			System.out.println(i); 
	}
	
}



package com.codewithmosh; 

public class Main {
	public static void main(String[] args) {
		System.out.println("Start");  
		printNumbers(4); 
		System.out.println("Finish"); 
	}
	
	public static void printNumbers(int limit){
		for(int i = 0; i <= limit; i++)
			System.out.println(i); 
	}
	
}













5. Packaging Java Applications: 

The last thing we want to cover in this course is deploying java applications. So once you build a java application, you want to deploy it so other people can use it. And that's what I'm going to show you in this tutorial. Before we get started, I just need to clarify that our focus here is on deploying console or command line applications, like the applications we have built so far. Sometimes we call them programs to differentiate from other sophisticated applications like mobile apps or web applications.

Deploying web or mobile apps requires additional steps that go beyond the scope of this course. So, let's say you have built a java program and you want to give it to someone else to use. You need to package your code into a jar file. Jar is short for Java archive, and it's a package file format that contains all your code for distribution. Once you have a jar file, you can give it to anyone who has installed Java runtime environment on their computer.

If they don't have java run time environment, they can get it from java.com/download. Now let me show you how to create a jar file in IntelliJ. So, here we have a simple program to print this hello word message on the terminal, on the top, under the file menu, let's go to project structure. Now here on the left side, select artifacts, an artifact is an assembly or combination of all our project assets that we put together. Let's add a new artifact here, so we have various types of artifacts, we have jar files, android applications, java fx applications and so on.

Java fx is a platform for creating desktop applications. Now in this demo we're going to go with jar, and here we should select from modules with dependencies. Now in this project we have a single module that is hello world, and module is another level of abstraction above packages. Now here we need to select our main class, so let's click on this icon, currently we have only a single class in this project, if we had multiple classes, all these classes would be listed here. So let's double click on the main class, now if this project uses other java libraries, we can decide what to do with those libraries.

For now we don't have to worry about this, so let's click on okay, beautiful, so now we have a new artifact for building this project as a jar file. Now let's click on okay, and finally to build this jar file, we go to the build menu, and select build artifacts, so currently we have only a single artifact, this jar file, we can build it, we can rebuild it, we can clean it, which removes all files in the directory that contains this jar file. Rebuild is basically the combination of clean and build. Sometimes if things are not working properly, it's good to rebuild an artifact. So, let's build this, it's going to take a few seconds, alright, now in the project panel, let's expand the out folder, here we have artifacts, we have hello world underline jar, and in this folder we have this jar file.

Now, we right click on this, and in this context menu we go to open in terminal. Unfortunately, it's not visible in my recording window, but it's somewhere down the bottom. So open this on terminal, so here we have a terminal window pointing to this folder. In this folder we have a single file, that is our jar file, now to run this program, we use java, so this will invoke java virtual machine as I explained earlier in the course, now here we add a switch dash jar, and then we type the name of our jar file. So hello world dot jar.

If you're on Mac or Linux, make sure to use the right capitalization, because these operating systems are case sensitive. Now enter and here is the output of our program. So we can give this jar file to anyone who has the Java run time environment on their machine, and they can easily execute it. So that's all about building and deploying java applications as jar files.

Ultimate Java Part 2: Object-oriented Programming


Getting Started:

1. Introduction: 


Welcome to the second part of my ultimate Java series. In this part we're gonna talk about object oriented programming. Before we get started, I'm assuming that you know the basics of Java fairly well. You should know your variables, data types, methods, control flow statements, pretty much everything I've covered in the first part of this series. If not, I would highly encourage you to start from the first part and make sure that you know the basics well before we talk about the intermediate topics in this course.

My name is Moshe and I'm super excited to be your instructor in this series. So, let's jump in and get started.







2. Programming Paradigms: 

In computer science we have various programming paradigms or styles of programming like procedural, functional, object oriented, event driven, logic, aspect oriented, and so on. Out of all these, object oriented and functional are the two most popular paradigms we use these days. So these programming paradigms are ways or styles of writing code, they're not programming languages. We classify programming languages based on the paradigms they support. Some languages like small talk support a single paradigm, other languages like Python, Ruby, Java, and JavaScript to name a few, support multiple paradigms.

In object oriented programming, everything is based on the concept of objects. These objects are units that contain some data, also called state, and operations on the data are also called behavior. These are the methods or functions that modify the data. So in object oriented programming, we bring together the data and methods that operate on it in a single object. In functional programming, we take the opposite approach.

We assume that data and behavior are fundamentally different, so we keep them separate. This is a completely different style of programming. We can solve the same problem using both these paradigms, your code will end up looking different. Now, which style is better? Well, it depends on who you ask.

Some people like John Smith here love a certain style of programming, and they try to solve every single programming problem in the world that way. Here's another John Smith who hates object oriented programming because he tried it and failed. In my opinion, he never learned it properly, and it's not his fault. The problem is that object oriented programming is complex, and most books and courses out there fail to explain it properly. So back to our question, what programming paradigm is the best?

The answer is, it depends on the problem you're trying to solve. One of the major issues we have in the software industry these days is that developers are more excited about languages, tools, and frameworks than solving problems. A lot of developers out there, even those with years of experience have no idea what I mean by problem solving. They think they do, but they really don't. Problem solving is the process of defining a problem, identifying and comparing different solutions, and picking the one that best solves the problem with respect to the context and constraints.

A lot of developers out there dismiss the context and constraints and they see things as black or white. They have their favorite languages and tools and they use them for every single problem. Now back to our question, some people claim that object oriented programming is often a better choice for creating graphical user interfaces and games. On the other hand, functional programming makes more sense for applications that require a high level of reliability or problems that involve messages being passed around and getting transformed along the way. But this is just a basic guideline, don't take it as a hard and fast rule.

In a single application, we may use different paradigms. We may use the event driven paradigm in certain parts where it makes the most sense, and functional or object oriented in other parts. It all depends on the problem we're trying to solve. No paradigm works best in all situations. Remember, in software engineering, there's no such a thing as one size fits all.

Every problem is different. So there's no such a thing as the best language or the best paradigm or the best framework. It all depends on the problem, and more importantly on the context and the budget. So don't fall in love with one solution and apply to every single problem like this guy over here. So now that you know object oriented is a programming paradigm, let's look at it's benefits next.

3. Benefits Of Object-oriented Programming: 

So object oriented programming is about objects. These objects interact with each other to perform various tasks. As an analogy, think of a car. Cars consist of hundreds or thousands of collaborating objects. Almost all these objects are reusable and replaceable.

So if any of them malfunctions, a technician can pull it out and replace it with another object. Applications built using object oriented programming techniques are exactly the same. Using these techniques, we can reduce complexity by breaking down a large complex application into smaller, more manageable, and easier to maintain objects. If an object is not working properly, we can only focus on the code for that object, not the entire application. We can either fix that object or plug in a different object as a replacement.

Another benefit is that we'll have more opportunities to reuse code. Once we build and test an object, we can use it in other applications. For example, let's say we're building an image editing application. In this application we're going to give our users various filters to apply to their photos. Once we build these filters, then we can reuse them in other image editing applications.

And this means we're going to spend less time building applications. Now this is where things get interesting. These are the real benefits of object oriented programming. But there are folks out there like this John Smith who claim that these benefits are all theoretical and have no application in the real world because they could never reuse their objects or replace them with other objects. But in my opinion, the reason John failed is because he didn't know how to do object oriented programming properly.

So just because you don't know how to use a tool, it doesn't mean that tool is bad. Also, another thing is that people who love functional programming argue that we get the same benefits, and to be honest that is true. But in this course we're not going to talk about functional programming. All I want you to take away is that both object oriented and functional programming are great and have benefits, but neither work best in all situations. So my advice to you is to learn both these paradigms well, and then choose them wisely depending on the problem you're trying to solve.

Easier Maintenance
Code Reuse
Faster Development

2. Classes and Objects: 

Let's start this section by taking a closer look at classes and objects. First, let me clarify something. The words classes and objects are often used interchangeably, but they're fundamentally different. A class is a blueprint or a template for creating objects. For example, we can have a class called car, and in this class we can define everything we need to know about a car.

So a car has attributes like whether it's starter or not, it's current speed, it's current gear and so on. In programming we implement these attributes using fields, which are like variables that we define in a class. So we store data like the current gear of a car in this field. Now, a car can also have behaviors or methods like start, stop, break, change gear, and so on. When we call these methods, the state of a car changes.

For example, when we call the change gear method, the value of the current gear field changes. So this car class is a template or blueprint for creating new objects. An object is an instance of a class, so based on this template, we can create many instances like car one, car two, and car three. All these car objects will have the same fields and methods, but they are independent of each other. Each car object will be in a separate space in memory, and it can be in a separate state.

For example, their current gear and current speed might be different. So the data in each car object can be different. By the way, here I'm using uml to represent these classes and objects, uml is short for unified modeling language, and it's a simple visual language that we use to show classes and their relationships. Now there's more to uml that we can cover in this course, it's a topic for an entirely different course, but here we're going to use a bit of uml to visualize our classes. Let's look at another example.

Think of a lamp. What states and behaviors does a lamp have? A lamp can be in only two possible states, on or off. So we can have one field here called is on, and the type of this field should be boolean. It's either true or false.

Now our lamp class will also have two behaviors or two methods, like turn on and turn off, when we call these methods, the state of the lamp changes. Now here's a question for you, what states and behaviors does a tv object have? Pause the video and think about it for a second. Here's the answer. A tv object can have states like the current volume, the current channel, whether it's turned on or off, and behaviors like turn on, turn off, increase volume, decrease volume and so on.

Alright, now you might say, but Mosh, we don't have cars and TVs in our applications, give me an example of a real software object. Well, think of a text box. Text boxes are everywhere, right? We can have a text box class with attributes like the text that is currently inside the box, it's limit in terms of the number of characters we're allowed to type, it's length in pixels, whether it's in focus or not, whether it's disabled or not and so on. In this class we can have methods like set text, clear, enable, disable, focus and so on.

So now you have a good understanding of classes and objects. Next I'm gonna show you how to create a class in Java.

3. Creating Classes: 

In this tutorial I'm going to show you how to create a new class in Java. Just like the first part of this series, I'm using IntelliJ as my IDE or code editor, if you prefer to use a different editor, that's perfectly fine, it doesn't really matter. So here we have a brand new project, in this project we want to add a text box class. Now in Java we should add each class to a separate file, and this helps us organize our code in a better way. So let's open the project panel and expand the source folder, this is the main package that we have in this project, in this package we have only a single class, the main class.

Now let's right click here and add a new Java class, we're going to call this text box. Now look at the naming convention I have used here. This is Pascal naming convention. So we capitalize the first letter of every word, okay? Make sure the kind is set to class, okay, so here's our text box class, let's collapse this window, this public keyword we have here is an access modifier, and it determines if other classes in this project can use this class or not.

We're going to talk about access modifiers later in this section. So for now we're just going to go with public, which means this class is visible to all other classes in this project. So we have public class text box, now inside these curly braces we're going to add three members. First is the text field, and we use that to store the text inside the text box, so we start with the public access modifier, then we specify the type of this field, just like declaring a variable, that's going to be a string, and then we give it a name. Now here we are using the camel casing notation, with camel case, we should capitalize the first letter of every word, except the first word.

So here we have only a single word, that's why all characters are in lower case. So this is our field. Now let's declare a couple of methods. We start with public, then we specify the return type of this method, I'm going to use void because I don't want to return any values from this method. Let's call it set text, and give it a parameter, string text.

Now in this method, we want to set the value of this text field based on what is passed here. So we type text equals text, but this code is confusing, it's not clear what we have on the right or the left side of the assignment operator. So in situations where the name of a parameter is exactly the same as the name of a field, we can use the this keyword. Let me show you. So this is a reference to the current object.

So here we can use the dot operator to see the members of this object. Here we have the text field, look, the icon of this member is f, which represents a field, we also have these methods like clone, equals, and so on, we didn't declare this method, so how did they appear here? Well, this happens through a mechanism called inheritance, so every class that we declare in Java automatically inherits these methods from another class. We're going to look at that in the next section. So, now, we set this text field to the value of this text parameter.

So here's one method. Now let's declare another method, once again, public void, we're going to call this clear, and in this method we don't want to have any parameters. We just want to clear the text of this text box. So, we can type this, the text, we set this to an empty string. Now in this case, we don't really need to use the this keyword, because we don't have a parameter called text, so if we remove this, it's obvious that this text is the field that we declared over here.

So, here's our text box class with three members, a field, and two methods. Now technically in Java we should not declare fields as public, so if you're a bit more experienced and are watching this course as a refresher, you might be wondering why I declared this as public. This is purely because I want to take you on a step by step journey, so over the next few lectures, we're going to look at this in detail. Alright, we have created a new class, next I'm going to show you how to create instances of this class.

4. Creating Objects: 

So we created this text box class, now let's create a new text box object. So back to our main class, here in the main method, let's declare a text box variable, we call it text box one, and initialize it like this. Use the new operator to create a new instance of the text box class. So, here we're instantiating the text box class, which means we're creating a new instance of this class, okay? Now before going any further, there's something we can improve here.

Look at this text box, it's repeated twice here, and this is making our code a little bit noisy. We can simplify this using the var keyword in Java. So we can replace the first text box with var, and the Java compiler will detect the type of this variable based on what we have on the right side of the assignment operator. So this var is different from the var that we have in languages like JavaScript, in javascript when we use var, we can declare a variable and give it different types of values. So here we can set text box one to a number, but as you can see this is not valid in Java, because in Java variables have a static type, so once we set the type of a variable, we cannot change it throughout the lifetime of our programs.

So using the var keyword, we can write shorter and cleaner code, and we let the Java compiler determine the type of this variable based on what we have here. Alright, now we use the dot operator to look at the members of this class, Here we have a couple of methods, set text and clear, as well as the text field. So, let's call this set text method, and here we can pass box one, next we can print this on a terminal, so s o u t tab, to get this print line statement, and here we type text box one dot text. Let's run this program, so we get box one, beautiful. Now what if we remove this line of code?

And print the text field. We get null. Well, in the first part of this series we talked about primitive types and reference types. Primitive types are numbers, booleans and characters. Everything else is a reference type.

So back to our text box class, this text field is a string object, strings are reference types. And by default, if we don't initialize these reference types, they're set to null. Now, these nulls can be dangerous and they can crash our programs. Let me show you. So, back to our main class, what if we want to display the text inside this text box in upper case?

So because text is a string object, we can use the dot operator to access it's members. So here I'm going to call the two upper case method, now let's run this program and see what happens. Our program crashed, we got an exception, and the type of this exception is null pointer exception which is one of the most popular exceptions in Java. So, we got this exception because this text field is null, so it's not referencing a real object in memory. And here we're trying to call this method on an object that doesn't exist.

That's why we get a null pointer exception. Now how can we prevent this from happening? We can go back to our text box class, and initialize this field to an empty string. So the text field will never be null. Now back to our main class, if we run this program one more time, we simply see a blank line here, so our program didn't crash.

Beautiful. Now one more time, let's set the text in this text box, so we call text box one, that set text, let's say box one. Now similarly I'm going to create another text box object, so var text box two, we set this to a new instance of the text box class, but here we're going to give this text box a different value. So, text box two, dot set text to box two. If you print this, we're going to see a different value.

Take a look. So we can see that these objects are completely independent of each other, they have different data, they are in different states, here we can see the state of the first text box and below that we can see the state or the data in the second text box.

5. Memory Allocation: 

Alright, now let's talk about memory allocation. I'm going to show you exactly what happens under the hood when we create new objects in Java. So Java manages two different areas of the memory. One is called the heap, and this is where it stores our objects, the other area is called the stack, and this is where it stores short lived variables like all the primitives as well as the variables that store references to objects on the heap. What do you mean by that?

Well, look at this piece of code. When Java run time executes this, first it will evaluate what we have on the right side of the assignment operator. So it will create a new text box object and it will store it on the heap. Now let's say the address of this object on the heap is 100, then Java run time is going to execute this other part of the code. Here it will allocate some memory on the stack, and in this memory location it will store the address of our text box object on the heap.

So this variable that we have here is referencing an object on the heap. That's why we refer to these variables as reference types. Because these variables don't store the actual values. Primitive types on the other hand are different. When we declare an integer, that integer is stored on the stack, and the value that we have in that variable will be stored in that memory location.

Now let me show you something interesting. Here I'm going to declare another text box variable, and I'm going to set it to text box one. So once again we're using the var keyboard, so we're letting the Java compiler detect the type of this variable, if we look at what we have on the right side, it knows that this variable is a text box object, so the type of this other variable will also be a text box. Now here's the interesting thing about this piece of code. In this example, both these variables are referencing the same text box object on the heap.

So we don't have two different text box objects, there's only a single text box object on the heap, and we have two variables on the stack that are referencing this object. Because on line seven, we're copying the value that we have in this variable into this other variable. What do we have here? It's the address of our text box object on the heap, like 100, right? So with this piece of code, both these variables are referencing the same object, and that means if I modify that object through one of these references or one of these variables, the changes would be visible to the other variable.

So here I'm going to use the second reference, the second variable, and with this we're going to set the text to hello world. Now let's print the value of the first text box. Because both these variables are referencing the same object, the changes are going to be visible. Take a look. So we get hello world, and this proves that both these variables are referencing the same object.

Now what about memory deallocation? Well, in Java unlike c plus plus we don't have to worry about deallocating memory. Java will automatically take care of that. And this is how it works. When we exit a method, Java run time will immediately remove all the variables that are stored on the stack.

So in this example, these two variables are stored on the stack and they will be immediately removed after we finish executing the main method. Now we end up with an object that is on the heap, and there are no references to this object. So there is another process in the background that is watching these objects on the heap, if an object becomes unused for a certain period of time, that process is going to automatically remove that object on the heap. This is called garbage collection. So Java run time has this component called garbage collector which will automatically remove unused objects on the heap.

So this is all about memory allocation and deallocation in Java.

6. Procedural Programming: 

Earlier in the course, I told you about the benefits of object oriented programming. Now, over the next few lectures, I'm going to show you these benefits in action. So we're going to start off by writing some code using the procedural paradigm, this is the paradigm that we had before object orientation, and it's based on procedure or function calls. So I'm going to write some code using the procedural paradigm, I'm going to talk about the problems with this code, and then over the next few lectures, we're going to apply object oriented programming techniques and make this code object oriented. So you will see the benefits in action.

So, let's say we're going to write a program to calculate the wage of our employees. Let's declare three variables, base salary, we'll set this to 50,000, let's declare another variable called extra hours, now for simplicity I'm declaring all these variables as integers, so we don't have to worry about the optimum data type for each variable. Okay? Now we set this to ten and one last variable called hourly rate, we set this to 20. Alright, now let's implement a method for calculating the wage of an employee.

So, after our main method we're going to declare another method, now our main method is declared as static, we're going to talk about static methods later in this section, for now all I want you to know is that because the main method is declared as static, we need to declare this other method as static, so we can easily call it in the main method. So public static, we want to return an integer, and we want to call this calculate wage. Now let's add the parameters in this method. So, we need base salary, as well as extra hours, and finally hourly rate. And here we add the braces, inside this method we want to return the base salary plus extra hours times hourly rate.

I think that's pretty straight forward, I really don't need to explain this. So here we have a method for calculating the wage, now back to our main method, after we declare these variables, we're going to call this method pass our variables, so base salary, extra hours, and hourly rate, this will return an integer, so we can store it in this variable, and finally we can print this on the terminal. Very straight forward. So take a look, here is the total. 50,200.

Now, what is wrong with this code? Well, technically there is nothing wrong here, because this is a very simple program, our main method has only five lines of code, and we have another method that is only one line long. However, if we extend this program, if we add more features to it, with this style of programming, we'll end up with a very fat main method, so our main method is going to get bloated with so many lines of code, yes we can definitely extract some of these methods and put them in other methods in this class, like our calculate wage method, but again our main class will end up getting so bloated. This is what we call procedural programming. So we don't really have the notion of classes and objects, we have a bunch of variables and a bunch of functions, also called procedures, we're simply calling procedures in this program.

Now let me tell you something interesting. I've seen a lot of developers, a lot of Java developers who write code like this and they assume they're doing object oriented programming because Java is an object oriented programming language. So just because they use the main class or declare a few other classes they assume they're doing object oriented programming, but that's not true. Just because you're using classes and objects in your programs doesn't mean you're doing object oriented programming. Object oriented programming is way more than using classes and objects.

It has a lot of techniques that I'm going to show you throughout this course. So, this is what we call procedural programming, even though we're writing this code inside of a class. Here's one of the symptoms of code written in procedural style. You will end up with methods that have so many parameters. Now in this case, this method is not too bad, here we only have three parameters, but I've seen developers who have functions or methods with five or even 10 parameters, sometimes even more.

If you have methods like that, that's a definite symptom that you are not doing object oriented programming. So if you're constantly calling methods and passing so many arguments, that means you're doing procedural programming. So, back to the problems with the procedural code. Let me code like this, we'll end up with really fat methods or fat classes, programs written this way are very hard to maintain, if something goes wrong, you have to go all over the place, look at so much code and figure out what you need to change. And as soon as you change something, so many things break.

You will create other bugs in your programs. Another problem with code like this is lack of reusability. So you will end up with so many functions or methods all over the place, and if you want to reuse them in other programs, you have to go copy some code from here, some code from there, and then you cannot put them together because their parameters or arguments are different, it's just so messy. This is what we call spaghetti code, code that looks like spaghetti. Everything is interconnected with each other.

It's really hard to change programs like this. So, over the next few lectures, we're going to refactor this code, which means we're going to change it's structure and make it object oriented. So, I will see you next.



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

Main.java: 

package com.codewithmosh; 

public class Main{
	public static void main(String[] args){
		int baseSalary = 50_000;
		int extraHours = 10; 
		int hourlyRate = 20; 
		
		int wage = calculateWage(baseSalary, extraHours, hourlyRate)
		System.out.println(wage); 
	}
	
	public static int calculateWage(
		int baseSalary, 
		int extraHours, 
		int hourlyRate
	){
		return baseSalary + (extraHours * hourlyRate);
	}
}















7. Encapsulation: 

In this lecture we're going to apply the first principle of object oriented programming, encapsulation. And that basically means that we should bundle the data and methods that operate on the data inside a single unit or object. So in this program we are storing some data inside these three variables in the main method, these variables are local to this method, so they are not accessible outside of this method. On the other hand we have this calculate weight method, so we are passing those variables as arguments to this method. So here we should follow the encapsulation principle and bundle these variables and this method inside a single unit.

We want to encapsulate them like a capsule inside a single unit, Okay? So what should we call that single unit or class? Here we're dealing with the concept of an employee because we're calculating the wage of that employee. So let's add an employee class to this project. So over here, let's right click our package and add a new class, we're going to call that employee, in this class we're going to declare three fields.

The same as the variables that we declared in our main method. So, public int base salary, but we're not going to initialize this to this value because we don't want to hard code this for all employee objects. This is just a blueprint. So later when we create an employee object, for each employee we'll set the base salary. Okay?

Now let's declare the second field, hourly rate, and finally, extra hours. So three fields. Now we should declare our calculate wage method. So public, we don't need to use static, because earlier I told you that we use static only when we want to call a method from the main method. So public and calculate wage, now what parameters do we need here?

Shall we add the base salary and these other values? No, because we are storing these values inside these fields in this class. So there is really no need to add parameters to this method. So in this method we can simply return base salary plus hourly rate times extra hours. So this is the first sign of object oriented code.

Our calculate wage method has no parameters. Previously we had three parameters, now we have zero parameters. So instead of passing values around, we should encapsulate these values along with the methods that operate on them inside a single object. Now let's make an assumption. Imagine that the base salary and the hourly rate are fixed for one year, but extra hours is going to be different every month.

So we don't want to store that in this field, instead we want to pass that as an argument to this method. So here I'm going to add extra hours and remove it from here. So every month we call this method and pass the extra hours in that month, okay? Now let's go back to our main class, on the top, first we're going to create an employee object. So var employee, we set it to a new employee, and here we can use the dot operator to directly set the value of these fields.

So we set the base salary to 50,000, next we set the hourly rate to 20, now we're going to call the calculate wage method and pass the extra hours, okay? So employee.calculate wage, and we pass 10 as the extra hours. This will return the wage, we store it here, and finally we can print it on the terminal. So, with these changes, we don't need these variables here anymore, we should also remove the call to this method, and that means that we can remove this method from our main class. So with these changes, our main class is now cleaner, it has fewer lines of code, it's easier to maintain, we have encapsulated the data and the method that operates on the data inside a single class, so if tomorrow we want to build another application that involves calculating the wage of our employees, we can simply copy this class to that project or put it in a reusable library so we don't have to copy paste code around.

But wait a second, we're not done yet. This code has an issue, and we're going to look at that next.

8. Getters and Setters - Title: 

Alright, we encapsulated some data and a behavior around employees inside this employee class. Beautiful. However, this implementation has a problem. What if I come here and set the base salary to zero or a negative number. Our employee object is going to go in a bad state, and it's not going to be able to produce the right result.

It's not going to be able to calculate the weight properly. But you might say, Mosh, we should have set the base salary to a negative number to start with. And I totally agree with you. But what if we read these values from the user? So here we need to do some data validation, we want to make sure that the base salary that the user enters is a positive value.

Now here's one way to solve this problem, we can write an if statement, get the base salary from the user interface, and then check to see if it's a negative number. If this condition is true, then we're going to print an error message. However, the problem with this implementation is that every time we use the employee object, we'll have to write this if statement. We don't want to do that. We want to encapsulate this logic inside the employee class.

Here's how we're going to do it. What if we hide this field so it's not accessible outside of the employee class and instead we declare a method like set base salary. So we pass this value to this method, and we can do our data validation inside this method. Because this method is part of the employee class, wherever we use this class, we're going to have this data validation logic, so we don't have to repeat it all over the place. So, let's go back to our employee class, earlier we talked about access modifiers.

We have declared this field as public, and this means that this field is accessible outside of this class. Now if I change this to private, see what happens. Back to the main class, we have a compilation error here because the base salary field is private and it's not accessible outside of the employee class, Okay? So that was our first step. Now we need to create this method.

So, back to this class, let's add a new method here, public void set base salary, here we can add a parameter like base salary, and in this method we're going to do some data validation. So before we set the base salary field, we want to validate this parameter. So, if base salary is less than or equal to zero, here we're going to throw an exception. So we use the throw statement to raise an exception, and this will cause our program to terminate. So, to do that we need to create a new instance of the illegal argument exception.

We're going to talk about exceptions in the future. For now, all you need to know is that all these exceptions are classes in Java library, when we throw an exception, that exception if not handled will terminate our program. So here we're creating a new instance of this class, over here we can pass an error message to show it to the user. Salary cannot be zero or less. Alright?

So, if this base salary is invalid, we're not going to set our field. Otherwise, we're going to write this base salary equals base salary. Now back to our main class, let's run our program and see what happens. So here's our exception, it terminated our program, as you can see the type of this exception is illegal argument exception. And here's the error message, salary cannot be zero or less.

So, with this method, we're preventing an employee object from going to an invalid state. Now what if you want to read the value of base salary? With the current implementation, we cannot access this field from the outside, so if we type employee dot base salary, we get a compilation error. How can we read this value and print it on the console? Well, we can create a similar method like get base salary, and this will return the value of that field.

So, back to our employee class, let's create a new method, public integer get base salary, we don't need any parameters here, we simply return the value of the base salary field. These methods you see here are very common in Java. We refer to them as setters and getters, because we use them to set or get the value of a field. So in Java we want to make our fields private, and instead we want to add getters and setters to work with them. I'm going to explain the reason in the next lecture.

Now here's an exercise for you. I want you to follow the steps that I did in this lecture, and create a getter and a setter for the hourly rate field. Make it private so it's not accessible from the outside, and then go back to the main class and make the necessary changes. You'll see my solution next. Alright, let me show you a trick.

I'm not going to manually create a getter and a setter, I'm going to ask IntelliJ to automatically create this for me using a shortcut. So with the cursor on this field, look at this light bulb, we can activate this by clicking it or pressing alt and enter. Now look at the first item, encapsulate field hourly rate. Let's go with that, in this dialog box you can see the fields we have in this class, the first field is the base salary which is not selected, this field already has a getter and a setter, and look at the second field, that's our hourly rate. This field is selected and here, intelliJ is suggesting to create a getter and a setter for this field with these names.

Now here we can tell intelliJ if you want both a getter and a setter, sometimes you want a read only field, so we can only create a getter. In that case we will untick the setter, okay? Now for this exercise we want to keep both of them, below this we can tell intelliJ how to change the access modifier of this field after this refactoring. So as you can see, IntelliJ is going to automatically make this field private, and our access modifiers are going to be public. Now let's click on the refactor button down below, there you go.

So this field is now private, we have a getter, there you go, get our array, here we are simply returning the value of this field, and this is our setter, currently we don't have any data validation, we can add it like this, if our rate is less than or equal to zero, once again, we can throw a new illegal argument exception. And we can say, our rate cannot be zero or negative. There you go. So learn these shortcuts, they're very easy, they're very helpful for generating code, so you don't have to type everything by hand. Now back to our main class, let's set the base salary to 50,000, and the hourly rate to 20, now do you see what happened here?

IntelliJ automatically replaced our field assignment statement with a call to the set hourly rate method. This is extremely powerful. So if there are five places where we are directly working with that field, we don't have to go back and manually change all those places. Alright, so this is all about getters and setters in Java.

Employee.java: 

package com.codewithmosh;

public class Employee {

    private int baseSalary;
    private int hourlyRate;
  
    public int calculateWage(int extraHours){
	    return baseSalary + (hourlyRate * extraHours); 
    }
	
	public void setBaseSalary(int baseSalary) {
		if (baseSalary <= 0)
		  throw new IllegalArgumentException("Salary cannot be 0 or less.");
		this.baseSalary = baseSalary;
	}

    public int getBaseSalary() {
        return baseSalary;
    }
	
    public int getHourlyRate() {
        return hourlyRate;
    }

    public void setHourlyRate(int hourlyRate) {
        if (hourlyRate <= 0)
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Hourly rate cannot be 0 or negative.");
        this.hourlyRate = hourlyRate;
    }


}



Main.java: 

package com.codewithmosh; 

public class Main{

	public static void main(String[] args){
		var employee = new Emoployee(); 
		employee.setBaseSalary(-1); 
		employee.setHourlyRate(20); 
		int wage = employee.calculateWage(10); 
		System.out.println(wage); 
	}
	
}
















9. Abstraction: 

Now let's talk about the second principle of object oriented programming, abstraction. Abstraction means we should reduce complexity by hiding unnecessary details in our classes. Unfortunately, this is one of the most misunderstood concepts in object oriented programming. A lot of people don't know what abstraction is all about. So, let me give you a metaphor.

Think of the remote control of your TV. This remote control has a bunch of buttons that allow you to work with this TV, right? Inside this remote control we have an electronic board, a bunch of transistors and so on. Now here's the thing, we don't directly work with these transistors, these are the implementation detail. There's so much complexity there, we don't really understand that, and we don't want to understand that.

All we want to do is to change the channel of our tv. We don't care what happens under the hood. We don't care what states this transistor is going. This is abstraction in action. So with abstraction, we want to hide the implementation detail of a class, and treat it like a black box.

We don't care what's inside, we just have a simple interface to work with. Just like the buttons on a remote control. Now back to our code, in the last lecture we made these fields private, because these fields are like the transistors inside a remote control. We don't want to directly work with these fields, they're implementation detail. Tomorrow we may decide to change how we store data inside an employee object.

Currently we are using two integers, what if tomorrow we decide to replace these two integers with an array? If you make that change, all the other classes that are using this employee class have to be updated. So we want to hide the implementation detail and expose a bunch of methods for others to use. For example, we have this setter and getter here to work with the base salary field. Now here we have a one to one relationship between the underlying field and this getter and setter, but in more complex applications, we are not necessarily going to expose a getter and a setter for every field.

For example, we might have a tv object, we simply call the set channel method, and this method will internally set a few different fields. We don't know what fields are set to what values, we don't care. All we want is to change the channel of our tv. So remember the abstraction principle of object oriented programming. Abstraction says we should reduce complexity by hiding unnecessary details.

But there's more to abstraction, it has another benefit, and that's what we're going to look at next.



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

Reduce Complexity by hiding unnecessary details 












10. Coupling: 

In this lecture we're going to talk about coupling, which is an extremely important principle in object oriented programming. Yet, a lot of developers don't know what coupling is all about. So just because they're creating classes with fields and methods, they think they're doing object oriented programming, but they are not. So what is coupling all about? Coupling is about how much a class is dependent upon or coupled to another class.

Let me give you a metaphor. You use your phone so you're coupled to your phone. Now this is not necessarily a bad thing, but the more you use your phone the more you're dependent on it. If you use your phone every single minute for different reasons, you are really dependent on it. Now, if I take that phone from you, you're going to have a difficult time living.

We have the same concept in object oriented programming. So we want to reduce the coupling between classes. Now, there is no such thing as no coupling or zero coupling, there is always coupling. Because all these classes that we create, they should work together to perform some tasks. We just want to aim to reduce the coupling.

Let me give you an example. Let's say class a is using class b, so it's dependent upon or coupled to this class. Now if we change class b, class a may have to be modified or at least it has to be recompiled. And also any other classes that are dependent on class a may have to be modified or at least recompiled. Now this is not a big issue when we have only two classes in our application, but in a real complex application, you might have thousands of classes.

You don't want to change one class and end up with 100 broken classes. This is the problem with coupling. The more our classes are coupled to each other, the more costly our changes are going to be. So by reducing the coupling, we can reduce the impact of changes. Let me show you this in code.

So here in our main class, we are using the employee class, so the main class is dependent on the employee class. Now, what are our coupling points here? We have a few. Here's our first coupling point where we create a new instance of the employee class, currently we are not passing any values or arguments here, but if in the future we change the employee class such that we have to pass the name of the employee at the time of creation, then we'll have to come back to the main class and pass the name here. So we'll have to modify the main class, and the code in this class has to be recompiled.

Here's another coupling point where we're calling the set base salary method. So, in this example, in our main class we have one, two, three, and four coupling points to the employee class. Now this is not necessarily a bad thing, you really need these coupling points here. But let's take a look at the members of the employee class. So, employee dot here we have a couple of methods that we have not used in this class, get base salary and get hourly rate.

Let's say there is really no requirement to have these methods. So I just created these methods to show you how we can create a getter. If you don't have a requirement to use these methods, it's better to hide or delete them. Because the more methods a class provides, the more other classes are going to get coupled to it. Just like your phone and the apps you have installed on it.

If your phone has only five apps, you have five coupling points to your phone. But if it has 100 apps, you have 100 coupling points to your phone. So in this example, I'm going to go back to our employee class, and make this method private, so we're hiding this from the outside, once again this is abstraction in action, we're hiding the unnecessary detail, we're hiding the implementation detail, and with this we're reducing complexity. Let's do this with the get hourly rate method, so private, now back to the main class, take a look, employee dot, now we only have three methods to use. So the interface of this class is simpler, and it's easier to use.

And we're going to have less coupling in the future. Once again, let me give you a metaphor. You've probably used one of those remote controls that have so many buttons. They're really hard to use, right? In contrast, we have remote controls like this that are super easy to use.

So these buttons on this remote control are like the methods in our classes. We want to reduce the number of these methods exposed outside the class. So this was one example of coupling. We're going to look at another example next.

Employee.java: 

package com.codewithmosh;

public class Employee {

    private int baseSalary;
    private int hourlyRate;
  
    public int calculateWage(int extraHours){
	    return baseSalary + (hourlyRate * extraHours); 
    }
	
	public void setBaseSalary(int baseSalary) {
		if (baseSalary <= 0)
		  throw new IllegalArgumentException("Salary cannot be 0 or less.");
		this.baseSalary = baseSalary;
	}

    private int getBaseSalary() {
        return baseSalary;
    }
	
    private int getHourlyRate() {
        return hourlyRate;
    }

    public void setHourlyRate(int hourlyRate) {
        if (hourlyRate <= 0)
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Hourly rate cannot be 0 or negative.");
        this.hourlyRate = hourlyRate;
    }


}



Main.java: 

package com.codewithmosh; 

public class Main{

	public static void main(String[] args){
		var employee = new Emoployee(); 
		employee.setBaseSalary(-1); 
		employee.setHourlyRate(20); 
		int wage = employee.calculateWage(10); 
		System.out.println(wage); 
	}
	
}

Let's look at another example of coupling. Imagine we want to build a browser application. We probably have to write five to 10 lines of code. So, in this class we're going to have a separate method for getting the ip address of a website.

Now let me show you a very cool shortcut. I'm not going to manually create a method like this, like public string find ip address, forget about it. I'm going to show you my favorite short cut. So, let's pass that here, this method is going to return the ip address as a string. So we store it here. Let's go with that, so we press enter, next is confirming the parameter type. Because we passed a string to this method, it realized that string is probably what we are looking for.

So, press enter to confirm, and one more time, done. Now here it's returning null, let's change this to an IP address like one two seven zero zero one. It doesn't really matter. So, let's print that here, beautiful, now we're going to use this browser class in our main class. So, let me temporarily delete all this code, here we need to create a browser object. Once again, let me show you a very cool shortcut. I'm not going to type browser, browser equals new browser, that's so boring. We're going to do a new browser, enter, and here we have our light bulb, so alt and enter, introduce local variable.

Done. Now here we can change the name of this variable, I'm happy with what IntelliJ is suggesting, so enter, done. Now we can put the carrot on the type, and press alt and enter one more time, and here we're going to replace the explicit type with var. Okay, our code is cleaner, now look at the members of this browser object. We have these three methods, find ip address, navigate, and send http request.

In this application, our main class doesn't really care about these two methods, find ip address and send http request. These are implementation details. All we want to do in this main class is to navigate to a web address. So these two methods are unnecessary and they are polluting the interface of this class. Just like a remote control with so many buttons.

We want to hide this from the outside. So, back to our browser class, when IntelliJ created these methods for us, it declared them as private. That was deliberate. So, let's change this to private, and this one too, now back to the main class. Look at the members of this browser object.

Now we only have the navigate method. And of course we have these other methods, but they're inherited automatically, we don't have to worry about them. So now the interface of this object is much simpler, we're going to have less coupling in the future, because this main class is not going to accidentally call one of these other methods in this browser class. Like send http request or find ip address. If tomorrow we decide to change any of these methods, our main class is not going to get affected.

Let me show you. So if I add a new parameter here, boolean, like cache, because this method is used only internally inside this class, our main class is not affected. You don't see that method here. So with this technique, we're isolating the changes to this browser class. Now we have a compilation error here because we added a new parameter to this method and where we're calling it that is inside the navigate method, we need to pass a boolean value.

So this is the only place where we're modifying code. In contrast, if this method was public, and some of the classes in our application used it, let me show you, so here we're going to call find IP address, pass something here, and then a boolean. Now tomorrow we go back to our browser class and decide to remove this parameter. Now look at our main class. This class is broken, we need to modify this code, and this is how a bad design will cause so many breaking changes in software.

Earlier we talked about this guy here, he hates object oriented programming because he tried it and he failed. This is one of the reasons he could have failed. He didn't know about the coupling, he didn't know about the impact of changes, he didn't know what is abstraction, all he did was create in classes with fields and methods. That is not object oriented programming, Because object oriented programming has a bunch of techniques, it's not just about features like fields and methods, it's a way of thinking, it's a way of building software. So, once again, remember the abstraction principle, we want to reduce the complexity by hiding unnecessary details.

In this example, finding the IP address is a detail, so we're going to hide that, there you go, now we have a compilation problem in this class because we removed this parameter, alright, the problem is gone, now back to our main class, here our browser object only has a single method. It's easier to use and we're going to have less coupling in the future.

12. Constructors: 

Alright, back to our employee example. We have applied a couple of principles of object oriented programming. Encapsulation and abstraction. But this implementation still has a problem. What if I forget to call this or these two methods?

Our employee object will start in an invalid state. The base salary and the hourly rate fields are going to be zero. Let me show you. So temporarily, I'm going to make the base salary public, so we can quickly read it for this demo, before we calculate the wage, let's print employee.base salary. We run the program, and this is zero.

Alright, now let's remove this line, we don't need it anymore, and let's make this field private, so, here we have a bad implementation. Because whoever is using the employee class has to remember to call these methods in order. This is bad design. We don't want to force the consumers or the clients of our classes to use them in a particular way. This is like having a magical remote control that forces you to press a and then b before you can change the channel.

That's really strange. So we want to make the interface of this class as simple as possible. We want to reduce the guesswork. How can we do this? Well, at the time we create this employee object, we want to put it in an initial valid state.

So we want to pass these values over here. And here we have a compilation error, so let me temporarily remove these arguments, We're going to create a constructor in this class. A constructor is a special method that is called when we create a new object. Look at this line. This looks like we're calling a method, right?

Well actually, we do have a method by this very name in our employee class. We didn't create it, but the java compiler automatically creates this method, and this method is what we call a constructor. It's used to construct or create a new employee object. The job of this default constructor is to initialize our fields to their default values. So numbers are going to be zero, booleans are going to be false, and reference types are going to be null.

So back to our employee class, let's create a constructor. After we declare these fields, we declare a constructor method like this. Public, this method is not going to have a return type, not even void. Here we should type the name of the class with the same capitalization, exactly the same. So, employee, we add parenthesis, and here we want to have two parameters.

Base salary and hourly rate. So now we have a custom constructor, and Java compiler is not going to create a default constructor for us. So in this constructor, we want to initialize these fields based on the values that we receive here. Now, there are two ways to do this. One way is to write code like this.

This, that base salary equals base salary. However, with this implementation, I can still pass a zero or a negative value as the base salary. So our employee object will start in an invalid state. So here we should use the setters that we created. These setters will throw an exception, so if we try to create an employee object with invalid values, our program is going to crash.

We'll never have an employee object in a bad state in memory. So, back to our constructor, let's call set base salary, and we give you this value. Similarly we're going to call set hourly rate, and we pass that here. Now back to the main class. Here we have a compilation error, because our employee class has a custom constructor that expects two arguments.

So we're going to pass 50,020 here, now this line is getting a little bit too long, so I'm going to break it down so we can see clearly. Alright? Now, we no longer need to call these methods in order, so, let's delete these two lines, now our main method is even cleaner, it has only three lines of code. So, we use constructors to initialize our objects. If we don't create a constructor, the java compiler will automatically create one for us, this is called the default constructor, and it will initialize all the fields to their default values.

Now one last thing before we finish this lecture, look at the members of the employee class. So we have these two setters and the calculate wage method. Let's assume that once we create an employee object, we don't want to change it's hourly rate or base salary. So once again, we can apply the abstraction principle and hide these details. Back to our employee class, I'm going to make this private, we're only going to use it inside this class.

Once again, let's make this other method private as well, now back to the main class, look at the interface of the employee object. It's very simple, it only has a single method. This is a beautiful implementation, this is object oriented programming in action.

14. Method Overloading: 

There are situations where we need different implementations of a method but with different parameters. For example, look at this calculate wage method. Here we're passing 10 as the extra hours, but what if in a given month this employee doesn't work any extra hours? We can either pass zero here or create a different implementation of this method that doesn't take any parameters. Like this.

This is what we call method overloading. So overloading a method means creating different implementations of it but with different parameters. So, back to our employee class. Here's our calculate weight method, I'm going to select these few lines and press command and d to duplicate them. Like this, right?

Now we have a compilation error because we have repeated this method with the exact same parameter. So we're going to change the second implementation and remove this parameter. Now here we need to change this logic, because we don't have extra hours anymore, we need to remove this expression. So we only return the base salary. This is one way to solve this problem.

Another way is to call the calculate wage method, but pass zero as the extra hours. So we can reuse the logic that is already here. Let me show you. So we call calculate wage and pass zero as the argument. So we have overloaded the calculate wage method twice, in one implementation we get the extra hours, in the other implementation we don't receive any values.

Now back to our main class, now we can call this method with or without parameters. Now in some languages like Python and c sharp, we can set default values for our parameters, so instead of overloading this calculate weight method here, we can set a default value for this parameter. Unfortunately, this is not possible in Java at the time of recording this video. So, the only way to make one or more parameters optional is by overloading a method. However, remember that overloading a method too many times will make your application hard to maintain, because you'll end up with so many different variations and these methods will call each other, it just gets ugly.

In this tutorial, I just wanted to show you how to overload a method, but I personally would prefer not to overload this method and pass zero as the extra hours here. The benefit that we get when overloading this method is really tiny. So if we don't pass any values, we can call this method like this, this is a tiny improvement in terms of making our code cleaner. In my experience, it's often better to overload a method if you're dealing with completely different types of parameters. For example, one implementation might take two integers, the other might take an employee object.

These are radically different. So we'll be aware of method overloading, but don't overuse it.

Employee.java: 

package com.codewithmosh;

public class Employee {

    private int baseSalary;
    private int hourlyRate;
	
	public Employee(int baseSalary, int hourlyRate) {
		setBaseSalary(baseSalary); //can also be done using this.baseSalary = baseSalary;,However, I can still pass a zero or a negative value as the base salary. 
		setHourlyRate(hourlyRate);
    }

  
    public int calculateWage(int extraHours){
	    return baseSalary + (hourlyRate * extraHours); 
    }

    public int calculateWage() {
      return calculateWage(0);
    }
	
	private void setBaseSalary(int baseSalary) {
		if (baseSalary <= 0)
		  throw new IllegalArgumentException("Salary cannot be 0 or less.");
		this.baseSalary = baseSalary;
	}

    private int getBaseSalary() {
        return baseSalary;
    }
	
    private int getHourlyRate() {
        return hourlyRate;
    }

    private void setHourlyRate(int hourlyRate) {
        if (hourlyRate <= 0)
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Hourly rate cannot be 0 or negative.");
        this.hourlyRate = hourlyRate;
    }


}
