14. Constructor Overloading: 

So you learned about method overloading. We can also overload constructors because technically constructors are also methods. Here's an example, when creating an employee object, let's imagine that some of our employees have a base salary and they can also do extra hours every month, but we have other types of employees that don't do extra hours. So we don't want to pass this second argument here. Now one way is to pass zero, and that is my preference, the other way is to overload this constructor.

So let me show you how to overload constructors if you really have to. Back to our employee class. Here's our first constructor, I'm going to select these few lines and press command and d to duplicate this code, alright, now we need to clean it up a little bit, alright, so we have two constructors, I'm going to change the first constructor and remove this parameter. Now here we need to pass zero as the hourly rate, and this means we have to go to the implementation of this method and change our data validation logic. We can quickly jump to this method by pressing command and b.

If this doesn't work on your machine, look under the navigate menu, here's the shortcut. Navigate to declaration is command and b on mac. On Windows it's going to be different and honestly I don't know. So, command and b, here's our data validation logic, we want to remove the equal sign, because the outer rate might be zero. Now, back to our constructors.

So here's one way to implement this constructor, the other way is to reuse the logic that we have in the second constructor. So instead of repeating these statements, we can simply call this constructor and pass the values we want. So how do we do that? We're not going to be able to call employee here, instead we need to use the this keyword. So this as I told you before is a reference to the current object.

Now, we can use parenthesis to call the second constructor. So we pass the base salary and zero as the hourly rate. This way we're reusing the logic that we have in this method, and that is better. With this change we can go back to our main class, here we can create an employee object with two arguments or another employee object with only a single argument. Now let me show you another shortcut.

Look under the view menu. Here's the shortcut for looking at the parameters of a method. Command and p. So if I press command and p here, we can see that this method is overloaded twice, in the first implementation we only have to pass the base salary, in the second implementation we have to pass the base salary and the hourly rate.

	    return baseSalary + (hourlyRate * extraHours);

      return calculateWage(0);

15. Static Members: 

The last concept we want to talk about in this section is the concept of static fields and methods. You have seen the static keyword throughout this course, but what is it all about? Well, in object oriented programming, a class can have two types of members. Instance members and static members. Instance members belong to instances or objects.

For example, in our employee class, all the members that we have declared here, like these fields, and all these methods are instance members. They belong to each instance of the employee class. So, when we create an employee object, we can access these members using the dot operator. So these are the instance members, right? In contrast we have static or class members, these are the fields and methods that belong to a class, not an object.

So here if we type the name of our employee class, and use the dot operator, we don't see anything. Because this class doesn't have any static fields or methods. Now you might ask what is the purpose of these static members? When do we use them? We use them in situations where we want to represent a concept that should be in a single place.

Like the number of employees. This concept does not belong to each individual employee. So this is where we use static members. Now let me show you how to use these static members. So back to our employee class, after we declare our instance field, we declare static fields.

So we type public static integer number of employees. Now, here in our constructor, every time we create a new employee object, we want to increment the number of employees. So we type number of employees plus plus. Now back to our main class, here we're creating an employee object, so we should have one instance in the memory. To prove that, let's print this field on the terminal.

So, print line, employee dot number of employees. So as we can see, with these static fields, we don't have to create an object, we access them directly through the class. Let's take a look. So we run this program, and currently we have one employee object in memory. So we use static fields in situations where a value is independent of objects, and we want to share it across all objects.

Now we can also create static methods. For example, back to our employee class, after our constructors, I'm going to declare a static method, so public static void print number of employees. Here we simply do a print line, and print the value of our static field. Now back to our main class, we can replace this statement by a call to employee dot print number of employees. So now our employee class has two static members.

One field, and one method. Now one thing you need to know about these static methods is that they can only see the other static methods in this class. So, here in this static method, even though we are inside the employee class, we cannot access the calculate wage method, because this method belongs to an employee object. It's an instance method. So we have to create an employee object, and then we'll be able to access this method.

That's something you need to remember. That's the reason why in the first part of this series, all the methods that I added to our main class, I declared them as static. So we could call them from the main method. Now you might be wondering why the main method is declared as static. This is to enable the java run time to directly call this method without having to create a new object.

So that's why the main method is always declared as static. Also as another example, this system class that we have here has a bunch of static members, out is one of it's static fields. Here's another example, integer dot parse int. We talked about this in the first part of the series. This is another example of a static method in Java.

As you write more Java code, you will see that many of the classes in Java library have static members. So this is all about static fields and methods.




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


Employee.java: 

package com.codewithmosh;

public class Employee {
  private int baseSalary;
  private int hourlyRate;

  public static int numberOfEmployees;

  public Employee(int baseSalary) {
    this(baseSalary, 0);
  }

  public Employee(int baseSalary, int hourlyRate) {
    setBaseSalary(baseSalary);
    setHourlyRate(hourlyRate);
    numberOfEmployees++;
  }

  public static void printNumberOfEmployees() {
    System.out.println(numberOfEmployees);
  }

  public int calculateWage(int extraHours) {
    return baseSalary + (getHourlyRate() * extraHours);
  }

  public int calculateWage() {
    return calculateWage(0);
  }

  private void setBaseSalary(int baseSalary) {
    if (baseSalary <= 0)
      throw new IllegalArgumentException("Salary cannot be 0 or less.");
    this.baseSalary = baseSalary;
  }

  private int getBaseSalary() {
    return baseSalary;
  }

  private int getHourlyRate() {
    return hourlyRate;
  }

  private void setHourlyRate(int hourlyRate) {
    if (hourlyRate < 0)
      throw new IllegalArgumentException("Hourly rate cannot be 0 or negative.");
    this.hourlyRate = hourlyRate;
  }
}

		var employee = new Emoployee(50_000, 20); 
		var employee2 = new Employee(10_000);

		System.out.println(wage);

16. Classes Quiz: 

Questions
1- What is the difference between a class and an object?
A class is a blueprint or template for creating objects. An object is an
instance of a class. 


2- What does instantiating mean?
Instantiating means creating an instance of a class: new Customer()


3- What is the difference between stack and heap memory? How are they managed?
Stack is used for storing primitive types (numbers, boolean and character) and variables that store references to objects in the heap. Variables stored in the stack are immediately cleared when they go out of scope (eg when a method finishes execution). Objects stored in the heap get removed later on when they’re no longer references. This is done by Java’s garbage collector. 


4- What are the problems of procedural code? How does object-oriented programming help solve these problems?
Big classes with several unrelated methods focusing on different concerns and responsibilities. These methods often have several parameters. You often see the same group of parameters repeated across these methods. All you see is procedures calling each other passing arguments around. 
By applying object-oriented programming techniques, we extract these repetitive parameters and declare them as fields in our classes. Our classes will then encapsulate both the data and the operations on the data (methods). As a result, our methods will have fewer parameters and our code will be cleaner and more reusable. 


5- What is encapsulation?
Encapsulation is the first principle of object-oriented programming. It suggests that we should bundle the data and operations on the data inside a single unit (class). 


6- Why should we declare fields as private?
How we store data in an object is considered an implementation detail. We may change how we store the data internally. Plus, we don’t want our objects to go into a bad state (hold bad data). That’s why we should declare fields as private and provide getters and or setters only if required. These setters can ensure our objects don’t go into a bad state by validating the values that are passed to them. 


7- What is abstraction?
Abstraction is the second principle of object-oriented programming. It suggests that we should reduce complexity by hiding the unnecessary implementation details. As a metaphor, think of the remote control of your TV. All the complexity inside the remote control is hidden from you. It’s abstracted away. You just work with a simple interface to control your TV. We want our objects to be like our remote controls.


8- What is coupling?
Coupling represents the level of dependency between software entities (eg classes). The more our classes are dependent on each other, the harder it is to change them. Changing one class may result in several cascading and breaking changes. 


9- How does the abstraction principle help reduce coupling? 
By hiding the implementation details, we prevent other classes from getting affected when we change these details. For example, if the logic board and transistors inside a remote control change from one model to another, we’re not affected. We still use the same interface to work with our TV. Also, reducing these details and exposing fewer methods makes our classes easier to use. For example, remote controls with fewer buttons are easier to use.


10- What are constructors?
Constructors are called when we instantiate our class. We use them to initialize our objects. Initialization means putting an object into an early or initial state (eg giving it initial values). 


11- What is method overloading?
Method overloading means declaring a method with the same name but with different signatures. The number, type and order of its parameters will be different. 


12- What are static methods? 
Static methods are accessible via classes, not objects. 







17. Summary: 

In this section you learned some essential concepts in object oriented programming. You learned how to code new classes with fields, methods, and constructors. We also talked about some important principles such as encapsulation, which means bundling the data and methods that operate on the data inside a single unit. We talked about abstraction, which means hiding the implementation detail of classes. This will result in classes that are easier to use and it will also reduce the coupling between classes.

So you can change the implementation of your classes in terms of their methods and their parameters without impacting other classes. In the next section we're gonna take all these concepts and apply them in a real project. So I will see you next.

2. The Problem: 

Here's the mortgage calculator project that we built in the first part of this series. I've included the source code for this project in a zip file that I provided early on in the course. Now if you missed the first part, don't worry, let me give you a quick overview of this project. So when we run this program, we have to answer three questions. The principle or the amount of money we want to borrow.

Here we have to enter a value between 1,000 and 1,000,000. So if we enter an invalid value like zero or negative one, we get this error message. Let's say $100,000 next we need to enter the annual interest rate, let's say 4%, and we want to pay this off over ten years. See what we get. So in this report we have two sections.

The first section is the mortgage section, so here we can see our monthly payments, and below that we have another section that is our payment schedule, so every month as we contribute this amount we can see the remaining balance. So it starts from 99,000 and over ten years you can see this amount eventually gets down to zero. So this is our mortgage calculator. Now if you look at the code for this project, you can see that this code is very procedural. We have a bunch of unrelated methods, for example here we have print payment schedule which is purely responsible for printing something on the console, here we have read number which is responsible for reading a value within a given range, then we have calculate balance, we also have calculate mortgage, and all these methods are part of the main class.

So we don't have encapsulation, we don't have abstraction. We just have a bunch of methods calling each other. This is what we call procedural code. Now in this section we want to refactor this code towards an object oriented design. Refactoring as I told you in the first part of this series means changing the structure of the code without changing it's behavior.

So we don't want to change the functionality of this program, we just want to change how we structure our code. Now in this section we want to take a different approach. Instead of you watching me do this, I want you to treat this as a project, as an opportunity to practice all the concepts that you learned in the last section. So please do not continue watching until you solve this problem. This is extremely important.

You cannot learn how to ride a bicycle by watching someone, you have to do it. So spend twenty to thirty minutes, refactor this code, extract the classes that you think are necessary here, and then come back and see how I would solve the same problem. So get the source code and start refractoring this project.



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


Main.java:

package com.codewithmosh;

import java.text.NumberFormat;
import java.util.Scanner;

public class Main {
    final static byte MONTHS_IN_YEAR = 12;
    final static byte PERCENT = 100;

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int principal = (int) readNumber("Principal: ", 1000, 1_000_000);
        float annualInterest = (float) readNumber("Annual Interest Rate: ", 1, 30);
        byte years = (byte) readNumber("Period (Years): ", 1, 30);

        printMortgage(principal, annualInterest, years);
        printPaymentSchedule(principal, annualInterest, years);
    }

    private static void printMortgage(int principal, float annualInterest, byte years) {
        double mortgage = calculateMortgage(principal, annualInterest, years);
        String mortgageFormatted = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(mortgage);
        System.out.println();
        System.out.println("MORTGAGE");
        System.out.println("--------");
        System.out.println("Monthly Payments: " + mortgageFormatted);
    }

    private static void printPaymentSchedule(int principal, float annualInterest, byte years) {
        System.out.println();
        System.out.println("PAYMENT SCHEDULE");
        System.out.println("----------------");
        for (short month = 1; month <= years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR; month++) {
            double balance = calculateBalance(principal, annualInterest, years, month);
            System.out.println(NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(balance));
        }
    }

    public static double readNumber(String prompt, double min, double max) {
        Scanner scanner = new Scanner(System.in);
        double value;
        while (true) {
            System.out.print(prompt);
            value = scanner.nextFloat();
            if (value >= min && value <= max)
                break;
            System.out.println("Enter a value between " + min + " and " + max);
        }
        return value;
    }

    public static double calculateBalance(
            int principal,
            float annualInterest,
            byte years,
            short numberOfPaymentsMade
    ) {
        float monthlyInterest = annualInterest / PERCENT / MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
        float numberOfPayments = years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double balance = principal
                * (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPaymentsMade))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return balance;
    }

    public static double calculateMortgage(
            int principal,
            float annualInterest,
            byte years) {

        float monthlyInterest = annualInterest / PERCENT / MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
        float numberOfPayments = years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double mortgage = principal
                * (monthlyInterest * Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return mortgage;
    }
}













3. What Classes Do We need?

One of the common questions amongst beginners is what classes do we need in our application? Well, that depends on the concerns or responsibilities your application deals with. Let me give you a metaphor. Think of a restaurant. In a restaurant we have different people working together to provide a service.

Each person has a clear and predefined responsibility. So we have a chef, a waiter or waitress, a cashier, and so on. So each person in a restaurant is like an object. All these objects collaborate together to provide some service, right? We should design our classes or objects in a similar fashion.

So we should find the concerns or responsibilities in our application, and make sure that each class has a single responsibility. So back to our code, let's have a quick look at what we have here to figure out what responsibilities we have and how we can give these responsibilities to different classes. So in our main method we're calling the read number method, this method is responsible for reading a number from the console. So potentially we can create a console class and move this method there. Then in the future, whenever we want to build a console or command line application, we can reuse that class to read a number from the console.

Okay? What else do we have here? Here we're calling the print mortgage method, this method is calling the calculate mortgage method, see we are just calling methods one after another, this is procedural programming. We are not using objects, we don't have encapsulation, we don't have abstraction. So this print mortgage method is responsible for printing the monthly payments in a nice format, like some kind of a report.

So potentially we can create a class called mortgage report, and move this method there. What else do we have? Print, payment, schedule, very similar. All we are doing here is creating some kind of report. So this method is using the calculate balance method to get some data and then it prints it on the console.

Now you might be asking, wouldn't that be better if we move this method to our console class? No, because that console class is a generic class, it doesn't know anything about mortgage. If we move this method there, then that class is going to get a little bit bloated with something that it shouldn't be responsible for. This is like a restaurant where the chef comes and takes orders. So we want to make sure that each class or each object has a single responsibility.

Our console class is going to be generic, it only knows how to read values from the console. It doesn't know about mortgage or any other kind of domains. Next we have the read number method which we talked about, after that we have the calculate balance method, here we have some basic mathematical calculation for calculating the remaining balance after a number of payments. And finally we have calculate mortgage, very similar to the other method, now look at the parameters of these two methods. So here we have three parameters, principle, annual interest, and years.

And we have the exact same parameters in this other method. This is what we call procedural programming. We're just calling methods and passing values around. Now whenever you see you're passing the same values to multiple methods, that is a great opportunity to apply the encapsulation principle. So here we want to encapsulate these values and the methods that I play on them, like calculate balance and calculate mortgage inside a single object.

So instead of passing these values around, you want to store them in the fields of a class. And then our calculate balance method is not going to have four parameters. It's only going to have this parameter. So this was my initial analysis of various responsibilities we have here. Over the next few lectures, we're going to refactor this code step by step.

import java.util.Scanner;

public class Main {
    final static byte MONTHS_IN_YEAR = 12;
    final static byte PERCENT = 100;

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int principal = (int) readNumber("Principal: ", 1000, 1_000_000);
        float annualInterest = (float) readNumber("Annual Interest Rate: ", 1, 30);
        byte years = (byte) readNumber("Period (Years): ", 1, 30);

        printMortgage(principal, annualInterest, years);
        printPaymentSchedule(principal, annualInterest, years);
    }

    private static void printMortgage(int principal, float annualInterest, byte years) {
        double mortgage = calculateMortgage(principal, annualInterest, years);
        String mortgageFormatted = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(mortgage);
        System.out.println();
        System.out.println("MORTGAGE");
        System.out.println("--------");
        System.out.println("Monthly Payments: " + mortgageFormatted);
    }

    private static void printPaymentSchedule(int principal, float annualInterest, byte years) {
        System.out.println();
        System.out.println("PAYMENT SCHEDULE");
        System.out.println("----------------");

            double balance = calculateBalance(principal, annualInterest, years, month);
            System.out.println(NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(balance));
        }
    }

    public static double readNumber(String prompt, double min, double max) {
        Scanner scanner = new Scanner(System.in);
        double value;
        while (true) {
            System.out.print(prompt);
            value = scanner.nextFloat();
            if (value >= min && value <= max)
                break;
            System.out.println("Enter a value between " + min + " and " + max);
        }
        return value;
    }

    public static double calculateBalance(
            int principal,
            float annualInterest,
            byte years,
            short numberOfPaymentsMade
    ) {
        float monthlyInterest = annualInterest / PERCENT / MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
        float numberOfPayments = years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double balance = principal
                * (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPaymentsMade))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return balance;
    }

    public static double calculateMortgage(
            int principal,
            float annualInterest,

        float numberOfPayments = years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double mortgage = principal
                * (monthlyInterest * Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return mortgage;
    }
}









4. Extracting the Console Class: 

Alright, the first thing we want to do is to extract the read number method and put it inside a class called console. First I'm going to show you the poor way of doing this, and then I'm going to show you a better way. So here's the poor way. We go to our project, and inside this package we add a new class, we call it console, okay, now we go back to our main class, so here's where we're calling the read number, let me show you a shortcut to jump to the declaration of this method. So on the top, under the navigate menu, look at the shortcut for declaration.

On mac, that's command and b. So we press command and b, here's our read number method, so the poor way of doing this is by selecting this code, cutting it, and then going back to the console class and paste it here. Now why is this bad? You can see that here we have a few compilation errors. We haven't imported the scanner class, that's why IntelliJ doesn't recognize it, also, the code in our main class is broken.

So wherever we call the read number method, now we have to manually prefix it by console dot. So this is the poor way of refactoring this code. We'll end up with several compilation errors. Now let me rewrite these changes back, so undo, undo, okay, here's the read number method in our main class, we brought it back, I'm going to delete the console class, there you go, alright, now let me show you the proper way to refactor this method. So we put the carrot on the method name, and on the top, under refactor, look at the shortcut for refactor this.

On mac, it's control and t. So wherever we press control t, IntelliJ suggests a few refactoring techniques. Here we want to move this method to a different class, so I'm going to select move here, on the top you can see that we're moving this method from this class, where do we want to move this to? Com. Code with mosh dot console.

Now we don't have this class yet, and that's why it's displayed as red. But don't worry, IntelliJ is going to automatically create it for us. Now on the right side look at the visibility, so here we have various access modifiers, we want to make sure that this method is public, so it can be called from outside of this class. Now, let's click on refactor, it's asking that this class doesn't exist, do you want to create it? Of course.

So here's our console class, IntelliJ moved all the code here, and because we're using the scanner class, it also imported it on the top. So we don't have the compilation error that we had earlier. Also, it changed our main class, so on the top, where we call the read number method, it prefixes it with the console class. This is what we call safe refactoring. So instead of us manually moving code around, we use intelligent.

This is much faster and produces fewer errors.

        printPaymentSchedule(principal, annualInterest, years);

        System.out.println();

        System.out.println("--------");

    }

    private static void printPaymentSchedule(int principal, float annualInterest, byte years) {
        System.out.println();

        System.out.println("----------------");

            System.out.println(NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(balance));
        }
    }

    public static double calculateBalance(
            int principal,
            float annualInterest,
            byte years,
            short numberOfPaymentsMade
    ) {
        float monthlyInterest = annualInterest / PERCENT / MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
        float numberOfPayments = years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double balance = principal
                * (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPaymentsMade))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return balance;
    }

    public static double calculateMortgage(
            int principal,
            float annualInterest,
            byte years) {

        float numberOfPayments = years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

                * (monthlyInterest * Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

Console.java: 

package com.codewithmosh;

import java.util.Scanner;

public class Console {
  public static double readNumber(String prompt, double min, double max) {
	  Scanner scanner = new Scanner(System.in); 
      double value;
      while (true) {
          System.out.print(prompt);
          value = scanner.nextFloat();
          if (value >= min && value <= max)
              break;
          System.out.println("Enter a value between " + min + " and " + max);
      }
      return value;
  }
}











5. Overloading Methods: 

So here's our console class, currently we have a single method here, that is our read number method with these three parameters. Prompt which is the question that we display, as well as min and max. Now what if you want to ask a question but we don't want to enforce a valid range of values, all we want is a number. This is an opportunity for overloading this method. Now this is not part of the requirements, we don't currently need it in this application, so technically we shouldn't implement this method unless we need it.

But I thought to include it in this lecture to review the materials you learned in the last section. So, let's overload this method. On the top, we're going to create another public static method, and by the way both these methods are static because we only have a single console to work with. In console applications, we don't have multiple consoles. So it makes sense to use static here.

Now here we're going to return a double, we call it read number, we add this prompt, but we're not going to add the other two parameters. So, like this, now here once again we can use a scanner object, but we're not going to have this infinite loop as well as this data validation logic, our implementation is much simpler. So we simply return scanner dot next double. And by the way here we have a problem from the first part of the course, here we should call next double because this method returns a double. Now here we have an issue, every time we call the read number, this class creates a new instance of the scanner object.

So this is an opportunity for us to declare this object as a static field. So, over here, we're going to declare this as a private field, because it's implementation detail, we don't want other classes to use this field. It's only used internally inside this console class. So private static scanner, and we can initialize it right here. New scanner with system dot in.

And with this we can remove this line of code, as well as this line over here. Now back to our main class, when we call console dot read number, we can see this method is overloaded twice, the first implementation forces the user to enter a value within this range, whereas the second implementation doesn't care. It simply reads a number and returns it. So this console class is very handy, whenever we want to read a value from the console, we can simply use this class. We don't have to create a new instance of the scanner object.

Okay? Next we're going to work on the print mortgage method.

Console.java: 

package com.codewithmosh;

import java.util.Scanner;

public class Console {
  private static Scanner scanner = new Scanner(System.in);

  public static double readNumber(String prompt) {
      return scanner.nextDouble();
  }

  public static double readNumber(String prompt, double min, double max) {
      double value;
      while (true) {
          System.out.print(prompt);
          value = scanner.nextDouble();
          if (value >= min && value <= max)
              break;
          System.out.println("Enter a value between " + min + " and " + max);
      }
      return value;
  }
}











6. Extracting the MortgageReport Class: 


Alright, now we're going to work on these two methods, print mortgage and print payment schedule. Where should we move these methods to? Well, because both these methods are about reporting, I would prefer to create a new class called mortgage report. So, once again we're going to use the refactoring tool in IntelliJ, with a caret under method name, let's press control and t, and move this method to a new class. We're going to call that com, that code with Mosh, mortgage report, now in this list currently, only this method is selected, let's also select print payment schedule, and make sure that both these methods are going to be public.

Now let's refactor, yes, so here's our mortgage report class with two static methods, look at line seven, here we're calling the calculate mortgage method of the main class. Now technically this method shouldn't be there. It should be part of another class called mortgage calculator. So we're going to refactor that soon, but before we do so, let's have a quick look at our main class. So, IntelliJ automatically updated our main method, we don't have any compilation errors, so here we're calling the print mortgage method of the mortgage report class.

Alright, so we're done with this step, next we're going to move the remaining methods to the mortgage calculator class.



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

Main.java:

package com.codewithmosh;

import java.text.NumberFormat;
import java.util.Scanner;

public class Main {
    final static byte MONTHS_IN_YEAR = 12;
    final static byte PERCENT = 100;

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int principal = (int) Console.readNumber("Principal: ", 1000, 1_000_000);
        float annualInterest = (float) Console.readNumber("Annual Interest Rate: ", 1, 30);
        byte years = (byte) Console.readNumber("Period (Years): ", 1, 30);
		
        MortgageReport.printMortgage(principal, annualInterest, years);
        MortgageReport.printPaymentSchedule(principal, annualInterest, years);
    }

    private static void printMortgage(int principal, float annualInterest, byte years) {
        double mortgage = calculateMortgage(principal, annualInterest, years);
        String mortgageFormatted = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(mortgage);
        System.out.println();
        System.out.println("MORTGAGE");
        System.out.println("--------");
        System.out.println("Monthly Payments: " + mortgageFormatted);
    }

    private static void printPaymentSchedule(int principal, float annualInterest, byte years) {
        System.out.println();
        System.out.println("PAYMENT SCHEDULE");
        System.out.println("----------------");
        for (short month = 1; month <= years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR; month++) {
            double balance = calculateBalance(principal, annualInterest, years, month);
            System.out.println(NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(balance));
        }
    }

    public static double calculateBalance(
            int principal,
            float annualInterest,
            byte years,
            short numberOfPaymentsMade
    ) {
        float monthlyInterest = annualInterest / PERCENT / MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
        float numberOfPayments = years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double balance = principal
                * (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPaymentsMade))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return balance;
    }

    public static double calculateMortgage(
            int principal,
            float annualInterest,
            byte years) {

        float monthlyInterest = annualInterest / PERCENT / MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
        float numberOfPayments = years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double mortgage = principal
                * (monthlyInterest * Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return mortgage;
    }
}





MortgageReport.java:

public class MortgageReport {
    public static void printMortgage(int principal, float annualInterest, byte years) {
        double mortgage = Main.calculateMortgage(principal, annualInterest, years);
        String mortgageFormatted = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(mortgage);
        System.out.println();
        System.out.println("MORTGAGE");
        System.out.println("--------");
        System.out.println("Monthly Payments: " + mortgageFormatted);
    }

    public static void printPaymentSchedule(int principal, float annualInterest, byte years) {
        System.out.println();
        System.out.println("PAYMENT SCHEDULE");
        System.out.println("----------------");
        for (short month = 1; month <= years * Main.MONTHS_IN_YEAR; month++) {
            double balance = Main.calculateBalance(principal, annualInterest, years, month);
            System.out.println(NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(balance));
        }
    }
}

}

package com.codewithmosh;

public class MortgageCalculator {
    private int principal;
    private float annualInterest;
    private int years;

    public MortgageCalculator(int principal, float annualInterest, int years) {
        this.principal = principal;
        this.annualInterest = annualInterest;
        this.years = years;
    }

    public double calculateBalance(short numberOfPaymentsMade) {
        float monthlyInterest = annualInterest / Main.PERCENT / Main.MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
        float numberOfPayments = years * Main.MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double balance = principal
                * (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPaymentsMade))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return balance;
    }

    public double calculateMortgage() {

        float monthlyInterest = annualInterest / Main.PERCENT / Main.MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
        float numberOfPayments = years * Main.MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double mortgage = principal
                * (monthlyInterest * Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return mortgage;
    }
}

8. Moving Away from Static Members: 

As I told you in the last lecture, we don't want to initialize this private field inside this print mortgage method, the right place to initialize this is inside the constructor. However, we use constructors to initialize instance fields. But this calculator object is declared as a static field. And also, both these methods here are declared as static. So we want to move away from all these static members and have a proper object that has some state and some behavior around that state.

As I will show you later in the course, we shouldn't use static fields and static members that often, they're troublesome. We should reserve them only for situations where we're dealing with a concept that should be only in a single place. In this case, we don't want this mortgage report class to have a single mortgage calculator, perhaps in a real application we want to have multiple windows, each window displays a report, and each report can use a different calculator object with different values. So we don't want to have a single instance of this calculator object in memory. So, let's convert this to an instance member.

Once again, we press control t, unfortunately at this time, we don't see the option for converting this field to an instance field, so we'll have to do it manually. Let's remove the static keyword, obviously now we have a breaking change, because this static method cannot see our calculator object. So, let's put the carrot on the method name, press control t, and convert this method to an instance method. Now we want to make sure that this method is going to be public, let's refactor it, beautiful, so here is where we're initializing our calculator field, let's delete this line, so in this method we're simply calling calculator that calculate mortgage. Now, note that these parameters are grayed out, we no longer need them, because we encapsulated these values inside our calculator object.

So there is no need to pass these parameters around. We haven't used them anywhere in this code. So let's change the signature of this method. We press control and t, change signature, and remove these parameters. Refactor, done.

Now we need to make the same changes to the other method. So, first we need to make it an instance method so we can access this private field, convert to instance method, we want to make sure it's public, refactor, good, now, these two parameters are grayed out, we haven't used them anywhere in this method, but this method needs the years parameter. Because it's used here, on line 13. Now we can read this from our calculator object, so again, there is no need to pass these parameters around. So I'm going to change the signature of this method, and remove all these parameters.

Refactor, continue, good. Now here we have a compilation error, because we don't know how to get the number of years. So let's prefix this with calculator dot, now this is a private field in this object, we don't want to access it directly, so we should create a getter for it. Let's call that get years, now this getter doesn't exist yet, but we can easily create it. Look at this light bulb.

We can activate this using alt and enter, here we have a few options, create method, create property which means it's going to create a getter and a setter, and create read only property. This will only create a getter. Let's go with this, now we are inside our mortgage calculator class, IntelliJ is confirming the return type of this method, let's go with short, and here we have this return statement to return the value of the years field. MortgageReport.java:

package com.codewithmosh;

import java.text.NumberFormat;

public class MortgageReport {

    private MortgageCalculator calculator;

    public MortgageReport(MortgageCalculator calculator) {
        this.calculator = calculator;
    }

    public void printMortgage() {
        double mortgage = calculator.calculateMortgage();
        String mortgageFormatted = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(mortgage);
        System.out.println();
        System.out.println("MORTGAGE");
        System.out.println("--------");
        System.out.println("Monthly Payments: " + mortgageFormatted);
    }

    public void printPaymentSchedule() {
        System.out.println();
        System.out.println("PAYMENT SCHEDULE");
        System.out.println("----------------");
        for (short month = 1; month <= calculator.getYears() * Main.MONTHS_IN_YEAR; month++) {
            double balance = calculator.calculateBalance(month);
            System.out.println(NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(balance));
        }
    }
}

        this.principal = principal;
        this.annualInterest = annualInterest;
        this.years = years;
    }

        float monthlyInterest = annualInterest / Main.PERCENT / Main.MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
        float numberOfPayments = years * Main.MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double balance = principal
                * (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPaymentsMade))

        float numberOfPayments = years * Main.MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

                * (monthlyInterest * Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

9. Moving Static Fields: 

Through all the refactoring we have done so far, we have improved the design of this application significantly. But we're not done yet. Look at these static fields we have declared in the main class. These fields don't really belong here. Where do you think we should move these to?

Well, let's find out who is using them. So, right click, find usages, as you can see we're using these fields four times inside our mortgage calculator class, and once in our mortgage report class. So these fields are mainly used for calculations, we should move them to our calculator class. So, let's collapse this window, control t, we're going to move this to mortgage calculator. And look at the shortcut I'm using here, I'm not typing the full name of the class, it's very tedious, I'm using a few characters in the name, like m c, this is what we call camel hops.

So So with this we can quickly look up classes in IntelliJ. Alright, let's select this class, now in this list I'm also going to select this other field, and we want to make sure that both these fields are public, so the report class can use them. Now later I will show you that we can refactor the report class even more, so it's going to be independent or decoupled from these fields, and then we can make these fields private, so they will be part of the implementation. Let's refactor this, alright, so here's our mortgage calculator, we have two public static fields on the top, below that we have three instance fields, so the data that we store in these three fields represent the state of our calculator object. Now look at this method here, our calculate balance method has a single parameter, previously we had four parameters.

Look at the other method, calculate mortgage, no parameters, because we're using the fields inside this class. This is the benefit of object oriented programming. We're encapsulating data and operations around data inside a single unit. However, here we have a tiny problem. In both these methods, we have duplicated some code.

How we calculate the monthly interest and the number of payments. Look at these two lines, we have the exact same lines in this other method. We're going to fix this issue next.

package com.codewithmosh;

public class MortgageCalculator {
    public final static byte MONTHS_IN_YEAR = 12;
    public final static byte PERCENT = 100;
    
    private int principal;
    private float annualInterest;
    private short years;

    public MortgageCalculator(int principal, float annualInterest, short years) {
        this.principal = principal;
        this.annualInterest = annualInterest;
        this.years = years;
    }

    public double calculateBalance(short numberOfPaymentsMade) {
        float monthlyInterest = annualInterest / PERCENT / MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
        float numberOfPayments = years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double balance = principal
                * (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPaymentsMade))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return balance;
    }

    public double calculateMortgage() {

        float monthlyInterest = annualInterest / PERCENT / MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
        float numberOfPayments = years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;

        double mortgage = principal
                * (monthlyInterest * Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return mortgage;
    }

    public short getYears() {
        return years;
    }
}

















10. Extracting Duplicate Logic: 

We have duplicated these expressions twice in this class. To remove the duplication we have two options. One option is to promote these local variables as private fields in this class, and initialize them in the constructor. So here to calculate the monthly interest, we need to know the annual interest as well as these two constants. So here in the constructor, we get the annual interest, and this could be an opportunity for us to set the monthly interest.

However, if we change the annual interest in the future, we have to recalculate the monthly interest. Currently we don't have a setter for setting this field, but if we implement it in the future, we'll have to repeat this logic one more time. So we have to calculate the monthly interest once in the constructor and once in the setter of the annual interest field. So this is not a good option. A better approach is to extract this expression as a private method in this class.

We call it get monthly interest. So, let's select this expression, now we activate our refactoring menu using control n t, you cannot see it properly here, so I'm going to bring this menu from the top. So refactor, we want to extract, and method. So here's the name that IntelliJ is suggesting, get monthly interest, and this method is going to be declared as private, because this is implementation detail, we don't want to expose this to the outside. So this is abstraction in action.

Now, let's refactor this, now here's what I love about intelliJ, it detects that we have repeated this expression somewhere else, so it's asking us if we want to replace this other occurrence. Let's click on all, alright, so now we have this new method, get monthly interest, in our calculate mortgage method we are calling get monthly interest to set this variable. If we change the annual interest in the future, this method will always return the updated monthly interest. Now we're going to repeat the same steps with this second expression, where we calculate the number of payments. So, let's select it, go to refactor, extract, method, this method is going to be called get number of payments, I'm happy with the name, let's refactor it, and replace both occurrences.

Beautiful. Alright, now let's review the entire code in our mortgage calculator class. So we have these static fields on the top, below these we have our instance fields, here's our constructor, here we have our calculate balance method, below that we have get monthly interest which is like a getter. Now it's a good convention to move all getters and setters to the bottom of the class. This makes it easier to read and understand the code.

So, this get method shouldn't be here, because below that we have another calculate method, so I'm going to select these few lines, now let me show you another cool shortcut. Under the code menu, look at these two items, move line down and up. Look at the shortcut, on mac it's alt, shift, and down. So, with these lines selected I'm going to press shift, alt and down, and now we can bring this code down and see how it fits. So let's scroll down, okay, that's better, so we have all our getters in a single place, get monthly interest, get number of payments, and get years.

These two methods are declared as private because they're implementation detail, however, get years is declared as public because other classes need to use it. So it's part of the public interface of this class. Now what is beautiful about this code is that here we don't have anything about presentation. This class is purely responsible for calculations, it doesn't care what we want to do with these values. Do we want to print them on the console?

Do we want to write them to a file? Do we want to show them in a desktop application with a graphical user interface? These are all presentation concerns. So in this design we have moved all the presentation concerns to this mortgage report class. This class gets a calculator, so it delegates all the responsibility around calculations to this class, and it will simply print the results on the terminal or console.

Now here we have one more refactoring opportunity. Look at this logic for displaying the payment schedule. So we have this for loop where we initialize month to one, as long as month is less than or equal to the number of years, times months in year, your incrementing month, every month we calculate the balance, and then print it. Now I would argue that this logic shouldn't be part of this class, this class should be purely responsible for presentation. It shouldn't worry about calculating the remaining balance for each month.

So we can move this logic to our calculator class, we can add a new method there called get payment schedule which will return the remaining balance for every month as an array or list. We'll do that next.





Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


MortgageCalculator.java: 

package com.codewithmosh;

public class MortgageCalculator {
    public final static byte MONTHS_IN_YEAR = 12;
    public final static byte PERCENT = 100;

    private int principal;
    private float annualInterest;
    private short years;

    public MortgageCalculator(int principal, float annualInterest, short years) {
        this.principal = principal;
        this.annualInterest = annualInterest;
        this.years = years;
    }

    public double calculateBalance(short numberOfPaymentsMade) {
        float monthlyInterest = getMonthlyInterest();
        float numberOfPayments = getNumberOfPayments();

        double balance = principal
                * (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPaymentsMade))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return balance;
    }


    public double calculateMortgage() {

        float monthlyInterest = getMonthlyInterest();
        float numberOfPayments = getNumberOfPayments();

        double mortgage = principal
                * (monthlyInterest * Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments))
                / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

        return mortgage;
    }

    private int getNumberOfPayments() {
        return years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
    }

    private float getMonthlyInterest() {
        return annualInterest / PERCENT / MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
    }

    public short getYears() {
        return years;
    }
}

Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


MortgageCalculator.java:

package com.codewithmosh;

public class MortgageCalculator {
  private final static byte MONTHS_IN_YEAR = 12;
  private final static byte PERCENT = 100;

  private int principal;
  private float annualInterest;
  private byte years;

  public MortgageCalculator(int principal, float annualInterest, byte years) {
    this.principal = principal;
    this.annualInterest = annualInterest;
    this.years = years;
  }

  public double calculateBalance(short numberOfPaymentsMade) {
      float monthlyInterest = getMonthlyInterest();
      float numberOfPayments = getNumberOfPayments();

      double balance = principal
              * (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPaymentsMade))
              / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

      return balance;
  }

  public double calculateMortgage() {
      float monthlyInterest = getMonthlyInterest();
      float numberOfPayments = getNumberOfPayments();

      double mortgage = principal
              * (monthlyInterest * Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments))
              / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

      return mortgage;
  }

  public double[] getRemainingBalances() {
    var balances = new double[getNumberOfPayments()];
    for (short month = 1; month <= balances.length; month++)
      balances[month - 1] = calculateBalance(month);

    return balances;
  }

  private float getMonthlyInterest() {
    return annualInterest / PERCENT / MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
  }

  private int getNumberOfPayments() {
    return years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
  }
}





MortgageReport.java:

package com.codewithmosh;

import java.text.NumberFormat;

public class MortgageReport {

  private final NumberFormat currency;
  private MortgageCalculator calculator;

  public MortgageReport(MortgageCalculator calculator) {
    this.calculator = calculator;
  }

  public void printPaymentSchedule() {
      System.out.println();
      System.out.println("PAYMENT SCHEDULE");
      System.out.println("----------------");
      for (double balance : calculator.getRemainingBalances())
        System.out.println(NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(balance));
  }

  public void printMortgage() {
      double mortgage = calculator.calculateMortgage();
      String mortgageFormatted = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance().format(mortgage);
      System.out.println();
      System.out.println("MORTGAGE");
      System.out.println("--------");
      System.out.println("Monthly Payments: " + mortgageFormatted);
  }
}

Beautiful.

So we have properly separated various concerns, this class is entirely responsible for presentation, we don't have any calculations here, however, I still see one more opportunity for refactoring. Look at this expression. Number format, dot get currency instance. We have duplicated that here. We need to store this currency object in a field in this class, so we can reuse it in two places.

So let's refactor this, I've selected this expression, let's go to refactor extract field. Here we can see the preview of this field, it's going to be private number format called currency instance. And here we can initialize this in the current method, which is not a good idea, we should initialize it either while declaring the field or in the constructor. I'm going to initialize it in the constructor for consistency, let's go with that, now we can tick this box to replace both occurrences, finally we need to accept or change this highlighted name, I would prefer to call it just currency, that is shorter and cleaner. So press enter, done.

Take a look. Now we have two fields in this class, intelligent declared this field as final, so once it's set in the constructor, it cannot be reset. And that's perfectly fine. We don't need to recreate a currency object inside this class. Now, look at our print methods.

Our code is cleaner and more readable.

MortgageReport.java: 

package com.codewithmosh;

import java.text.NumberFormat;

public class MortgageReport {

  private final NumberFormat currency;
  private MortgageCalculator calculator;

  public MortgageReport(MortgageCalculator calculator) {
    this.calculator = calculator;
    currency = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance();
  }

  public void printPaymentSchedule() {
      System.out.println();
      System.out.println("PAYMENT SCHEDULE");
      System.out.println("----------------");
      for (double balance : calculator.getRemainingBalances())
        System.out.println(currency.format(balance));
  }

  public void printMortgage() {
      double mortgage = calculator.calculateMortgage();
      String mortgageFormatted = currency.format(mortgage);
      System.out.println();
      System.out.println("MORTGAGE");
      System.out.println("--------");
      System.out.println("Monthly Payments: " + mortgageFormatted);
  }
}






MortgageCalculator.java:

package com.codewithmosh;

public class MortgageCalculator {
  private final static byte MONTHS_IN_YEAR = 12;
  private final static byte PERCENT = 100;

  private int principal;
  private float annualInterest;
  private byte years;

  public MortgageCalculator(int principal, float annualInterest, byte years) {
    this.principal = principal;
    this.annualInterest = annualInterest;
    this.years = years;
  }

  public double calculateBalance(short numberOfPaymentsMade) {
      float monthlyInterest = getMonthlyInterest();
      float numberOfPayments = getNumberOfPayments();

      double balance = principal
              * (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPaymentsMade))
              / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

      return balance;
  }

  public double calculateMortgage() {
      float monthlyInterest = getMonthlyInterest();
      float numberOfPayments = getNumberOfPayments();

      double mortgage = principal
              * (monthlyInterest * Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments))
              / (Math.pow(1 + monthlyInterest, numberOfPayments) - 1);

      return mortgage;
  }

  public double[] getRemainingBalances() {
    var balances = new double[getNumberOfPayments()];
    for (short month = 1; month <= balances.length; month++)
      balances[month - 1] = calculateBalance(month);

    return balances;
  }

  private float getMonthlyInterest() {
    return annualInterest / PERCENT / MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
  }

  private int getNumberOfPayments() {
    return years * MONTHS_IN_YEAR;
  }
}



Console.java:

package com.codewithmosh;

import java.util.Scanner;

public class Console {
  private static Scanner scanner = new Scanner(System.in);

  public static double readNumber(String prompt) {
      return scanner.nextDouble();
  }

  public static double readNumber(String prompt, double min, double max) {
      double value;
      while (true) {
          System.out.print(prompt);
          value = scanner.nextDouble();
          if (value >= min && value <= max)
              break;
          System.out.println("Enter a value between " + min + " and " + max);
      }
      return value;
  }
}




Main.java: 

package com.codewithmosh;

public class Main {

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int principal = (int) Console.readNumber("Principal: ", 1000, 1_000_000);
        float annualInterest = (float) Console.readNumber("Annual Interest Rate: ", 1, 30);
        byte years = (byte) Console.readNumber("Period (Years): ", 1, 30);

        var calculator = new MortgageCalculator(principal, annualInterest, years);

        var report = new MortgageReport(calculator);
        report.printMortgage();
        report.printPaymentSchedule();
    }

}























Inheritance: 

1. Introduction: 


In this section I'm gonna talk about the third principle of object oriented programming, inheritance. You're gonna learn what inheritance is, how it works, and how to use it. As part of this we'll be talking about some very important concepts such as constructors, access modifiers, overriding methods, comparing objects, and polymorphism. So let's jump in and get started.







2. Inheritance: 

Let's imagine we want to build a framework for building graphical user interfaces. So other people can use this framework to build applications with a graphical user interface. You can create forms with various types of input fields like text boxes, check boxes, drop down lists, and so on. Now, all these objects share some common behavior. For example, we can enable or disable them, we can set their size in terms of their width and height on the screen and so on.

When coding these classes, we don't want to implement all these features in every single class. That's where we use inheritance to reuse code. So we define all the common behavior in a single class, let's call it UI control, and then have other classes inherit these behaviors from this class. In this example, we refer to the UI control as the base class, super class or parent, and text box as a subclass or child. Let's see this in action.

So here I want to create a new class called UI control and define all this common behavior inside this class. So let's add a new class to this package, we call it the ui control, and note that I have capitalized the first letter of every word. Okay? A scal naming convention. Alright, what behaviors do we want to define here?

For this demo, let's add the ability to enable or disable each UI control. So here we declare a private boolean field called is enabled. Initially we can set this to true, because all UI controls should be enabled by default. We can disable some of them depending on the values in our form. Now we need two methods to enable or disable this object, so public void enable, here we simply set is enabled to true, and public void disable, we set is enabled to false.

Now we also want a getter to read the value of this field. So, public boolean is enabled. Here we want to return the value of our field. So note that both the field and the method have the same name and that's perfectly fine. Because these are different types of members.

Alright, now back to our main class, let's use our UI control object. So we create a new control object, let's disable it first, and then print control dot is enabled. Let's see what we get. We get false because we disabled this control object. Beautiful.

So we implemented this feature inside this UI control class. Now let's say we want to create a text box class, we don't want to repeat all this code, we want to reuse it, and that's where we use inheritance. So earlier in the course we created this text box class, and here we have a tiny issue, we declared this field as public because we didn't talk about the abstraction principle. So, let's change this to private, now, to have this class inherit all the features that we implemented in our UI control class, we simply type extend UI control. Now let's go back to our main class and replace UI control with text box.

See what we get. So, this control object has these two methods that we declared in our text box class, but it also has these three methods that we declared in our UI control class, so it inherited these three methods. We also have a bunch of other methods, these are inherited from another class in java, which we're going to talk about next.

    }

3. The Object Class: 

In this lecture we're going to talk about the object class in Java. So every class that we declare directly or indirectly inherits from the object class. So, earlier we declared this text box class, this class extends or inherits from the UI control, and UI control in turn extends or inherits from the object class. Now here we didn't explicitly type extends object, the Java compiler automatically adds this for us. That is the reason why every class that we declare has some additional methods.

What are these? Let's take a look. First I'm going to delete this code, now back to the main class, let's declare a variable, call it obj, and set it to a new instance of the object class. Look, this class is declared in the java dot lang package, so it's available everywhere. Now, let's look at the members of this object, so we have the get class method which returns a class object, and with this we can read metadata about an object.

For example, we can find all the fields and methods defined in that object. Next we have equals which we use for comparing objects, we have hash code which returns an integer based on the address of this object in memory. We're going to look at this in a second. We have two string which returns the string representation of an object, and a few other methods like notify, notify all, and wait which are used in concurrency. That's an advanced topic, so for now, let's not worry about it and focus on these basic methods.

So I'm going to delete this code, and create a new text box object. Let's call it box one and set it to a new instance of the text box. Now let's print box one dot hash code. We get this integer and this is calculated based on the address of this object in memory. A lot of people think this is the address of this object in memory, but that is not correct.

The address of this object goes through a special function called a hash function, and the job of this function is to get a value and map it to a numeric value which we call a hash. So this is the hash code of this object. Now why does this matter? This hash code is used in a lot of situations, one of them is comparing objects for equality. Let me show you.

So I'm going to declare a second variable, let's call it box two, and set it to box one. Now you know that both these variables are referencing the same object in memory, so they should have the same hash code. Let's take a look. I'm going to duplicate this line and also print the hash code of box two. As you can see we get the same values.

Now earlier I mentioned the equals method. So So with this method we can check objects for equality. Now if you pass box two here, this method will return true because both these variables are referencing the same object and they have the same hash code. However, if I create a new text box here, now this method will return false because these two objects are in different locations in memory, and they have different hash codes. Now, there are situations where we don't want to compare objects by their hash code, we want to compare them based on their content.

For example, imagine we have a point class, a two dimensional point has x and y coordinates. Now, if we have two point objects, and both these point objects have the exact same coordinates, technically they're equal, right? So in that case, we don't want to compare two point objects based on their hash code, we want to compare them based on their content. This This is one of those situations where we can change the implementation of the equals method, so instead of comparing objects by their hash code, we can compare them using the values stored in their fields. We'll look at that later in this section.

Now finally, let's look at the two string method, this method will return the string representation of an object which has two parts, the fully qualified name of the class, which contains the package it originates from, followed by an at sign, and finally the hash code represented as a hexadecimal. If you're not familiar with hexadecimal, it's a numbering system that goes beyond our decimal numbering system. In the decimal system we have the digits zero to nine, but in hexadecimal we also have the characters a to f. So this value you see here is the hash code of this object represented in hexadecimal. Now once again there are situations where the default implementation of the two string method is not desirable, but we can always change it.

For example, if you have a point class, we can change the two string method, so instead of returning this string, it will return the x and y coordinates. So this is all about the object class. Next we're gonna look at constructors and inheritance.

So, let's press command and n, and generate a constructor. Now by default, this field is selected and it will be added as a parameter. Now we want to do the same with our text box class.

So, over here, after our private field, we generate a constructor. This is a convention that a lot of Java developers follow. First we add our fields, then we add our constructors, and finally we add all the public methods. So let's generate a constructor without any parameters, alright, now here we want to print text box. Now back to our main class.

Here we're creating a new text box object. Let's see what happens when we run this program. So as you can see, first the constructor of our base class is called, and then the constructor of the subclass. Now what if the constructor of our base class has a parameter? So back to the UI control class, let's introduce a parameter, a boolean is enabled.

Now we can use this to initialize our field, so this that is enabled, we set it to the value of this argument. Now, back to the text box class, we have a compilation error. There is no default constructor available in UI control. So here we need to explicitly call this constructor.

Let me show you how to do that. So back in the text box, here we use the super keyword followed by parenthesis to call the constructor of the base class. Now here we need to pass a boolean value, true or false, it doesn't really matter, what matters is that this statement should be the very first statement inside this constructor. So if I move this over here, once again, we get a compilation error. So, let's move it back, alright, this is all about constructors and inheritance.

5. Access Modifiers: 

In this video, we're going to take a closer look at access modifiers in Java. So you have seen public and private, you know that public members are accessible from outside of a class, but private members are not. For example, here in our UI control class, we have declared this field as private, so we cannot access it outside of this class. So if we go to our main class and create a new UI control object, look, is enabled is not here. What we have is only the method, not the field.

Another thing you need to know about private members is that they are not inherited by subclasses. So if you go to our text box class, and here in the constructor, let's type this dot is enabled, look, the field is not here. So private members which means private fields and private methods are not inherited by subclasses and they are not accessible outside of a class. So we use them to hide the implementation detail of a class. So we can change the implementation in the future without impacting other classes.

Now we have another access modifier in Java, it's considered bad practice or a code smell, but I'm going to explain it briefly so at least you know how it works. Back to our UI control class. That access modifier is protected. Now protected is like public in package, so as long as we are inside this package code with Mosh, this field is treated like a public field. Let me show you.

So back to our main class, this class is also declared in the same package, so here we can access our protected field. It's public inside this package. However, if we go to a different package, this field is not going to be accessible. So temporarily, I'm going to put the main class inside a different package. Let's say programming with Mosh.

Now we have a couple of compilation errors, because the text box class is not declared in this package, I need to import it from the package in which it's declared. So, we put the carrot here, press alt and enter, and import this class. Similarly, we need to import the ui control class, beautiful, now look, the is enabled field is not accessible. So protected members are public in their package, but they are also accessible by child classes in different packages. For example, if I put the text box class inside a different package and have it extend the UI control, this class will still be able to access the protected members declared in this class.

So as we can see, protected is kind of confusing and a little bit too complicated. That's one of the reasons it's considered a bad practice and you should avoid it, because it makes it hard to maintain your applications. So stick to public and private, use public for anything that should be exposed outside of class, and private for hiding the implementation detail. Now, what happens if we remove this access modifier? This is the default access modifier which is called package private.

And that means this field is public anywhere in this package, but private outside of this package. Even classes in other packages will not be able to inherit this field. It's private everywhere outside of this package. So here in our main class, we cannot access this field because it's only public inside the code with Mosh package. Now let's change the package back to code with Mosh, so we no longer need these import statements, and here we can access this field because it's public inside this package.

Once again, I would say stay away from the default access modifier, it's confusing, just stick to public and private.

6. Overriding Methods: 

There are situations where we inherit a method from a base class, but we are not happy with the implementation, we want to change it. This is what we call method overriding. So we override a method declared in the base class. Now, don't confuse this with method overloading. Method overloading means declaring a method multiple times but with different signatures, different parameters. Ok, now. Here's an example of method overriding, so you know that every object has this twosring method that is inherited from the bases object class. Now if we print the value that is returned from this method. We get this the fully qualified name of the class followed by an ad sign and the hash code represented in Exxon decimal. I'm not happy with this implementation, i want to change it instead, i want to print the text inside this text box. So let's go to our text box class and override the twos string method declared in the base object class. So we can override any of the methods that we inherit, whether they're inherited from the parent class or the grandparent, and so on. So after the constructor, let's type public void to a string. Now here we add an A sign followed by override. This is what we call an annotation. An annotation is basically a label that we attach to a class member, and with this we give extra information to the Java compiler. So with this we're telling the Java compiler that we are overriding the twostre method declared in the object class, and with this the Java compiler will check the signature of this method. It will make sure that this method has the exact same signature. Now here we have a compilation error because the two string method that is declared in the Object class returns a string. But here we are using void, so that is the benefit of using this annotation. It helps the Java compiler double check our code for correctness. So let's change this to string and here we want to return the text inside this text box now back to our main class. Before calling the two string method, let's set the text for this text box to Hello World. Now we run this program. And we see the text inside this text box now one more thing before we finish this video here. We don't have to explicitly call the twosring method because the print line method automatically calls the twostring method on any objects that we pass here. So that's all about overriding methods.
