7. Upcasting and Downcasting: 

In this video we're going to talk about upcasting and downcasting. Upcasting means casting an object to one of its supertypes or parents, while downcasting means casting it to one of its subtypes. Let me show you this in action here. I'm going to declare a control object new UI Control with this initial state. Now let's declare a public static void method called Show and give it a parameter of type UI control. In this method we just want to print this object on the console, so print control. And finally let's call this method and give it a controller object. When we run our program we see what the Twos string method returns beautiful. Now what if instead of this controller object, we pass a textbox object to our Show method. So I'm going to declare a textbox new textbox and pass it here. This code is perfectly valid because this textbo object inherits all the members of the control object. So every textbo object is also a control object. That's why we say inheritance represents and is a relationship. A textbo is a control so we pass this textbox object here and this object gets automatically cast to UI control. This is what we call upcasting so this object is getting cast to its parent. We could also change the parameter type to object and once again we have upcasting because every textbo is also an object. Now let me revert this back here. Let's run the program and see what we get. This time we send an emptytry string. Why? Because earlier in our textbox class we overwrote the tworing method. So we changed the default implementation of this method. And here we're returning the text inside our text box. So back to our main class at runtime we're passing a takesbox object. So when 912 gets executed, the twostre method of the text box gets executed because at runtime we're dealing with a textbox object. Oka, now here's the interesting part, even though at runtime we're passing a textbox object here at compile time. When we are coding this method, we don't have access to any of the methods in our text box. So if we type control dot, we only see the members of the control class. Now what if you want to work with one of the methods of the textbox class? Well we need to explicitly cast this control to textbox, so we prefix it with textbox and store the result in this variable. Now we can access all the methods declared in the textbox class, so let's change the text in this object to hello, world. Now when we run our program, instead of an empty string, we see Hello, work. So let's quickly recap after this point. Because the show method accepts a control object, we can give it an instance of the UI control or any of its derivatives at compile time. However, we can only work with the members of the UI control unless we explicitly cast this control object to a different type. This is what we call downcasting. Now there is a problem in this implementation, what if we pass our controller object here? We don't have any compile time errors because this method expects a UI control, and of course we are giving it a UI control. However, when we run our program, our program is going to crash with an exception. Let's take a look. So we've got an exception. The type of this exception is class cast exception and here's the Message. Class UI control cannot be cast to textbox. Here's the reason on line 12 we're trying to cast a control to a text box. Now this doesn't make sense to Java. Here's the reason every text box is of course a control object, but not every control object is necessarily a text box. It could be a dropdown list or a checkbox. So that is the reason why we got an exception here. We gave this method a controller object which is more generic, and we are trying to cast it to a more specialized type. Java cannot do this, so how can we prevent this error from happening well before this casting? We need to make sure that the object that is passed here at runtime is an instance of the textbox class. Then we can safely cast it as textbox. So here we can type an IFT statement like this, if control is an instance of take box, so this is the instance of operator, and this expression will return true if the object that we pass through at runtime is an instance of the textbox class, so then we can safely cast it to textbox, call one of its methods and redone. So this is all about upcasting and downcasting. Next we're going to talk about comparing objects.

Main.java: 

package com.codewithmosh;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var point1 = new Point(1, 2);
        var point2 = new Point(1, 2);
        System.out.println(point1 ==  point2); // false, since point1 and point2 are reference types and they store addresses and addresses are different
		System.out.println(point1.equals(point2)); // false, because the default implementation of this method in the object class compares two objects based on their references.
    }
}





refactored: 

Point.java:

package com.codewithmosh;

import java.util.Objects;

public class Point {
    private int x;
    private int y;

    public Point(int x, int y) {
        this.x = x;
        this.y = y;
    }

    @Override
    public boolean equals(Object obj) {
        if (this == obj)
            return true;

        if (!(obj instanceof Point))
            return false;

        var other = (Point) obj;
        return other.x == x && other.y == y;
    }

    @Override
    public int hashCode() {
        return Objects.hash(x, y);
    }
}




















9. Polymorphism: 

You've learned about the three principles of object-oriented programming, encapsulation, abstraction, and inheritance. In this video, we're going to talk about the 4th principle polymorphism, so poly means many. Morph means form. Polymorphism means many forms. This is one of the features of object-oriented programming languages that allows an object to take different forms. This is extremely powerful. Let me show you so here in this project, I'm going to create a new class. Add Java class. We're going to call this checkbox. And we want to extend the UI control class here. We have a compilation error. There is no default constructor available in UI control. So earlier we added this constructor here, which means we no longer have a default constructor without any parameters. So every time we want to extend this class, we'll have to call the base constructor using the super keyword. Let's not worry about that in this video, so I'm going to comment out this constructor now back to our checkbox. The compilationer is gone beautiful. Now let's go to our main class. We're going to create an array of control objects and render each object on the console. So we're going to create a UI control array. We call it controls and initialize it like this. Here we want to add a text box followed by checkbox. That is enough for this demo. So imagine we want to render a form that has multiple control objects. We could have a few text boxes, a checkbox, a radio button, and so on. Now if you want to render different control objects in a procedural way, we'll end up writing code like this. We iterate over this array. So for var control in controls. Here we will have an IF statement like. IF control is a text box then call this method, render textbox. Otherwise if it is a checkbox then call this other method, render checkbox, and so on. So this is the procedural way of solving this problem. The problem is that we're going to end up with a big fat IF statement and every time we declare a new class that extends the UI control, we'll have to come back here and modify this IF statement. This is where we apply the polymorphism principle of object-ed programming. So we go to our UI control class and declare a generic method for rendering control objects so public void render. Now in this method we're not going to have any implementation because we don't know how to render a control. It really depends on what type of control object we're dealing with because the algorithm for rendering a text box is obviously different from the algorithm for rendering a checkbox. So we're going to leave this default implementation empty. But now we can go to each subclass and overwrite this method. So let's go to our text box. Here we have a compilation error because we're calling the base constructor and passing true. Now let's comment this out. We don't need this anymore. Instead we're going to overwrideite the render method, so generate. Here we're going to override the render method in the UI control. Now look at the beauty of this dialog box. You can see all the parents of this class and all the methods that we can override. So here we have all the methods that we have declared in UI control and below that you can see all the methods that are declared in the object class all right. Let's go ahead, so here's our render method for this demo. Let's print a message like render textbox. Now we need to override this method in our checkbox class as well. So let's bring up the generate panel and overwrite the render method. Here we want to print render checkbox, so each class has its own algorithm for rendering. Once again this is the encapsulation principle in action. So we are bundling the data and all the operations around data inside a single unit. Instead of having ten render methods like render textbox, render checkbox, render dropdown list, and so on. Every class has its own render method now back to the main class. Here we are iterating over our controls array. Now in each iteration we simply call control. Render this is polymorphism in action. So at comply time we have declared this array as an array of UI controls. But at one time we are inserting different objects inside this array. So the first element is a text box, the second element is a checkbox. Now when we iterate over this array, obviously we're dealing with different types of objects. Each object has its own render method, so this control object that we are working with is taking many different forms. Polymorphism. Let's run this program and see what happens. So as we can see each control object is getting rendered on the console.

10. Abstract Classes and Methods: 

In this video we're going to talk about abstract classes and methods. We use abstract classes in situations where we declare a class, but we don't want to be able to instantiate it. We don't want to be able to create a new instance of that class. Here's an example. In the last video we created this array of UI control objects. In this array we can have an instance of the UI control or any of its subclasses like a text box and checkbox and so on. So I can come here and create a new instance of UI control. Now there is a problem here, what is a UI control? You don't really know it's an abstract concept. How are we going to render this control? We don't know that is the reason why we left the implementation of this method empty. So the whole purpose of this class is to provide some common code for its subclasses like textsbox and Checkbox. We know what it takes box is, we know what the checkbox is, we know how to render it. But UI control we don't really know it's an abstract concept. So in situations like this we can declare this class as abstract. And we cannot instantiate this class anymore, we can only extend it. We can create new classes that derive from the UI control. Now we can take this to the next level back to the UI control. We can also declare this method as abstract, and this will force any classes that derive from the UI control. To implement this method, let me show you. So we add the abstract keyword here and then remove the body or the implementation of this method. We simply terminate this with a semicolon, so all we have here is the method declaration, but not the implementation. Now let's go to our checkbox glass, so here we have implemented the render method. Let's see what happens if we comment out this code, we get a compilation error class checkbox must either be declared abstract or implement abstract method render. So because we're extending the UI control, we should either implement the render method or declare this class as abstract as well. And then it's the responsibility of the class that extends the checkbox to implement the render method. So this is how abstract classes and methods work.

package com.codewithmosh;

public abstract class UIControl {

    private boolean isEnabled = true;

//    public UIControl(boolean isEnabled) {
//        this.isEnabled = isEnabled;
//    }

    public abstract void render();

    public void enable(){
        isEnabled = true;
    }

    public void disable(){
        isEnabled = false;
    }

    public boolean isEnabled(){
        return isEnabled;
    }
}















11. Final Classes and Methods: 

So you learn that when we declare a class as abstract, we cannot instantiate it, we can only extend it. In contrast, we have final classes. When we declare a class as final, we cannot extend it anymore. Let me show you. So I'm going to declare this checkbox class as final, and now we cannot declare a class that extends the checkbox. Let me show you. So if I declare. A public class. Let's call it my checkbox that extends our checkbox. We get a compilation. Error. Cannot in inherit from the final class checkbox. So we use final classes in situations where we are 100 percent sure about the implementation of a class and we want to prevent other classes from extending that class. Is it something that we use that often? To be honest, no, and we shouldn't really use final classes that often because it prevents us from using inheritance, polymorphism, and many other powerful object-oriented features? But there are situations where the implementation of a class is so specific, we have made so many assumptions in that implementation, especially about changing the state. In those cases, we can declare our classes as final, and this will prevent other classes from extending that class. An example of this in Java is the string class, so we cannot extend the string class because it's declared as final now. You might be wondering why because strings in Java are immutable. So once we create an initialized string, we cannot change its content. If we call any of its methods like to uppercase or to lowercase, we'll get a new string. So this is the assumption that is made in the implementation of this class. People who have implemented the string class have made sure that every method that modifies a string object will in fact return a new instance. That is the reason why they declared this class as final. So we wouldn't accidentally extend this class and breakfast assumption. So this is all about final classes in Java. We also have final methods. When we declare a method as final, we cannot override it. Let me show you. So I'm going to delete this line now back to our UI Control. We can declare this method as final and now if you go to our checkbox and try to overrate one of the methods in the base class, we cannot see the enabled method anymore because it's declared as final. So we use final methods in situations where again we have made certain assumptions, we're changing the state of the class in a particular way. We don't want the subclasses to accidentally change this behavior or wreck our assumptions. So this is all about final classes and methods.

  @Override
  public void render() {
    System.out.println("Render CheckBox");
  }

    isEnabled = true;
  }

    isEnabled = false;
  }

  public boolean isEnabled() {
    return isEnabled;
  }
}










12. Deep Inheritance Hierarchies: 

So ye have've seen the benefits of inheritance. With inheritance, we can reuse code and achieve polymorphic behavior that's great. However, as I always say too much of a good thing is a bad thing, so do not create deep inheritance hierarchies. This is one of the common mistakes that a lot of people new to inheritance make. Let's say we're building a web application where people can sign up as students or instructors. An amateur developer might come up with an inheritance hierarchy like this. So we have regular and premium instructor classes. Both these classes share some common attributes and behaviors so we can extract the common code into a base class like Instructor. Now we also have students but both students and instructors have a few things in common. For example, they both have a username and a password so we can extract their common code into a base classlike user. On the other side we have courses. These courses have a few attributes like id, name and so on. We need the same attributes in the user class so we can extract a new base class like Edith and put these attributes there. What's the problem here? Well, classes involved in inheritance hierarchies are tightly coupled to each other, so here both the user and course classes are tightly coupled to the entity. If we make any changes to the entity, we have to modify the user and course classes. For example, if we change the constructor of the EN and add or remove a parameter, we may have to modify these child classes even if you don't have any breaking changes. Just because the entity class is changed, it has to be recompiled and redeployed and that means all his children and grandchildren as well as every other class that depends on any of these classes should be recompiled and redeployed. Also in these deep Hierarchies it is likely that you introduce a new field or method in one of your base classes but that field or method doesn't make sense to be inherited by its children. So you end up polluting your hierarchy with irrelevant stuff so avoid deep Inheritance hierarchies. Inheritance is good but up to one or two levels and no more than three levels. Beyond that you're really going in the wrong direction. So here we should collapse this hierarchy. Let's say both their student and instructor classes need an attribute like last login. We can redefine this in each class. Yes, we are duplicating a bit of code. But now these two classes are decoupled and they can evolve independently without impacting each other. Also, we don't really need separate classes for regular and premium instructors. All we need is a bulloleion field like is Premium. We can simply differentiate between different types of instructors. So just because a Premium instructor is an instructor doesn't mean we should use the inheritance between them. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

(Bad Design)

Entity

User course => extends Entity

Instructor Student => extends User

RegularInstructor PremiumInstructor => extends Instructor 










13. Multiple Inheritance: 

In some programming languages like C Plus Plus and Python, a class can have multiple parents. This is what we call multiple inheritance. Designers of Java have decided not to implement this feature because it brings a number of complexities. Let's say C inherits from A and B and both these classes define a method with the same signature but with different implementations now which implementation should see inherit? It's ambiguous. We don't know. As another example, what if both A and B declare a field called name should inherit only one or two copies of the same field? Again, this is another ambiguity problem. An extended version of this diagram is what we call the diamond problem. So here B and C inherit from A and D inherits from both B and C, so we have a diamond. Now let's say A has a method like two string, and we have overridden this in both B and C. Each implementation is different for obvious reasons, which implementation of the twostring method should deinherit. We don't know now here's the thing in Java. As you know, the object class is the root of all classes. So every class that exists in Java directly or indirectly inhertz from the object class. So here A is essentially the object class in Java, b and C could be two independent classes like student and instructor. Now if you have a user who can be both a student and instructor which twostring method trade in inherit. As you can see, because the object class is the base class for all classes in Java, we would naturally face the diamond problem if Java supported multiple inheritance. That's why the creators of Java decided not to support this feature for the complex it brings. They wanted Java to be simple and robust, and to be honest with you, this VI sure is not something that you would need that often it's rarely used. I personally have never felt the need for multiple inheritance so just be aware of it. The problems it brings and the fact that Java doesn't support it. 








14. Inheritance Quiz 

Questions 
1- How can we have ClassA inherit from ClassB?  
a) class ClassA inherits ClassB  
b) class ClassA extends ClassB 
c) class ClassA : ClassB 
d) class ClassA implements ClassB 

- B 


2- What will be printed on the console and why?  
var point1 = new Point(1, 2);
var point2 = new Point(1, 2); 
System.out.println(point1.equals(point2));

False - even though point1 and point2 have the same coordinates, the default implementation of the equals() method compares objects for reference equality. These two objects are in two different locations in memory, that’s why the equals() method returns false. 


3- What does hashCode() method of the Object class return? 
The hashCode() methods returns a numeric value that is calculated based on the address of the object in memory.


4- What is a default constructor? 
A constructor without any parameters. If we don’t create it, the Java compiler will automatically add one to our classes.  


5- How can we add a constructor to the Customer class?  
a) public Customer(String name) { } 
b) public void Customer(String name) {} 
c) public Constructor(String name) {} 
d) public void Constructor(String name) {}

A - constructors don’t have a return type, not even void.


6- What is the super keyword? 
The super keyword is a reference to the base or parent class. We can use it to access the members (fields and methods) or call the constructors of the base class. In contrast, the this keyword returns a reference to the current object.  


7- What is the difference between private and protected access 
modifiers?  
Members marked with protected or private access modifiers are only accessible inside of a class. Protected members are inherited and are accessible by child (derived) classes. Private members are not.  


8- How accessible is a field or method if it’s declared without an access 
modifier? 
If we omit the access modifier, the member will have the default access modifier which makes that member public in package. In other words, that member will be public in the package but private outside of the package.  


9- What is method overriding? How is it different from method 
overloading?  
Method overriding means changing the implementation of an inherited method in a subclass. For example, we can override the equals() or hashCode() methods of the Object class. Method overloading means declaring a method with different signatures(different number, type and order of parameters). 


10- What is the benefit of applying the @Override annotation when 
overriding a method? 
It signals the Java compiler that we’re overriding a method in the base class and this helps the compiler check our code for correctness. It will ensure the signature of the method in the subclass matches the on declared in the base class. Also, if we remove this method from the base class, the compiler will let us know and we can remove the method in the subclass as well.


11- The Customer class inherits from the User class. Can we pass a 
Customer object to this method? Why?  
public void print(User user) {} 

Yes. We can pass an instance of any classes that inherit directly or indirectly from the User class. In this case, the customer object will get automatically upcast (meaning it’ll get converted to its base type - User). If we need to work with members of the customer object in this method, we need to explicitly downcast it by prefixing the object with (Customer). 


12- What is the usage of the instanceof operator? 
It tells us if an object is an instance of a class. We use it before casting an object to a different type to make sure we don’t get a casting exception. 


13- What are the four principles of object-oriented programming? 
The four principles of object-oriented programming are:  
- Encapsulation: bundling the data and operations on the data inside a single unit (class).  
- Abstraction: reducing complexity by hiding unnecessary details (metaphor: the implementation detail of a remote control is hidden from us. We only work with its public interface.)  
- Inheritance: a mechanism for reusing code.  
- Polymorphism: a mechanism that allows an object to take many forms and behave differently. This will help us build extensible applications. 


14- When do we use abstract classes? 
An abstract class is a partially-implemented (half-cooked) class. We cannot instantiate them. But we use them to share some common code across their subclasses.  


15- Can we have an abstract class without any abstract methods?  
Yes! An abstract class does not need abstract methods. But if we mark a method as abstract, we should mark the class as abstract as well. 


16- When do we use final classes? 
Final classes cannot be inherited. We use them when we’ve made certain assumptions about a class and we want to prevent other classes extending our class and break those assumptions. 


17- What is the diamond problem? 
The diamond problem happens in languages that support multiple inheritance. If two classes (B, C) derive from A and are also the parents of another class (D), we see a diamond. If the top class (A) declares a method (eg toString) and its children (B and C) override this method, it’s not clear which implementation will be inherited by D. 

 
18- Does Java support multiple inheritance? 
No.









15. Summary: 

So in this section you saw the third and 4th principles of object-iented programming. In action we talked about constructors. You learn that if we don't create a constructor, the Java compiler automatically creates a default constructor without any parameters. You learn about access modifiers, so mainly we use public and private, but we also have protected which you should avoid because it creates applications that are hard to maintain. We talked about overriding methods, which means changing the implementation of a method in a subclass. As part of this, you learned how to overwrite the equals and the hash code methods for comparing objects, and finally we talked about abstract and final classes. Abstract classes cannot be instantiated and their sole purpose is to provide some common code to their subclasses. We also have final classes which cannot be extended anymore. We use them in situations where we have made certain assumptions in the implementation of a class and we want to make sure that these assumptions don't get broken in subclasses. In the next section we're going to talk about interfaces, so I will see you next.

2. Interfaces: 

Interfaces are one of the most misunderstood features in Java, partly because a lot of people don't really know the real purpose of interfaces and also because the meaning of interfaces have changed since Java Eight. So I'm going to walk you through the original and real purpose of interfaces and then we'll look at how this has changed over the past few years. We use interfaces to build loosely coupled, extensible and testable applications. What does this mean well? Earlier in the course we talked about coupling, which is the level of dependency between two software entities like classes. So if class A uses class B, we say A is dependent or coupled to B. Now if we change B you might have to change the code in A and every other class that depends on A even if you don't have any breaking changes in the code. Just because B is changed it has to be recompiled and that means every other class that depends on B and their dependencies should also be recompiled and redeployed. Now this is not an issue in a small application with a few classes, but in a large complex application with 100 or thousands of classes we don't want one simple change to cause a lot of cascading breaking changes in several places. We don't want to change one line and then wait several seconds for 100 of classes to get recompiled. To prevent these we should try to keep the coupling or relationship, treatment or classes as loose as possible. Let me give you an analogy. Think of your car. If there is an internal problem in the engine, you only want to replace that part right. You don't want to replace the tires. That makes no sense. This is loose coupling in action. Now how can we reduce the coupling between these two classes well? Earlier we talked about the abstraction principle of object-resented programming, which says, we should hide the implementation details of our classes and only expose what is necessary. So by hiding certain members using the private keyword, class A will know less about class B and this will loosen the relationship between these two classes. But that's not enough, and that's where interfaces come to the rescue. With that interface we can completely decouple A from B, so it knows absolutely nothing about it like it doesn't even exist. If we change B. A is not going to be affected. Now what is an interface really? An interface is a type similar to a class, but it only includes method declarations, no implementation. It has no code, it only defines the capabilities that a class should have. Let me give you an analogy. Imagine you have a restaurant. You need a chef. You don't care who the chef is as long as they have certain capabilities. This is an example of loose coupling. If something happens to your chef or he or she decides not to come to work, you can replace them with someone else with the same capabilities right now. In contrast, what if the chef had to be John? Then your restaurant is tightly coupled or strongly dependent on John. If anything happens to John, you're in trouble. So back to our classes, we don't want class A to be directly dependent or coupled to class B. Because with this, if we change B. A might be a factor. So to minimize the impact of changes, we put an interface in between these two classes and decouple them. Now if we change the code in B. A will not be affected because it knows nothing about B. This is what we call programming against interfaces. So we code our classes to work with interfaces, not concrete implementations. And with this we can build applications that are loosely coupled and extensible. Tomorrow we can easily replace B with another class that implements this interface. This makes it relatively easy to extend our applications. For example, let's say we use B to perform face recognition. Tomorrow we can replace B with another class like C that does a better job at face recognition. It's faster and more accurate. This will not affect the rest of our application. We simply replace one class with another one. As long as both of these classes follow our contract or interface, everything will continue to work. As another analogy. Think of your phone. Your phone has an input for the charger. This input defines an interface. You can use any charger that follows this interface or contract. As long as the size fits, you can use it to charge your phone. If one charger is slow or becomes faulty, you can replace it with another charger. So an interface gives the idea of what should be done and classes that implement that interface determine how it's done anywhere. We need to have the flexibility to swap how something is done. We can use an interface to make our design loosely coupled and extensible. Examples are data compression, encryption, sorting, and searching to name a few. Today you may be using one searching algorithm. Tomorrow, you may want to use a different algorithm that is faster and more accurate. You define an interface that says you need to be able to search data. Then you create different classes that implement that interface or contract. Each class will use a different algorithm for searching data. As another example, let's say you're building an application for calculating taxes. Each year tax rules might change, but every year you need to be able to calculate the tax. So your interface specifies what should be done which is calculating the tax and different classes will provide different ways for calculating the tax. Each year you can solve one implementation with another, so interfaces are about watts and classes are about how. So with interfaces we can build applications that are loosely coupled and extensible. We can easily extend or replace various parts with minimal or zero impact on our application. We can also independently test these parts and make sure they're working. This is what we call unit testing which is a topic for a completely different course, so this is the real purpose of using interfaces over the next few videos we're going to see these concepts in code.

In this video I'm going to show you an example of tightly coupled code. So let's add a new class of this project. We're going to call this tax calculator. Now what pieces of data do we need to store in this class at a minus we need to know the taxable income. So let's declare a private field called Taxable income. Now we could have ten other fields, but it doesn't really matter this field is sufficient. Now let's say we want to initialize this in the constructor. So here we can generate the constructor. And initialize this field beautiful now. What methods do we need here? At a minimum, we need one method for calculating the tax. So public double calculate tax. In this method we simply return taxable income times zero 0.3 so 30 percent tax. Now let's create another class like tax report that uses our tax calculator. So over here I'm going to add a new class. Called, tax report. Now what state and behavior do we need in this class? Well here we need to work with our tax calculator so we can store it in a private fill. We also need a method for showing the tax report. So I'm going to declare a private field of type tax calculator and call it calculator. Now let's declare a constructor so public tax report and in this constructor we're going to initialize this fill. So calculator equals new tax calculator and here we pass our taxable income. Let's say 100 thousand dollars great. Now we need a method for showing the tax report. In this method we're going to use our calculator to calculate the tax and then print it on the console. So Vartan equals calculator calculate tax and then we do a print line to print this on the cons. This is an example of tightly coupled code. Why because in this class in this report class we are using our tax calculator. So we are dependent on this class. If our calculator changes, our report class might be affected. Here are a three examples, look at the constructor. Currently our constructor has only a single parameter. But if you go back to our tax calculator and add a new parameter here like double insurance, now our report class is broken. We need to come back here and pass an argument to this constructor. This is not a big deal in a small application, but what if we had ten classes that were dependent on the tax calculator? Just by introducing a new parameter, we have to go back and modify all those classes. Here's another example. Recalling the calculate tax method. If you add or remove a parameter again, a report class is going to break. Now, here's the thing. Even if you don't change the signature of this method or our constructor, our tax report is still tightly coupled to our calculator. Here's an example. I'm going to go back to the calculator class and remove this new parameter, so we're not going to make any changes to the signature of the constructor or this method. All we want to do is to change how we calculate the tax. We modified the code in this class. That means this class and all its dependencies have to be recompiled and redeployed. This is the problem with tight coupling over the next few videos. I'm going to show you how to use interfaces to decouple our tax report on our tax calculator.

All right now let's add a new interface here. So new Java class. We call this Tax Calculator and change the type to interface. So here we have public interface instead of class. In this interface we don't have state or data, so we don't have any fields. We only have method declarations that determine what needs to be done. In this case we need to be able to calculate the tag, so we need a method like this public double calculate tax without any parameters. So here we have an abstract method because we don't have curer braces, we only have a method declaration. Now look at the public keyword. It's graded out and that means it's not necessary. Here it's redundant because every method that we declare here has to be implemented by class and these methods should be public there, so they can be accessed by other classes. Because this interface determines the public interface of a class, the public contract okay, so let's get rid of this unnecessary public keyword. All right now, let's go to our main class. So we cannot instantiate this interface like a class. In other words. I cannot declare a variable like C and state it to a new instance of the tax calculator. Because this is an interface, a contract, not a concrete implementation, it doesn't have any code. So how are we going to use this? Well, let me delete this line. Ta calculator. Now if this class had a base class we would have thetens keyword, so extends let's say object. So our interface said every class that implements me should have a public method with the exact same signature. It's just one piece missing. Even though we don't have a compilation here here. As a best practice we should apply the override annotation to our interface methods. So annotations as I told you before are labels that we attach to our methods or classes. And with this we give extra information to the Java Compiler. Now you might be asking what is the benefit of doing this here? Now if in the future we decide to remove this method from our interface, so let's go back here temporarily comment this out. But a report class is still using this concrete implementation. It's still tightly coupled to this class. TaxCalculator.java:

package com.codewithmosh;

public interface TaxCalculator {
    double calculateTax();
}




TaxCalculator2018.java: 

package com.codewithmosh;

public class TaxCalculator2018 implements TaxCalculator {
    private double taxableIncome;

    public TaxCalculator2018(double taxableIncome) {
        this.taxableIncome = taxableIncome;
    }

    @Override
    public double calculateTax(){
        return taxableIncome * 0.3;
    }
}












5. Dependency Injection: 

In this video we're going to talk about a very important concept in object oriented programming called dependency injection. According to this concept, our classes should not instantiate or new up their dependencies. So here our report class is instantiating this calculator and this is something we need to change. Because the dependence injection principle says this report class should not worry about creating the calculator object. You should only use it creating a calculator object and using it are two different concerns and we need to separate them. This is what we call the separation of concerns as an analogy once again. Think of a restaurant. Each person in a restaurant has a separate role. We don't want a chef to come and take orders. We want these concerns to be separated and everyone be focused on what they are responsible for. So we want to take the responsibility of creating this dependency from our report class and give it to another class. We'll have that class give our report class a calculator object. This is what we call dependency injection so that other class will pass or inject a dependency. Now we have various ways to inject these dependencies. We can use constructor injection so we can pass a dependency via the constructor of a class. We can also pass a dependency via setter or a regular method over the next few videos. We're going to explore each of these approaches and pros and cons. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

6. Constructor Injection: 

In this video we're going to talk about dependency injection using constructors so we don't want our tax report class to be dependent on a concrete implementation. We want it to be dependent on an interface so I'm going to change the type of this field with our new interface tax calculator. We should also get rid of this new statement. We don't want our tax report to know anything about this concrete implementation like it doesn't even exist. So we're going to add a parameter here of type tax calculator and use this to set our field. So we set this the calculator to calculator. This is what we call constructor injection. So we're injecting a dependency in this case tax calculator using the constructor now who's going to inject this well, let's go back to our main class. Here we can create an instance of tax calculi class. So calculator equals New Tax Calculator 2018. So here we're dealing with a concrete implementation. Let's pass our taxable income here. Now we can create a report, object, tax report and password inject this. Calculator. Object. So here's the catch. Our tax report doesn't know anything about a concrete implementation. It's only working with an interface. This is what we call. Programming against interfaces is the responsibility of another class like our main class to pass or inject these concrete implementations. Now. Today we're using Tax Calculator 2018. Tomorrow we can create another class like Tax Calculator 2019 and pass this to our tax report. Our tax report doesn't care, it's not affected. Also, if we go back to this concrete implementation and change the code here. For example, if we change 40 percent to 30 percent, our tax report is not affected. Yes, the code in this class has to be recompied. But because the tax report is not dependent on that concrete implementation, it doesn't need to be recomlied. It doesn't need any modifications. So this is loose coupling in action. Now one thing before we finish this video back to the main class. The way we have injected this dependency here is what is called Poor man's Dependency Injection. In this simple program we have only two classes so we can easily create and inject this dependenceencies by hand. But in a large application you might have 100 of classes and these classes might have several dependencies. If you don't want to create 100 of objects in your main method and pass them to the constructor of your classes, that's where we use a dependency injection framework. So there are frameworks out there that makes it really easy to pass these dependencies to our classes. Spring is one of the popular ones now. How these frameworks work and how we can use them is beyond us Cobra Course and we'll look at that in the future. Next, we're going to look at another approach for dependency injection.

8. Method Injection: 

So setter injection is great and it allows us to change the dependency of our class at runtime, but we'll have to remember to call it. Another approach is to use method injection, so instead of using this setter, we can pass that dependency into this show method. Because this is where we're using that dependency, let me show you so back to our report class. I'm going to add a new parameter to our show method tax calculator. So now we're passing this dependency from the outside and this method simply calls the calculate tax method on this dependency. It doesn't care what implementation is behind this interface now. With this change, we don't really need the constructor or the setter, so I'm going to comment this out. Now back to our Main class. We can simplify the code here. So when creating a tax report object, we don't need to pass the calculator. We pass that to our show method like this. Also, we don't need to set the calculator again. So the second time we want to show the report, we can pass a new dependency Tax Calculator. 2019. So this is what we call method injection. We pass a dependency to the method that needs that dependency and reality most of the time use constructor injection. Because with this approach we can quickly see the dependencies of a class at a class all right one last thing before we finish this video with the changes we have made so far, we have decoupled our report class from our calculator implementations, but we still have coupling here. Our report class is coupled to our tax calculator interface, so we're coupled to this contract. But this coupling is far less than coupling to a class because here we don't have code, we don't have implementation. If we change how we calculate the tax, our report class is not going to get affected. But if we change this contract by adding a new method or changing or removing one of the existing ones, of course we have broken the contract and the classes that are dependent on this contract will be broken. So we should be careful when designing these interfaces. We want to make sure these interfaces are small and lightweight, so they're less likely to change. We don't want to create big fat interfaces with a lot of methods that we're going to end up modifying or removing. We're going to talk more about this in the next video.

9. Interface Segregation Principle: 

In this video we're going to talk about a very important principle in object- oriented programming and that is interface segregation principle this. On this principle we should segregate or divide a big fat interface into a bunch of smaller ones and with this we can reduce the impact of changes. Let me show you this in code in this project I'm going to add a new interface. Let's call it UI widget. Examples of UI widgets are text boxes, checkboxes, radio buttons and so on. So let's add this interface. Here I'm going to declare three methods. Drag, resize, and render. Let's not worry about the parameters of this method. All we need to know is that we need to have these capabilities in a UI widget. We should be able to drag it, resize it, and render it on the screen. Now add a class that uses this interface, so I'm going to add a new class called Drag. Let's imagine we use this class to drag an object on the screen. Here we're going to add a method called Drag that takes a UI widget. Now in this method we're going to call the drag method of this widget, so widget the drag and then perhaps we do some extra work. Let's just do a print line and say dragging done so. With this implementation, our drag class is coupled or dependent on the UI widget interface, so it's coupled to this contract. Now here's the problem. If we change the signature of the recipe method, let's say we add a new parameter here. Our drag class will get affected and has to be recompied. Even though it's not using this capability, it doesn't care about resizing UI widgets. Here. We're dealing with a relatively small interface, but we have mixed different concerns or capabilities now. What if we had an interface with ten methods or 20 methods? That interface could change for several reasons, and every time we change that interface or contract, all the classes that are dependent on that interface as well as their dependencies may get affected. So this is where the interface segregation principle comes to the rescue. It says that we should divide this interface into smaller ones. Each interface should be focused on a single capability. So here we're dealing with different capabilities. One is Dragon objects, the other is resizing them and finally rendering them. So here we should extract a new interface and move this drag method there. Let me show you, so we can declare any new interface. Call it dragable. Look at the naming convention. Here I'm using the E. Post. Fix like dragable or Resizable. So end this interface. We're going to have our drag method. I can move this method here very easily. Now we have a warning because we should declare each interface or each class in a separate file. Here we can press alt and Enter. And move this class to dragable the Java. Now back to our drag class we're no longer going to work with the UI Wit interface. We're going to work with the dragable interface, so all we need here is an object that can be dragged. We don't care if it's a UI widget or not. We don't care if it's resizable or not. We can also rename this parameter to dragable. Now look at the methods declared here. We only have the drag method, so this interface is very lightweight. We have only one coupling point. In contrast, if you had a big interface with ten methods, you would have ten coupling points now back to our UI widget interface. Let's say there are parts of our application where we need to work with this interface and there we need all these capabilities in one place. We need to be able to drag, resize and render an object. So here we can use inheritance between these interfaces. We can type extends dragable. Now this UI WIT interface will inherit the drag method from the dragable interface. So if you go back to the drag class and replace this with UI widget, look. Now we have drag, render and resize. So this is how we can apply the interface segregation principle. Let's do this one more time. I'm going to show you a shortcut so back to our UI widget interface. This time I want to move the resize method to a different interface like resizable. But I'm not going to type this by hand. I want to get intelligent to do it for me. So we put the character on this method. Press and T to bring up the refractoring palette. Here we're going to extract an interface. What is that interface? We can call it resizable. Over here we can select the methods that we want to move so the resize method and then refractor intelligence saying Interface resizable has been successfully created beautiful. At this stage IDEA can analyze usages of UI widget and replace them with usages of Resizable. Where possible do you want to proceed? No, we don't want to do that all right see what we have now. So back to the project we have a new interface res sizable. This interface is very lightweight and only has a single method. And by the way it doesn't mean that anytime you have an interface with three methods you should segregate it into three interfaces, each with a single method. That's not the point of this lesson. The point here is that you should segregate different capabilities, so resizing and dragging an object are two different capabilities, but we might have different ways to resize an object. For example, we might have another method that takes two parameters. Like this or we might have another method like size two and give it a UI widget so we can resize an object to match the size of another object. As you can see all these methods are around the same capability now one last thing before we finish this lesson back to our UI Wigan interface. So as we can see, this interface is extending two interfaces, dragable and resizable. So unlike classes, a Java interface can have multiple parents, but this is not a problem. This is not going to bring the problems with multiple inheritance because if these parents declare the same method with the same signature, this interface will inherit only one of them. There is no ambiguity. In contrast, a class cannot have multiple parents because it is possible that these parents declare a method with the same signature but with different implementations we talked about in the last section. So be aware that in Java a class cannot have multiple parents, but an interface can have multiple parents.

Next we're going to store this video database object to store the metadata about the video in a database. So this is one of the areas where our application might change. So instead of talking to this concrete implementation, we can talk to an interface and then supply different implementations of that interface. Another case where we may see changes is in how we store the metadata about our videos. Let's say tomorrow we're using a SQL database. Tomorrow we're going to use a non-relational database like Mongo or we might use a backend as a service provider like Firebase. So how you store the metadata about this video might change again. This is another case where instead of talking to this concrete implementation, we can talk to an interface. Finally, another area where we might expect changes is in how we communicate with our users. So today we're using an email service tomorrow, we may want to send them a text or a push notification to their mobile phone. So these are the areas where things might change now. What about the video class? Well, this video class is mostly a data container. Let's take a look. Algorithm. Compared to the other classes like our video encoder or email series, it's less likely to change once we define all the attributes that we need to know about the video were pretty much done with this class. So there is really no point to decouple the video processor from this video class using an AN interface. So here's what I want you to do, extract three interfaces and have the video processor talk to those interfaces. You'll see my solution next.

11. Solution: 

Alright let's start by decoupling the video processor from the video encoder. Now I'm going to show you a very cool trick. I'm not going to manually create an interface. I'm going to have intelligent J. Extract an interface from our Video Encoder. Class let me show you. We put the character on the class name right Click, go to refractor and then select extract. Now this is not visible in my recording window, so I'm going to bring it from the top re factor Extract. Interface. Here's the name that intelligence is suggesting Video Encoder, but we already have a class with the same name, so I'm going to select this option. Rename original class and use interface where possible. We're going to rename this implementation to, let's say. X Video Encoder. Let's imagine this is the name of the algorithm that we have used for encoding the videos now over here. We can select the methods that we want to include in our interface, so our class currently has only a single method. We want to declare this in our interface as a contract, so let's select this and then click refractor. See what happened. This class is renamed to xvideo Encoder. It's implementing our new interface. We can press command on B on Mac or control B on Windows to jump to the declaration of this interface. So we have a very simple lightweight interface with the encode method now back to our video processor. This class is still instantiating our video encoder. We want to apply the dependence injection principle and take this responsibility from this class. So here we can declare a private field private video encoder that is our new interface. And then we're going to initialize this using our constructor. So we're going to use constructor injection. Now let's bring up the generate palette and generate a constructor for this class. In this constructor we're going to initialize our new field. There you go. Finally, we're going to remove this line from here. So that was the first step. Now I'm going to pause the recording and use the same technique to decouple the video processor from video database and email service. So now in our video processor we have three private fields video encoder that you saw before. We also have video database as well as notification service, so I decided to call this interface notification service as opposed to email service because we're going to have different kinds of notifications. So let's take a quick look at our video database interface. We only have this method store and there is a class called SQL Video Database that implements this interface. So in this implementation, we are storing our video metadata in a SQL database. If you're not familiar with SQL databases. I have a comprehensive course on this topic that you can find on my website now back to our video processor. So here we have these private fields. We're initializing all these fields using our constructor constructor injection. Now look at the implementation of the process method. So here we are no longer creating objects and as a result our implementation is simpler and cleaner. We're simply telling the encoder Hey, encode this video. Then we tell our database hey. Store this video and finally use our notification service to notify the user who uploaded this video. Now finally, let's have a quick look at our main class. So this is where we are creating our video processor object now we need to pass these concrete implementations as dependencies, so in this application were using xvideo encoder. Tomorrow. If you come up with a new algorithm for encoding our videos, we create a new class that implements that interface and inject that new object here, our video processor is not affected at all. Similarly, we're using a SQL database for storing the metadata about our videos. And finally we're using this email service for notifying our users. Let's say we're using Gmail to send emails. If you're not happy with Gmail, we can always replace it with a different email provider or we can use a different kind of notification channel. We can send push notifications or text messages or all of these. Of course, if you want to support multiple notification channels instead of passing one object here, we need to pass a list of notification channels. Now one last thing before we finish this video back to our video processor. We have completely decoupled this class from its dependencies and this gives us an additional benefit and that is unit testing. So with these changes we can test this class in isolation without being dependent to a real database or a real video encoder or a real email service. This is what we call unit testing and that's a topic for an entire different course.

VideoProcessor.java: 
package com.mytube;

public class VideoProcessor {
    private VideoEncoder encoder;
    private VideoDatabase database;
    private NotificationService notificationService;

    public VideoProcessor(
            VideoEncoder encoder,
            VideoDatabase database,
            NotificationService notificationService) {
        this.encoder = encoder;
        this.database = database;
        this.notificationService = notificationService;
    }

    public void process(Video video) {
        encoder.encode(video);
        database.store(video);
        notificationService.notify(video.getUser());
    }
}




XVideoEncoder.java: 
package com.mytube;

public class XVideoEncoder implements VideoEncoder {
    @Override
    public void encode(Video video) {
        System.out.println("Encoding video...");
        System.out.println("Done!\n");
    }
}














12. Fields: 

So yeah, I've seen the real purpose of interfaces. They allow us to build loosely coupled, extensible and testable applications. Now, over the past few years, we have had a number of new features in Java that have changed the meaning of interfaces, and in my opinion and a lot of other people's opinions, this has been a bad direction. So over the next few videos we're going to look at these new features and I will explain why they are bad and why you should avoid them unless you really understand what you're doing and you have a valid reason for using these features. The first feature we're going to talk about is declaring fields in our interfaces. So here we can declare a field like float minimum tax instead it 200 dollars. All these fields are final, so there are constants, which means we should inialize them here and we cannot change their value in the future. Also they are static so we can use them like this. Let's go back to our calculator implementation. Here we can type Tax calculator. There you go. So we're dealing with the public static final field. We cannot change its value to something else. Now the intention behind this feature is to avoid magic numbers which are numbers that seem to pop out of nowhere. This makes our code cleaner and easier to maintain, but there are a couple of things we need to think about before using this feature. First we need to see if this value is the same across all implementations. Is it possible that the minimum tax would change from one year to another? I think it's very likely so. This constant is something we should declare in our implementations, not in our interface, because once we set it in our interface we cannot change it as you can see here now let's imagine that here we're dealing with a really constant value that will never change like the number pi or the number of months in a year. The fact that we use this constant in our implementation is an implementation detail and that should not leak to our interface. Why should other classes that use our interface know about this constant? For example. Let's go to our main class and declare a new method. Do something. Let's give it a tax calculator. So here we're talking to an interface. Why should we know that in this interface there is a public static final field called Minimum tax. This is an implementation detail. This is about how we calculate the tax. We don't want to worry about that because the house may and will change what if we change our algorithm tomorrow and we no longer need this constant, we have to go back to our interface and remove this field and that means we have broken our contract and this will impact all the classes that are dependent on this contract as well as their dependencies. So in my opinion, constants have no place in interfaces and this is one of the bad decisions that the team behind Java has made. So this is what I want you to take away. Just because Java supports various features doesn't mean you should use them. You need to understand these features and their implications. Next, we're going to look at another bad feature and Java interfaces.

13. Static Methods: 

Another bad feature introduced in Java over the recent years is the ability to define static methods in our interfaces. So here we can define a static method like this, static double. Get taxable income. We give it two parameters, income and expenses. All right and then we return income minus expenses. So what is wrong with this? Well, if you have been paying attention to everything we talked about, you should know the answer. The problem is that this interface has implementation. It has code, it has logic and this logic can change the future. Now. Here we're dealing with a very simple logic that will probably never change. But I've seen people adding several methods to an interface, each ten lines or more unbelievable. These people don't really understand what interfaces are for. Once again, interfaces are about. Was not how here we're dealing with how something is done or calculated? Now a common argument that I hear is mosh. What if we need to reuse this logic across all classes that implement this interface? Well, that's when you define an abstract class and move this logic there so it can be shared by all its children. Let me show you. So I'm going to add a new class called abstract tax calculator. We're going to make this abstract. You want to have this implement the Tax Calculator interface. And then we're going to go back to our interface and move the static method there. Now we don't want this to be AES static because this is implementation detail. Only the real concrete calculator implementations should use this method. This is where we use the protected access modifier. So we hide this method from the outside. But we'll be able to use it in classes that in inherit from this abstract tax calculator let me show you so back to this concrete implementation we have this class. Extend the abstract tax calculator. That means we don't need to explicitly type implement tax calculator because our abstract tax calculator already does this. So let's simplify this. Now in this class we can access get taxable income method so we have inherited some reusable logic and this logic is hidden from the outside. We have kept our contract simple and clean so avoid static methods in interfaces. Whenever you're defining logic you're dealing with how how don't belong to interfaces they belong to classes.

14. Private Methods:

Another awful feature introduced in Java Nine is the ability to implement private methods in an interface. When I first heard about this. I was like. Are you kidding me? This is probably the worst thing that has ever happened to Java. What are private methods? Their implementation detail why should they be allowed in interfaces? I have no idea you need to ask the team behind Java. Here is what some people say about why we need this feature. As part of writing these static methods which we shouldn't write in the first place, we might end up with some repetitive logic so we can refractor our code and extract that redundant logic into a private methodsight and interface. That is why this feature has been requested and added to interfaces. Terrible AEL said several times throughout this section, interfaces are contracts. They should not have code, no implementation, no static methods, no private methods, no field, nothing, just method, declarations, period. Again when John tells me why many these features and interfaces I know he knows nothing about interfaces and object-oriented programming in general. That's why he hates it and he loves functional programming because in functional programming he doesn't have to worry about any of these subtle yet important details. He doesn't need to hide the implementation detail. He doesn't need to understand the differences between tightly and loosely coupled code and how to use interfaces to achieve a loosely coupled design. Just to clarify, I'm not saying functional programming is useless or object oriented programming is superior. Not at all. They're both great paradigms for building software and they have their own uses. All I I'm saying is that people like John hate object- oriented programming because they didn't learn it properly. They didn't learn the essential concepts and techniques and ended up writing ugly, unmaintenable and complex code. That doesn't make object-iented programming rubbish just because you don't know how to use something properly doesn't mean it's bad. That's all I'm saying. 15. Interfaces and Abstract Classes: 

One of the popular interview questions is the difference between interfaces and abstract classes. In the old days this was very clearcut. We could say interfaces are purely contracts, they have no code or implementation and we use them to build loosely coupled, extensible and testable applications. Abstract classes, on the other hand, are partially implemented classes and we use them to share code between a few classes great. However, as you have seen with the bad features introduced in Java over the past few years, the meaning of interfaces has changed. Now a lot of people are abusing interfaces and are using them as a hack to achieve multiple inheritance. Because in Java a class can extend one class, but it can implement multiple interfaces. So a lot of people are treating these interfaces like classes. They put a lot of logic there and then have another class implement many of them. This is like inheriting a lot of code from multiple parents. As I told you in the last section. Multiple inheritance brings a lot of complexities and in my opinion it's better to avoid it. So if you can asked this question in an interview this is exactly what you need to say start with the history of interfaces and what they are used for and then briefly mention that with this new feature such as private or static methods, interfaces have lost their meaning and a lot of people are abusing them because they don't really understand what the interfaces are for now. If the interviewer happens to be one of those people, he will probably get offended and he's going to disqualify and reject you. Awesome good for you. You don't want to work with someone like that who knows very little about object- oriented programming and is so arrogant about it. A good interview is one who would ask you questions like they might ask, why do you think these features are bad? That's where you explain the fact that people are using this as a hack to achieve multiple inheritance. But interfaces should not be confused with classes. We should keep them as contract, so we can minimize the impact of changes so we can build loosely coupled applications. So if we change how we do something, we're not going to end up with breaking changes or a lot of recomilation in the code. That's all about interfaces and abstract classes, 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


Interfaces                                       Abstract Classes

(Contracts)                                   (partially-Completed Classes)  
To build loosely-coupled                         To Shared Code 
extensible, testable applications










16. When to Use Interfaces: 

In my courses I always emphasize the importance of problem solving. You shouldn't write code simply for the sake of writing it. Every bit of code you write, you need to understand why you're writing it and what problem you're trying to solve. I've seen a lot of people extracting an interface from every class in their project and call it the best practice because they're using interfaces as I've mentioned throughout this section, you should use interfaces in situations where you want to decouple a class from its dependencies, and this gives you a number of benefits. The first benefit is that you can easily s sol one implementation with another. We have had several examples of this earlier. For example, if you have an algorithm for encoding videos, you may want to replace that algorithm with a faster algorithm in the future. That's a good case for decoupling these classes, so you can supply a new implementation in the future with minimal or zero impact on your application. In other cases, when you're using third-party services, let's say to it you're using one service for sending emails. Tomorrow, you may want to switch to a different provider. Now back to our Mortgage Calculator project here, we're using this calculator class. It's very unlikely that we're going to replace this with another calculator because the calculation of mortgage and payment schedule is straightforward and doesn't change. We are not going to have a different algorithm that is faster or more accurate, so there is really no need to extract an interface from Mortgage Calculator and inject it into the mortgage Report class unless you want to do it for unit testing. We'll talk about this later. Another benefit of using interfaces is that you can easily extend your applications again with minimal impact. This is particularly useful if you're designing a framework for others to use. For example, let's say you're building a framework for building web applications. Your framework should have a templating engine. You should be able to parse some HTML templates, put some data there, and return a response to the client. Now you may supply a default templating engine your framework. But perhaps you want tot allow other people to build their own template engines. That's another case for using interfaces. So instead of programming against a concrete implementation like a concrete template engine you program against interfaces. Other people can create new template engines that implement your interface. So by using interfaces you add extensibility points in your framework. And finally, the last benefit of using interfaces is that you can test your classes in isolation. So if a class uses an email service or a storage engine, you can decouppo it from these dependencies and test that class in isolation. This is what we call unit testing that's a topic for an entirely different course. If you don't practice unit testing in your organization, then extracting these interfaces doesn't really give you any values. So once again, don't just blindly write code and follow these so-called best practices because someone told you to do so. For every piece of code you write, you need to understand the problem you're trying to solve. If you're not solving any problems, you're just wasting your time, you're not adding any values. In fact, you're increasing the costs because the more code you write, the more bugs you're going to create and the maintenance of your applications is going to be more costly. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

Bad Practice - An interface per class


benefits of using interfaces: 

Swap implementations
Extend Your Applications 
Test Your Classes in Isolation 













17. Interfaces Quiz:

1- Why do we use interfaces? 
We use interfaces to build loosely-coupled, extensible and testable applications. 

 
2- What is tightly-coupled code?
Tightly-coupled code is code that is hard to change because there is a strong dependency between the entities (eg classes) in the code. Changing one class may result in several cascading, breaking changes in the code.  


3- Is this code loosely or tightly coupled and why? 
public interface TaxCalculator 
 {}
 public class TaxCalculator2018 implements TaxCalculator 
{}
 public class TaxReport {
    private TaxCalculator calculator; 
    public TaxReport() {
        calculator = new TaxCalculator2018();
    }
 }
 
Even though the type of the calculator field in TaxReport is an interface, we’re initializing this field to an instance of TaxCalculator2018 in the constructor. So, TaxReport is tightly coupled to TaxCalculator2018, which is an implementation, not an interface. 
 
 
4- What is dependency injection?  
Dependency injection refers to passing or injecting dependencies of a class. 


5- What are the various types of dependency injection?  
We can inject dependencies via constructors, setters and regular methods.  


6- What is the Interface Segregation Principle (ISP)?
The Interface Segregation Principle (ISP) suggests that we should segregate or divide big, fat interfaces into smaller ones, each focusing on a single responsibility or capability. Smaller interfaces are less likely to change. Changes to one capability, will only affect a single interface and fewer classes that depend on that interface. 


7- Why shouldn’t we declare fields, static or private methods in 
interfaces? 
Fields, static and private methods are all about implementation. Interfaces are contracts and should not have any implementation. 


8- What are the similarities and differences between interfaces and abstract classes?  
Both are abstract concepts and we cannot instantiate them. Interfaces are contracts and should only have method declarations. Abstract classes are partially-implemented classes. We use them to share some common code across their derivates. The new features in Java allow writing code and logic in interfaces but this is a bad practice and should be avoided.  


9- Should we extract an interface from every class? Why?  
Blindly extracting interfaces doesn’t solve any problems nor is it considered a best practice. If you extract an interface from every single class, you’ll end up with an explosion of interfaces that don’t necessarily add any values. You should use interfaces in situations where you want to decouple a class from its dependencies so you can swap these dependencies. This allows building applications that are extensible and testable.

3. Types of Exceptions: 

In Java we have three types of exceptions, checked exceptions, unchecked exceptions also called runtime exceptions, and errors. A lot of people new to Java find this confusing, so let me make it super simple for you. Check the exceptions or exceptions that we developer should anticipate and handle properly. For example, let's say we want to read data from a file. What if the file doesn't exist? Maybe it got deleted just before we tried to open it for reading. Good developers always anticipate and handle these edge cases, so instead of letting the runtime terminate our program, we would better display a friendly message to the user saying. Hey, that file doesn't exist now. The good news is that the Java compiler enforces us to handle these errors. That is why they're called checked exceptions because they get checked at compile time. Let me show you. Let's create a new file reader for reading data from a file, so reader equals new file reader. Let's say File, txt. Now look at this red underline. The Java compiler knows that the constructor of the file reader will throw out exception if the file doesn't exist. So it's telling us. Hey, you haven't handled this exception file. Not found exception. This is an example of a checked exception. So checked exceptions are exceptions that we should anticipate and recover from, and that is why they're called checked exceptions because the Java compiler checks them at compile time. Now the second type of exception is called unchecked exception or runtime exception. As the name implies, these exceptions are not checked by the compiler at compile time. They occur because of programming errors, not pointer exception is an example of a runtime exception. In the last video we shouldnt have passed no to the Say Hello method. This is a programming error, so unlike checked exceptions, we don't want to anticipate and recover by displaying a friendly message like Hey. We made a mistake and tried to use an object reference with the null value doesn't make sense. Instead we should prevent exceptions like this from happening in the first place. How can we do that by good coding practices and testing a lot of testing, preferably automated testing. That's a topic for the entirely different course. Now other examples of runtime exceptions are. Arithmetic exception. This gets thrown if you try to divide a value by zero. We also have ill legal argument exception for indicating that the argument we passed to a method was not accepted again. This is a programming mistake, not a kind of error, that we should anticipate and validate. Another runtime exception you may see is the index outof- bound exception, which occurs if you try to access an element in an array or a string or list using an invalid index. For example, we have five elements in an array, but we try to access the ten th element. Another popular run-time exception is illegal state exception which gets thrown if we try to call a method but the underlying object is not in the right state, so these are runtime exceptions. The third type of exception we have in Java is an error which indicates an error external to our application. Examples are stack overflow error or out-orme error. So if the Java Virtual machine runs out of memory, there's nothing we can do about it. So just like runtime exceptions, we should let the application crash. Rather than display a friendly message to the user, we should try to identify the source of these errors. These errors can happen because of programming errors like an infinite recursion, or for reasons outside of our application like a problem in the Java Virtual Mission itself. Next we're going to look at the hierarchy of exception classes in Java,
