4. The Iterable Interface: 

The Iterable interface represents an object that is iterable. That means we can iterate or loop over it without knowing anything about its implementation detail. So we are using an array here, if we decide to replace this with an array list or a different data structure in the future, the clients of this class are not going to get affected because they are going to code against the iterable interface. Back to our remote control metaphor, think of the buttons for changing the volume. If the internal implementation of this remote control changes, you're not going to get affected. You're still going to use the same buttons. These buttons are the interfaces that you work with. So we want to implement the iterable interface in this class, and then have other classes that use our generic list program against the iterable interface. It's like saying that they use the volume control buttons on a remote control. Okay? Now, here's the documentation for the iterable interface. As you can see, this interface declares three methods, the first and last methods a default implementation, so we don't have to implement them, the only method that we have to implement is this method over here, iterator. It returns an iterator object. We're going to talk about iterators in the next video, but very briefly an iterator is an object that we use to iterate over an iterable. Now, I know the name is confusing, but don't worry, I'm going to make it super simple for you. So, back to our generic list class, here we type, implements iterable, of t. So this is also a generic interface. Now, we press alt and enter, and have IntelliJ implement the methods. So, here you can see the same three methods that I showed you, the iterator method is highlighted because it's a required method. So, let's press ok, and here you can see the iterator method. For now, don't worry about the code we're going to write in this method, we're going to come back and complete this in the next video. All I want you to pay attention to is the return type of this method. It returns an iterator object. So, back to our main method. Now that our list implements the iterable interface, we know that it has the iterator method. So we call it to get an iterator, and with this we can iterate or loop over our list without knowing anything about its internal implementation. So we can write code like this. While iterator has next call iterator.next, this will return the current object, so we store it over here, and then print it on the console. What's going on here? Well, imagine our list looks like this, so we have a, b, and c. This iterator is like a pointer that is pointing at one object. So initially it's pointing at the first object. At the beginning of our loop we ask, do you have more items? It says yes. So here we call the next method, this will return the current item, which is the a, and then it will advance the pointer to point to b. In the next iteration we ask the iterator, do you have more items? We still do, so this method will return true, then we read the current item and advance the pointer. One more time we read the c, and then the iterator ends up here, so in the next iteration what we call the has next method will get false. So this iterator simply an object that we use to iterate over a list, or more accurately an object that is iterable. Now let me show you something really interesting. Let's compile the code, then from the project window, let's select the main class and look at its bytecode representation. Take a look. So over here we have a reference to iterator that has next and iterator dot next. I'm going to delete all the code for using this iterator object, and instead use a for each loop. So, for each item in list, we're going to print each item. One more time, we're going to compile the code, and look at the bytecode representation of the main class. Take a look. We still have a reference to the iterator object. So the for each loop is actually a syntactical sugar over the iterator object. When we use the for each loop, the Java compiler will convert our code to use an iterator object. This is the reason why we can iterate over this list using a for each loop. Now, if I remove this bit of code over here, implements iterable, and go back to the main class, you can see we have a compilation error saying for each not applicable to type generic list. So, this is the benefit of implementing the iterable interface. it allows us to iterate over an object without knowing anything about its internal implementation. So, I'm going to bring this back, now if tomorrow we decide to replace this array with, let's say an array list, our for each loop is not going to get affected. Now, our implementation is not complete, we still have to implement the iterator method, and that's what we're going to do next.

GenericList.java:

package generics;

import java.util.Iterator;

public class GenericList<T> implements Iterable<T> {
    private T[] items = (T[]) new Object[10];
    private int count;

    public void add(T item) {
        items[count++] = item;
    }

    public T get(int index) {
        return items[index];
    }

    @Override
    public Iterator<T> iterator() {
        return null;
    }
}




Main.java: 

import generics.GenericList;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var list = new  GenericList<String>();
        var iterator = list.iterator();
        // [a, b, c]
        // ^
        while (iterator.hasNext()) { // checks whether there are more items
            var current = iterator.next(); //returns the current item
            System.out.println(current);
        }
    }
}



Main.java: 

import generics.GenericList;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var list = new  GenericList<String>();
        for (var item : list)
            System.out.println(item);
    }
}


















5. The Iterator Interface: 

this video we're going to complete our implementation of the iterator method. If index is less than list.count. Then, in our next method, instead of returning null, we're going to return list that items of index, and then we'll increment the index. So initially index is 0, when we call the next method, this will return list that items of 0. Now next time we call this method, index is going to be 1, so we'll return the second item in this array. And we're going to do this as long as index is less than the number of items in our list. Okay? So, pretty simple, we're done implementing our list iterator, now back to our iterator method, instead of returning null, we're going to return a new list iterator, now note that here we have another list iterator, which is an interface declared in the java.util package, we don't want this one, we want the list iterator that we declared here. Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

GenericList.java: 

package generics;

import java.util.Iterator;

public class GenericList<T> implements Iterable<T> {
    private T[] items = (T[]) new Object[10];
    private int count;

    public void add(T item) {
        items[count++] = item;
    }

    public T get(int index) {
        return items[index];
    }

    @Override
    public Iterator<T> iterator() {
        return new ListIterator(this);
    }

    private class ListIterator implements Iterator<T> {
        private GenericList<T> list;
        private int index;

        public ListIterator(GenericList<T> list) {
            this.list = list;
        }

        @Override
        public boolean hasNext() {
            return (index < list.count);
        }

        @Override
        public T next() {
            return list.items[index++];
        }
    }
}



Main.java: 

import generics.GenericList;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var list = new  GenericList<String>();
        list.add("a");
        list.add("b");
        for (var item : list)
            System.out.println(item);
    }
}


















6. The Collection Interface: 

next interface we're going to talk about is the collection interface, which represents an object that acts as a collection or a container for other objects. What are the operations that we need to work with a collection? We should be able to add an object to this collection, we should be able to remove an existing object, we should be able to check for the existence of an object and so on. These are the operations that the collection interface declares. Here on the documentation you can see that the collection interface is also a generic interface, because here we have a generic type parameter, but instead of t we have e, which is short for element, that is a common convention for declaring interfaces or classes that have a collection semantic, because a collection can have multiple elements, okay? Now, over here you can see that this interface extends the iterable interface, so every collection is also iterable, and that means we can use a for each loop to iterate over all collections in java. Now over here you can see various classes that implement this interface, as you can see, there are over 20 implementations of this interface. Most of the time, like 90% of the time, you only need to use the ArrayList class. These other implementations are for special purposes, and we're not going to cover them in this course. You can learn about them on your own, I just want to give you a strong foundation. So back to our project, I'm going to add a new package, a new package called collections, we're going to write all the code in this section, in this package. I'm going to add a new class called collections demo, in this class we're going to have a public static method called show, this is where I'm going to show you various operations in the collection interface, if you download the source code that I've included at the beginning of this course, you can see all examples in this class. But while I'm recording this video, I'm going to delete some of these examples to keep the screen clean and easy to read. back in the main class, we're going to call collections demo dot show. Now, here I'm going to declare a variable of type collection of string let's import this type, so that is an interface we call this variable collection, now here we should set this to a new instance of array list because this is one of the classes that implements this interface. Now let's add a few items to this collection, so collection.add, you can add a string, like a, then b, and then c, because this collection is iterable, we can use a for each loop here, so for item in collection, we can print each item, so we get a, b, c, we can also print the collection as a whole, so print collection, Now what if you want to add multiple items in one go, instead of calling the add method three times? Well, here we have another class called collections, that is a utility class declared in the java.util package, this class has a method called add all, the first parameter of this method is the collection that we want to modify, so we pass our collection over here, now look at the second argument, here we can see the list of parameters by pressing command and p on mac, or control and p on windows. If the shortcut doesn't work for you, you can find it under view, parameter info. So look at the second parameter, string dot dot dot. When you see dot dot dot, that means you can pass a variable number of arguments. You can pass zero or more strings. So here we can pass a, and then b, and then c. So instead of calling the add method three times, we're passing multiple arguments to this method. if we run our program, we get the same result as before. Another useful method is the size method that returns the size of a collection, so here we have three items, we also have a method for removing an object from a collection, so we can call collection.remove a, and then we can print the collection, so a is gone, we can also remove all items by calling the clear method, now the collection is empty, we can verify that by calling the is empty method, so it returns true, if you want to check for the existence of an item, you can call the contains method, so we want to see if this collection contains an a, so it contains a, and then we can print get true, there are times you want to convert a collection to a regular array, so here we call collection.2 array, as you can see, this method has 3 forms or 3 overloads, if you don't pass any arguments here, this will return an object array, so we can store it in an object array, so every item in this array is going to be an instance of the object class, that means if we get the first item and use the dot operator, we don't see any of the string methods. If that's what you need, then you need to convert this collection to a string array instead of an object array. That's very easy. We call collection.toArray, we're going to use this form, this overload that takes a t array. So, if we pass a new string array here, now how many items do we have in this collection? 1, 2, 3. So we pass a string array over here, and this will return a string array. Also, we don't really have to specify the size here, we could simply pass 0, and that is a common convention, because you don't want to worry about counting the number of items in your collection. You can simply pass an empty array, and this method would automatically create an array with enough capacity to hold all these items. Okay? Now, if we get the first item, we can access all the string methods. For example, we can convert the string to the lower case or upper case and so on. And by the way, we can use var here, just as usual. Now some people don't like var, they argue that we cannot see the type of this object, but that is not right. So if you put the caret over here, and then go to view, quick documentation, the shortcut is f1, you can easily see that this is an object array, or If you put the caret over here, and one more time, press f1, we can see that this is a string array. So I personally prefer var because it makes our code cleaner. So this is how we can use the two array methods. Now, there are times you want to compare two collections for equality. So I'm going to create another collection of type string, let's call it other, and set it to a new array list. Now what would happen if we use over here. Then the type of this variable is going to be the arrayless class, not the collection interface, so it's going to include the additional methods that are declared in the arrayless class. Now, as I explained in the second part of this series, we should program against interfaces. This will make our applications more flexible and loosely coupled. So, I'm going to revert this back to collection of string. Now, I want to add every object that we have in this collection in our second collection. Because these are two different objects in memory, look, here we have two new operations, so these are going to be different objects in memory, and this equality operator compares these objects by their references or their addresses in memory. Now if you want to check to see if two collections have the exact same content, you should use the equals method. So print collection equals other. Take a look. So we get false as a result of evaluating this expression, because these are two different objects in memory, but we get true because these two collections have the exact same content. CollectionDemo.java: 

package collections;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Collection;
import java.util.Collections;

public class CollectionDemo {
    public static void show() {
        Collection<String> collection = new ArrayList<>();
        collection.add("a");
        collection.add("b");
        collection.add("c");

        // Add multiple items in one go
        Collections.addAll(collection, "a", "b", "c");

        collection.remove("a");
        var containsA = collection.contains("a");

        collection.clear();
        var isEmpty = collection.isEmpty();

        Object[] objectArray = collection.toArray();
        String[] stringArray = collection.toArray(new String[0]);

//        var objectArray = collection.toArray();
//        var stringArray = collection.toArray(new String[0]);

        Collection<String> other = new ArrayList<>();
        other.add("a");
        other.add("b");
        other.add("c");
        System.out.println(collection == other);
        System.out.println(collection.equals(other));
    }
}
















7. The List Interface: 

list interface represents an ordered collection, also called a sequence. So with lists we can access objects by their index. In collections we don't care about the index of objects, we just care about adding or removing them from a collection. So in your applications, if you want to work with an ordered collection, if you care about the index of objects in a collection, you should use the list interface. So let's declare a variable of type list of string, we're going to import this, and set it to a new array list. Now because the list interface extends the collection interface, here we have all the methods you learned about in the last video. So we can call list.add to add a few items, a, b, and c, but we also have an overload of the add that takes an integer. So if you want to insert a value or an object at a particular index, use this overload. Let's say I want to add an exclamation mark at the beginning, so we pass 0 as the index of the first item. Now we can print the list and here's the result. We couldn't do this with the collection interface because collections don't support indexing. Now we can also add multiple items in one go, so here we call collections that add all, now look at the first parameter, it's a collection. So because every list is a collection here we can pass our list object along with a few values like a, b, and c. here we can get an object by its index using the get method, so we can get the first item and print it, and we get a. We also have a method for replacing an object at the given index, so list.set, let's set the first object to a plus, now we print the entire list, and here's the result, we can also remove an object by its index, here we call list that remove 0, again, we didn't have this method in the collection interface because collections don't support indexing. Now take a look, a is gone and we only have b and c. Another useful method is the index of method, so we can call the index of method and pass a, and this will return the index of the first occurrence of a, in this case, it's going to be 0. if you search for a value or an object that doesn't exist here, you're going to get negative 1. There you go. Another useful method is last index of, and this will return the index of the last occurrence of this object. So if you add another a here, the index of the second a is going to be 3. So, there you go. Now I'm going to remove this, another useful method is the sublist method, so we can call list.sublist, here we pass two indexes, from and to, from is inclusive but to is exclusive. So we can start from index 0 and get all the items up to index 2. And this will return the objects at index 0 and 1. Take a look. There you go. Just realize that the original list is not affected, because this method returns a new list, okay? So this is all about the list interface. Next we're going to talk about sorting data.

package collections;

import java.util.ArrayList;

        List<String> list = new ArrayList<>();
        list.add("a");
        list.add("b");
        list.add("c");

        // Add an item at a given index
        list.add(0, "!");

        // We can add multiple items in one go
        Collections.addAll(list, "a", "b", "c");

        var first = list.get(0);
        list.set(0, "!!");

        list.remove(0);

        var index = list.indexOf("a");
        var lastIndex = list.lastIndexOf("a");

        System.out.println(list.subList(0, 2));
    }
}
















8. The Comparable Interface: 

In this video I'm going to show you how to sort data using the comparable interface. Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

Customer.java: 

package collections;

public class Customer implements Comparable<Customer> {
    private String name;

    public Customer(String name) {
        this.name = name;
    }

    @Override
    public int compareTo(Customer other) {
        return name.compareTo(other.name);
    }

    @Override
    public String toString() {
        return name;
    }

}




Main.java: 

import java.util.Collections;
import java.util.List;
import collections.Customer;
import java.util.ArrayList;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Customer> customers = new ArrayList<>();
        customers.add(new Customer("b"));
        customers.add(new Customer("a"));
        customers.add(new Customer("c"));
        Collections.sort(customers);
        System.out.println(customers);

    }
}










9. The Comparator Interface: 

So you have learned how to sort objects by implementing the comparable interface. Now, this implementation is not very flexible, because it's always comparing customers by their name. What if tomorrow we introduce new fields like email, address, and so on? We're not going to be able to sort our customers by their email, or by their address. That's where the comparator interface comes to the rescue. This interface is very similar to the comparable interface, so it's a generic interface, and here we specify the type of objects that should be compared with each other. So we can create a new class called email comparator that compares two customers by their email. Let me show you. First I'm going to introduce a new field here, private string email. Now let's initialize this through the constructor. So we press alt and enter, and add a constructor parameter. Let's click on refactor, there you go. Now we can initialize the email via the constructor. of course our main method has broken, because IntelliJ didn't know what to pass for these emails. Let's quickly supply a few values like e3, e2, and e1. Now to compare customers by their email, we're going to create a class that implements the comparator interface. So, in this package I'm going to add a new class, let's call this email comparator. Here we should type implements comparator of customer. Let's import the type, and then implement this interface. Now as you can see, this interface has quite a few methods, but all of these except the compare method have a default implementation, so we don't have to worry about them. Now let's implement this method. So this method takes two customer objects and returns an integer. the logic we're going to write here is exactly like the logic we wrote when implementing the comparable interface. So if the first object is smaller than the second object, we're going to return a negative value, if it's greater, we're going to return a positive value, and if they're equal, we're going to return 0. Now, here I'm going to get the email of the first customer, now, because this is a private field, we cannot access it here, so we have to go back to our customer class and create a getter for that field. So, over here, I put the caret on the field name, and press alt and enter, let's create a getter and a setter for the email field. That's pretty easy, now we have this getter, get email, so, back to our email comparator, we get the email of the first customer, this returns a string, now, you know that strings are comparable, so here we can call the compare to method, and pass the email of the second customer, that get email. And then we return the result. As simple as that. Now, back to our main method. When calling the sort method, we can pass a comparator object. So the sort method has two overloads, one that takes a list, and the other that takes a list, along with the comparator. So, I'm going to use the second overload, and here we pass our email comparator. Now, let me run this program we're going to see the customer with the name C first, because it's email is going to come first. Then we're going to see the customer with the name A, and finally the customer with the name B. Take a look. So we got c, a, and b. So this is how we can use the comparator interface. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


EmailComparator.java:

package collections;

import java.util.Comparator;

public class EmailComparator implements Comparator<Customer> {
    @Override
    public int compare(Customer o1, Customer o2) {
        return o1.getEmail().compareTo(o2.getEmail());
    }
}





Customer.java: 

package collections;

public class Customer implements Comparable<Customer> {
    private String name;
    private String email;

    public Customer(String name, String email) {
        this.name = name;
        this.email = email;
    }

    @Override
    public int compareTo(Customer other) {
        return name.compareTo(other.name);
    }

    @Override
    public String toString() {
        return name;
    }

    public String getEmail() {
        return email;
    }

    public void setEmail(String email) {
        this.email = email;
    }
}



Main.java: 

import java.util.Collections;
import java.util.List;
import collections.Customer;
import collections.EmailComparator;

import java.util.ArrayList;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Customer> customers = new ArrayList<>();
        customers.add(new Customer("b", "e3"));
        customers.add(new Customer("a", "e2"));
        customers.add(new Customer("c", "e1"));
        Collections.sort(customers, new EmailComparator());
        System.out.println(customers);  // [c, a, b]

    }
}
















10. The Queue Interface: 

The next interface we're going to talk about is the queue interface. Similar to the list interface, it extends the collection interface, so every queue is a collection as well. We use queues in situations where we want to process jobs based on the order we receive them. For example, think of the printer at your organization. Your printer receives various jobs, puts them in a queue, and processes them one by one in the order it received them. Here on the documentation you can see that the queue interface has sub interfaces like blocking deck, blocking queue, deck, and transfer queue. Again, these are for special purposes and chances are you're not going to use them for the most of your career. And here you can see various implementations of the queue interface. The two implementations you use most of the time are array deck and priority queue. Deck is short for double ended queue. It's a special type of queue that has two ends. So items can enter the queue from either end. This is different from the queues that we have in real life. For example, when we go in a queue for a concert or a movie, we always enter the queue from the back. With double ended queues or decks, items can enter from either end. Priority queue is another common implementation, and again, it's a special type of queue. It's a queue where each item gets a priority. And this priority determines the position of this item in the queue. So items with a higher priority move to the front of the queue. A common application of this is the queue that your operating system uses to manage the processes. Some processes have a higher priority, so they get more CPU time. So let's declare a variable of type queue of string, we call it queue, and set it to a new array deck. Now, by the way, I didn't specify this generic type argument over here, because this is redundant. The java compiler can look at what we have on the left side figure out that this is an array deck of strings. This is the reason why a string is grayed out, so we can remove it. Now in this queue let's add a few items, let's imagine that c entered the queue first, followed by a, and then b. So our queue looks like this, b, a, and c. So c is at the front of the queue. Now we have another method for adding an item to a queue, and that is offer. So queue.offer, we can now offer d, so d will be at the end or the rear of the queue. Now what is the difference between add and offer? The difference depends on the implementation of the queue that we are using. In case of an array deck, there is no difference, but in some implementations, the queue might have a limited size. In those cases, if the queue gets full, the add method throws an exception, whereas the offer method returns false. So it's kind of less aggressive. now let me delete this line, so our queue looks like this, now we can get the item at the front of the queue by calling the peek method, so this will return the item at the front, we can print this, so at the front we have c, so now we should see the c, beautiful. We have another similar method that is element, the difference between peek and element is that peek returns null if the queue is empty, whereas element throws an exception, let me show you. So I'm going to comment out these few lines, now our queue is empty, So the element method throws an exception. In contrast, if we call the peek method, we'll get null. There you go. Now, let me bring this back, alright. Now, to remove the item at the front of the queue, we call the remove method, this will remove the item at the front and return it. So now, when we run this program, we get the c, and if we print the entire queue, you can see that the c is gone, and our queue only has a and b. We have a similar method called poll, the difference is that poll returns null if the queue is empty, whereas remove throws an exception. So once again, let me show you, I'm going to comment out these few lines, so poll is going to return null, there you go, whereas if we call the remove method we get an exception. There you go. So this is how we can use queues. In the next video we're going to talk about sets.

Java Queue interface -> https://docs.oracle.com/javase/8/docs/api/java/util/Queue.html


QueueDemo.java: 

package collections;

import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.Queue;

public class QueueDemo {
    public static void show() {
        Queue<String> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
        queue.add("c");
        queue.add("a");
        queue.add("b");

        var front = queue.remove();

        front = queue.element();

        System.out.println(front);
        System.out.println(queue);

        // We have alternative methods that don't
        // throw an exception:

        // offer() similar to add()
        // poll() similar to remove()
        // peek() similar to element()
    }
}















11. The Set Interface: 

let's talk about the set interface. This interface represents a collection that contains no duplicates. So it's a great way for storing a list of unique values. Let me show you. So here I'm going to declare a variable of type set of string, let's import this, and we set this to a new hash set. This is one of the implementations of the set interface that you use 90% of the time. Now we can add a few items to this set, so set.add, I'm going to add sky, is blue. Now I want to duplicate this line so we have two blues. Let's see what happens when we print the set. The duplicate is gone. But look at the order of items, we have sky, blue, is. So the set interface does not guarantee the order of items. That is one of the important characteristics of the set interface you need to understand. Do not rely on the order. A set only guarantees the uniqueness. Okay? Now let's say we have a collection of items, and we want to remove the duplicates. Using a set we can do it in one line. Let me show you. So I'm going to delete all this code. let's create a collection of string, call it collection, and set it to a new array list. Now in this collection we want to add a few items, so I'm going to use the collections class, and call the add all method, here we pass our collection along with a few strings, a, b, c, and c, so we have duplicates. Now we can remove the duplicates by only one line of code, so all we have to do is to put this collection in a set. So we declare a set of string, and set it to a new high set. Now look at the parameters of the constructor of the high set class. So this constructor is overloaded three times, look at this overload. Here we can pass a collection of unknown extends string, so once again we have a wild card here, and that means we can pass a collection of string, or any type that extends collection interface. For example, we can pass a list of string, or a queue of string, or another set. So I'm going to pass our collection over here, and now if we print the set, you can see that the duplicates are gone. So that's a very cool technique. All we have to do is to put a list or collection inside a set, and the duplicates are gone. Now, let's talk about the set operations. So I want to create a new set, set of string, let's call it set 1, here we set this to a new high set, now, this time instead of a collection I want to pass a list of string, but we don't have a list yet, one way is to create a list over here, but I'm going to show you a better way, a faster way. We have this class called arrays, this is also declared in the java.util package, this class has a static method, list now look at the parameter of this method. We have string dot, dot, dot. As I told you before, this is called for art which represents a variable number of arguments. So here we can pass 0 or more arguments. I'm going to pass three items, a, b, and c. So this method will return a list of strings initialized with these three items. Okay? Let me put this on a new line so you can see clearly. That is better. Now I'm going to create another set, but with different items. So b, c, and d. And we're going to call this set two. Now when it comes to sets, there are four operations that you need to understand. One of them is the union operation, which is the combination of two sets. So if I want to combine these two sets, I can write set one dot add all, now look at this parameter, it's a collection of string. Here we have a wild card, so we can pass a list of string, a set of string, or a queue of string. So we can add set2, and now if we print set1, you can see that it has all the items in both sets without any duplicates. So this is the union operation. Another useful operation is intersection. And that will give us the items that are common in both sets. To get the intersection we call the retain all method. So this method will retain or keep all the items in set 2 and remove everything else from set 1. Take a look. So the intersection of these two sets is b and c. Another useful operation is difference. You want to know what items we have in the first set that we do not have in the second set. To do that we call the remove all method. So we are removing everything that we have in set 2 and keeping the rest. Take a look. so the result is a. So this is how we can use that, in the next video we're going to talk about maps. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

SetDemo.java: 

package collections;

import java.util.*;

public class SetDemo {
    public static void show() {
        Collection<String> collection = new ArrayList<>();
        Collections.addAll(collection, "a", "b", "c", "c");
        Set<String> set = new HashSet<>(collection);
        System.out.println(set);


        Set<String> set1 =
                new HashSet<>(Arrays.asList("a", "b", "c"));

        Set<String> set2 =
                new HashSet<>(Arrays.asList("b", "c", "d"));

        // Union
        set1.addAll(set2);

        // Intersection
        set1.retainAll(set2);

        // Difference
        set1.removeAll(set2);
    }
}


















12. Hash Tables: 

In this video we're going to take a short break from Java and talk about one of the essential data structures in computer science, a hash table. So let's say we have a list of customers, let's import this, and we set this to a new array list. Now in this list we could have hundreds or thousands of customers that we read from a database. Now let's say we want to look for a customer with a particular email. To do that we have to write code like this. You have to use a for each loop for each customer in customers if customer.getemail equals let's say e1 then perhaps we're going to return that customer or print a message like algorithm you see here for finding an object in a list is not scalable. Because the more objects we have in this list, the longer this loop is going to take. We're going to need more comparisons. In the worst case scenario, if this object, if this customer we're looking for is at the end of the list, we have to iterate the entire list to find that customer. So in computer science, we represent the cost of this algorithm using a special notation called the big O notation. It looks like this. of n, where n is the number of items in our list. So if you have, let's say 10 items in this list, the cost of this algorithm is going to be 0 of 10, because in the worst case scenario, this customer we are looking for is at the end of the list, so we need 10 comparisons to find that customer. Now, what if our list has 1 million customers? Then the cost of this algorithm is going to be 0 of 1 million. So the cost of this algorithm increases linearly, and in direct proportion with the size of the input. That is why we represent it using O . Now, this is where hash tables come to the rescue. A hash table is a special data structure, so we can use it to store data like a bunch of customers, but the way a hash table stores data is different from how a list or an array stores data. And for this very reason, with a hash table we can quickly look up an object no matter how many objects we have stored in the hash table. So whether we have 10 customers or 1 million customers, we can find a customer using only one comparison, and we represent it using the big O of 1. Now technically there is no comparison involved when we look up an object using a hash table, but you can think of it as a small computation step. Now if you want to learn more about hash tables and how they work, take my data structures and algorithms course, I've covered this topic in so much depth there. this is one of the subjects that is taught to computer science students, so if you're a self-taught developer, if you didn't attend a college or university, I highly encourage you to take this course, because it comes up in coding interviews all the time. So this is all about hash tables, now in Java we have an interface called map, which represents a hash table. So in Java we call them maps or hash maps, in C sharp we call them dictionaries, in Python we also call them dictionaries, in javascript, we call them objects, so the objects that we create in javascript, they're actually hash tables. For example, if you create a person object like this, and give it a name, this is represented using a hash table under the hood. So different languages call it different things, but essentially it's the same thing, it's a hash table. In the next video I'm going to show you how to use the map interface in java.

package collections;

import java.util.ArrayList;

public class MapDemo {
    public static void show() {
        List<Customer> customers = new ArrayList<>();
        for (var customer : customers) {
            if (customer.getEmail() == "e1")
                System.out.println("found!");
        }
    }
}

// C#: Dictionary

13. The Map Interface: 

As I told you in the last video, in java we have an interface called map, this interface is declared in the java.util package, and it's part of the collections framework, but it's not part of this hierarchy that you saw earlier. So it's not a collection, it's not an iterable, it's something entirely different. Now as you can see over here, this interface has two generic type parameters, k and v, which are short for key and value. So with a map you're essentially mapping a key to a value. This value could be an object like a customer, and this key is something that we use to look up that object, like look up that customer. Let's say we want to look up customers by their email. So we create a map of string to customer, so our keys are going to be strings and our values are going to be customer objects. We call this map and set it to a new hash map. Again, this interface has many different implementations, but the one that you use most of the time is the hash map class. Now let me create a couple of customer objects over here, So c1, we set it to a new customer, here we should pass a name, let's say a, and then an email, let's say e1. I'm going to duplicate this line, let's create the second customer, call it b with e2 as the email. Now we want to store these customers in our hash map. So we type map.put, this method has two parameters, a key which is a string, and a value which is a customer. here I'm going to pass c1.getemail, I'm going to use the email of this customer as a key to look it up, and then I pass the customer object itself. Similarly, we add the second customer, now to get a customer from this hash map, we type map.get, we pass the key, let's say e1, and this will return a customer object. So if we print this, what do you think we're going to get? you're going to see the name of this customer, so there you go. So instead of using a loop to iterate over a list, we call the get method of the hash map to get an object. And this is extremely fast, it doesn't matter how many objects we have in this hash map, we could have 1 trillion objects. The cost of finding a customer is constant, and it would be very, very small. Now what if we look for a customer that doesn't exist in this list? Let's say e10. we get null. Here we have a similar method called get or default, so we can pass the key, and a default value, if this value we're looking for doesn't exist. So in this case, we can pass, let's say, an unknown customer. So I'm going to create an unknown customer over here, new customer, the name is unknown, and the email is nothing. So I'm telling this map, look for an object with the key e10, and if you cannot find it, return this unknown customer, take a look. Now we get unknown. Another useful method is contains key, so here we can call contains key, you want to see if you have a customer with this email, e10, obviously this returns a boolean, so let's rename this to exists, now run the program, we get false, we also have a method for replacing an object in a hash table, for example, we can call replace, and we want to replace this customer with the email e1, with another customer object, let's say we made a mistake, and this customer object, its name should be a++, with the same email, this method is going to replace the customer, so if we print our entire map, you can see that this map has two key value pairs, here's the first key value pair, and here's the second key value pair. So this is the reason this interface is called map in Java, because it maps a key to a value. In C sharp and Python it's called a dictionary, because it's kind of like a dictionary where we have words and their meanings. So each piece of data has two components, a key and a value. Now, as I told you before, this map interface is not an iterable object, so we cannot use it in a for each loop. So if you write for each item in map, look, we get a compilation error because this is not iterable. So how can we find the objects we have stored in a map? Well, here we have three methods, one of them is key set, this returns the list of keys in this map, this object is iterable, so we can iterate over it, in each iteration we get a key, so we can print it over here, there you go, these are our keys, we have a similar method called entry set, now look at the return type of this method, it returns a set of entry of string and customer. So it returns a set, and because sets are iterable, we can iterate over them using a for each loop. So in each iteration we get an entry, this entry if we print it we see something like this, key equals value, now we can also get the value by calling the get value method or the key. So if you need to access both the key and the value in each iteration, you should iterate over the entry set. And finally we have another method called values, it returns a collection of customers, so this is also iterable, let's rename this variable to customer, So in each iteration we get one customer. and b. And by the way, the order of these customers is not guaranteed exactly like our sets. So just because we added customer 1 before customer 2 doesn't mean when we print these customers, they're going to come out in the same order. So this is all about the map interface in java. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

package collections;

import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Map;

public class MapDemo {
    public static void show() {
        var c1 = new Customer("a", "e1");
        var c2 = new Customer("b", "e2");

        Map<String, Customer> map = new HashMap<>();
        map.put(c1.getEmail(), c1);
        map.put(c2.getEmail(), c2);

        var exists = map.containsKey("e1");

        var unknown = new Customer("Unknown", "");
        var customer = map.get("e1");
        customer = map.getOrDefault("e1", unknown);

        map.replace("e1", new Customer("a++", "e1"));

        for (var key : map.keySet())
            System.out.println(key);

        for (var value : map.values())
            System.out.println(value);

        for (var entry : map.entrySet())
            System.out.println(entry);
    }
}















14. Summary: 

quickly recap the key points in this section. We use the collection interface to represent a collection of objects that support add, remove, and get operations. In a collection, we cannot access elements by their index. The list interface extends the collection interface and gives us that additional functionality. The queue interface represents a queue of objects. We use queues to process jobs in the order we receive them. The set interface represents a unique list of values, and the map interface represents instead of key value pairs. In the next section, we're going to talk about lambda expressions and functional interfaces. So, I will see you in the next section.

1- Introduction

you might have heard of functional programming. It's one of the many programming paradigms or styles of programming that makes implementing certain tasks really easy and robust. Lambda expressions were introduced in Java 8 in 2014 to allow us to use functional programming techniques in Java. So in this section we're going to talk about Lambda expressions as well as functional interfaces like consumers, suppliers, functions, and predicates. These building blocks are extremely important, they're used a lot in real world applications and We're going to continue using them throughout the rest of this course. 2.  Functional Interfaces

Let's start this section by defining functional interfaces. A functional interface is an interface with only a single abstract method. So earlier we talked about the comparable interface, this interface has a single abstract method called compareTo. So this is a functional interface. We also talked about the comparator interface, that is a functional interface as well. Now in this video, I'm going to create a functional interface from scratch, we're going to use this functional interface throughout this section. So, in this package lambdas, I'm going to add a new class, let's call this printer, and change the kind to interface. So this is the interface of a printer. In one implementation we may want to print on the terminal, in another implementation we may want to talk to an actual printer. So we could have different implementations of this interface. Now what operations do we need here? We need a single method for printing a message, so void print message. Now we have an interface with a single abstract method, so this is a functional interface. Now in the second part of this series, told you that in the recent years, Java introduced the concept of default methods in interfaces. These default methods can have implementation. So we can have a default method like this. Default void print twice, which takes a message and prints it twice. So we can have code, we can have implementation in an interface. I told you that in my opinion, this is a bad approach that the java team has taken because interfaces should not have implementation. They should only be used as contracts. But that aside, what I want to point out here is that this interface has two methods, one of them is a default method with implementation, the other is an abstract method. This interface is still a functional interface because it has a single abstract method. So it doesn't matter if we have many default or even static methods in our interfaces. As long as we have a single abstract method, we refer to that interface as a functional interface. Now, let me delete Ugly default method all right, so let's go ahead and implement this interface in a class I'm gonna add a new class called console printer In this implementation, we're gonna print on the console or terminal. So let's have this class implement the printer interface Here we're gonna add a print statement and print this message pretty straightforward back to our demo class. Here I'm going to create a static method, public static, I'm using static so I can easily call it from this other static method, it doesn't have to be static. So public static void greet, this method needs a printer. So here we're going to talk to the printer interface, we don't care about the implementation, we're programming to an interface. So printer, then we call printer.print and here we can say hello world. Now, in our show method I'm going to call the greet method, here we need to pass an object that implements the printer interface, so we can use our console printer object. Now, when we run this program, we see the hello world message, so pretty straight forward. Now, sometimes we don't want to explicitly create a class to implement an interface, because this requires writing a bit of code, sometimes we may not want to reuse this class. In the next video, I'm going to show you how to use anonymous inner classes for this purpose.

3. Anonymous Inner Classes:  

in the last video we passed a new instance of our console printer class to a greek method. As I told you, sometimes we don't want to explicitly create a class to implement an interface. Perhaps we want to implement an interface only once and use it for a single purpose. We don't want to reuse that class. So that's where we use anonymous inner classes. So instead of creating a new instance of the console printer class, here we can type new printer, so we type the name of the interface, press enter. What we have here is called an anonymous inner class. It's anonymous because this class doesn't have a name. It's just an implementation. And we call it an inner class because we're using this inside a method. In this class we're implementing the print method, here we can use a print statement to print this message. Now, when we run this program, we get the exact same result. So, this is how we can use an anonymous inner class. With anonymous inner classes, we can achieve the same result by writing less code. But Java 8 introduced a better and more concise way to achieve the same result. That's called a lambda expression, and we're going to look at that next. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

LambdasDemo.java: 

package Lambdas;

public class LambdasDemo {
    public static void show() {
        greet(new Printer() {                  //anonymous inner class 
            @Override
            public void print(String message) {
                System.out.println(message);
            }
        });
    }

    public static void greet(Printer printer){
        printer.print("Hello World!");

    }
}















4. Lambda Expressions: 

So our grid method talks to the printer interface, and this is a functional interface because it has a single abstract method. Now, wouldn't it be nice if we could implement this method in a standalone function? Like a function that exists on its own without belonging to a class. That is what lambda expressions are for. So a lambda expression is like an anonymous function that we can pass around. Let me show you. So I'm going to rewrite this code using a lambda expression. First we call the greet method. Now here we want to pass an implementation of the print method as an anonymous function. So this print method has a single parameter of type string, let's add that over here, this is where we list our parameters, so string message. After parenthesis we add an arrow, this is called the lambda operator, and then we add braces to represent the body of this function. what should we do here? We should add a print statement, and print this message on the console. What we have over here is called a lambda expression. So if you have a functional interface, we can represent this functional interface using a lambda expression. That is why these interfaces are called functional, because they represent a function. So, here we have a lambda expression, as you can see, this code is very clean and concise, but we can make it better. First of all, we can remove the type of this parameter. Now, the java compiler knows that this parameter is a string object, because the lambda expression that we pass over here will be checked against the signature of the print method. So the java compiler knows that this method has a single parameter and the type of this parameter is string. So we don't have to repeat that type over here. If I type message dot, now we can see all the string methods. By the same token, if I add another parameter here, the java compiler complains, because it knows that our print method has a single parameter, but here we're passing two parameters, okay? So, for the most part we can exclude the type of parameters, and let the java compiler infer them. Now if you have a single parameter, we can also remove this parenthesis. That makes our code cleaner and more concise. We use parenthesis only if we have no parameters, or if we have a method with multiple parameters. So we separate these parameters using a comma, just like how we declare the parameters of a method, okay? So here we have a single parameter, and we don't need parenthesis. Also, if the body of this function has a single line of code, we don't need these braces either. So I can clean up this code by pressing alt and enter, and selecting replace with expression lambda. There you go. So this lambda expression looks really clean and concise. Compare this with anonymous inner class, this is really ugly, so let me remove this, alright, now here we're passing a lambda expression as an argument to a method, but we can also store a lambda expression in a variable, for example, we can declare a variable of type printer, and set it to a new console printer, so this is a concrete implementation, or we can set this to a lambda expression, so here we say message goes to print message. So lambda expressions are essentially objects, but we can use them to represent anonymous functions. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

package Lambdas;

public class LambdasDemo {
    public static void show() {
        greet(message -> System.out.println(message));  //Lambda expressions 

        Printer printer = message -> System.out.println(message); //storing a lambda expression in a variable
    }

    public static void greet(Printer printer){
        printer.print("Hello World!");

    }
}






















5. Variable Capture: 

In the body of this lambda, we're using the message parameter that we have declared over here. But we can also access the local variables in the enclosing method. So I'm going to declare a variable called prefix, and set it to hyphen. Now, here we can reference prefix, and then append the message. So when we run, we get hyphen hello world. We can also access the static fields in the enclosing class. So I'm going to move this from here and declare it as a static field in this class, public static string prefix, set it to hyphen, now we can access it in our lambda expression, so when we run, we get the same result as before. We can also access the instance fields, so I'm going to make this an instance field, and also make the show method an instance method, so as you can see we can access the prefix field. Now what about this? What do you think this represents here. It represents the current instance of the lambdas demo class. So if we type dot, you can see the prefix field and the show method. So this is one of the differences between lambda expressions and anonymous inner classes. In lambda expressions, this references the enclosing object, whereas in anonymous inner classes, this references the current instance of the anonymous inner class. Another difference between these two types is that anonymous inner classes can have state, so they can have fields to store some data. In lambda expressions we cannot have fields, because this lambda expression is just representing an anonymous function. So we cannot have instance fields here, we cannot have state. We can only access the local variables declared in the enclosing object, as well as the static and instance fields in the enclosing class.

6. Method References: 

Sometimes all we do in a lambda expression is passing the parameter or parameters to an existing method. For example, over here, we're simply passing this message to the print line method of this object. In these cases, it's easier to reference this method directly. Let me show you. So, here's the syntax. We type the name of the class or the object that contains this method, then we type double colons, followed by the name of the method without parenthesis, because we don't want to call this method we just want to add a reference to it. For example, if I want to rewrite this lambda expression using a method reference, I would type greet, now what is the object that contains the print line method? It's this object, right? So we type system dot out, then we add double colons followed by the name of the method without parenthesis. So, this is a method reference, and this code is identical to what we have over here. Now with IntelliJ, we can always convert this kind of lambda expressions to a method reference very easily. So we put the caret on the body of the lambda, press alt and enter, and then replace lambda with method reference. There you go. Now, with these method references we can reference static or instance methods in a class, as well as constructors. Let me show you a few examples. So, I'm going to delete these, and add a static method in this class, public static void print message, and the signature of this method matches the print method of our printer interface. So, here we call the greet method, we should pass a printer object, so here we can use a lambda expression message, we take it and pass it to this method. Right? Again, all we're doing here is passing the parameter to an existing method. So we can use a method reference here. we type the name of the class that contains this method, in this case, lambda's demo. So, lambda's demo, then double colons, followed by the name of the method. There you go. Or, you can convert this using IntelliJ. Now, what if this was an instance method? Let's see how it works. So, I'm going to create a lambda's demo object, demo, we set it to a new instance of the lambdas demo class, now we want to call the greet method and pass a lambda expression like this, message goes to demo dot, here we want to call this instance method, so print message. Now, to use a method reference we type the name of the object that contains this method, demo double colons, and print. If we use intelligent we get the exact same result. Now what about passing a value to a constructor? Let's take a look. So delete, I'm going to add a constructor here, in this constructor we want to add a string parameter, now, let's say we call the greet method, and pass a message to the constructor of the lambdas demo class. How can we use a method reference here? Well, we type the name of the class, that is lambdas demo, double colon, and here we type new to represent the constructor. If we use intelligent, we get the exact same result. So with method references, we can write compact and easier to read lambda expressions. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

    }

7. Built-in Functional Interfaces: 

Java provides many predefined functional interfaces that we can use to perform common tasks. These interfaces are declared in this package, java.util.function. So, as you can see, we have quite a few interfaces here that we can use to perform common tasks. Now the list may appear overwhelming, but trust me, this is actually easier than you think. You basically have four types of functional interfaces in Java. We have consumers, suppliers, functions, and predicates. All the interfaces you saw fall into these four categories. First I'm going to give you a very brief explanation of each of these types, and then over the next few videos, we're going to study each of these in detail. So the consumer interface represents an operation that takes a single argument and returns no result. That is why it's called consumer, because it consumes a value. For example, this printer interface that we declared, this is very similar to the consumer interface in Java. Because this print method simply consumes this message. It takes a single argument and doesn't return a value. The supplier interface is the opposite of the consumer interface. It represents an operation that takes no input and returns a value. So it supplies a value. The function interface represents a function that can map a value to a different value. And the predicate interface represents an operation that takes an object and checks to see if that object satisfies some criteria. We use this in filtering data. For example, with the predicate interface, can get the customers who don't have a phone number, or courses that are older than 2 years. Next we're going to look at the consumer interface.
