8. The Consumer Interface: 

consumer interface represents an operation that takes a single argument and doesn't return a value. Now, why does this matter? Why do you have to learn about the consumer interface? Well, a lot of methods in Java expect an object that implements the consumer interface. I will show you an example of this later in this video. But first, let's look at the methods of this interface. So, as you can see, here we have a single abstract method called accept that takes an argument of type T and and here t is our generic type parameter, and this method doesn't return a value. We also have a default method, as I told you before, these default methods have an implementation. I'm going to talk about this method in the next video. So the consumer interface represents an operation that takes a single argument and doesn't return a value. Now we have a few variations of this interface. One of them is by consumer that takes two arguments. So if you look at the accept method, you can see here we have two arguments, t and u, we also have int consumer, this is one of the primitive specializations of this interface, so the accept method takes a primitive integer, so if you're dealing with a lot of integer values, it is more efficient to use this interface as opposed to the regular consumer interface, because with this interface you don't pay the cost of auto boxing, you also have long consumer and double consumer, Now let me show you how to use the consumer interface. I'm going to create a list of integers, and here I'm going to use list of to initialize this list with a few items. So 1, 2, and 3. Now there are two ways to iterate over this list. We can use a for each loop, so for each item in list, print each item. Now there is another way to iterate over this list. We can call list and take a look. This method expects a consumer object, in fact, a consumer of integer. As you saw, the consumer interface is a functional interface because it has a single abstract method. So we can represent it using a lambda expression. In this case, we want to pass a lambda expression that takes an integer and doesn't return a value. So let's pass a lambda expression here, first we add the parameter, integer operator that goes to a code block. Now in this case, we don't really need to specify the type of this parameter because the Java compiler knows that every item in this list is an instance of the integer class. So this type is redundant, let's remove it, now we have a single parameter, so we can get rid of the parenthesis, that is better. Now, in the body of this lambda, I just want to print this item, because we have a single statement, we don't even need the code block, so print item. So these are two different ways to iterate over a list. What we have on the top is an example of imperative programming, that basically means implementing logic using instructions. For example, whenever we have for loops or if and else statements or switch and case, we are writing code in imperative style. So imperative programming is a style of programming or a programming paradigm. Now we have so many different programming paradigms or styles of programming, like functional, object oriented, event driven, and so on. What we have on the bottom is an example of declarative programming. So instead of using instructions to specify how something should be done, we specify what needs to be done. So here we don't have a for statement, we're simply saying for each item in this list, print it. Now we're going to talk more about this concept, so if this is new to you, don't worry. Next, we're going to talk about chaining consumers. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

https://docs.oracle.com/javase/8/docs/api/java/util/function/Consumer.html



package Lambdas;

import java.util.List;

public class LambdasDemo {
    public static void show() {
        List<Integer> list = List.of(1, 2, 3);

        // Imperative Programming (for, if/else, swich/case)
        for (var item : list)
            System.out.println(item);

        // Declarative Programming
        list.forEach(item -> System.out.println(item));
    }
}

















9. Chaining Consumer: 

earlier i briefly mentioned that the consumer interface has a default method called and then. With this method we can chain consumers, which basically means we can run many operations in sequence. Let me show you how cool and powerful this is. So I'm going to create a list of strings and initialize it using list of. Here I'm going to pass three strings like a, b, and c. Now, you learn that the consumer interface represents an operation that takes a single argument and doesn't return a value. So, let's declare a variable of type consumer of string. I'm going to call this print. Now we should set this to a lambda expression. This lambda expression should match this method. So we should have a parameter of type string, and it shouldn't return a value. So, here we add string item, goes to, let's just print this item. Now once again, we can simplify this lambda expression by removing this type, because the java compiler knows that here we're consuming a string, so the item that we pass here is a string object, so there's no need to explicitly specify the type. Now because we have a single parameter, we can get rid of the parenthesis, that is better. So, here we're declaring an operation called print takes an item and prints it on a terminal. Now let's declare another operation that prints a string in uppercase. So, once again, consumer of string, we call it print uppercase. Pay attention to how I'm naming this consumers. The name of this variable clearly represents this operation. So, once again, item goes to, you want to print this, but first you want to convert it to uppercase. Pretty straight forward, right? Now, we want to iterate over this list, and for each item, we want to execute these two operations in sequence. So we call list.foreach, here we should pass a consumer object, so I'm going to pass print, now this consumer has a method called and then, now look at the parameter of this method, here we can pass a second consumer, so we can pass print uppercase, now when I run this program, you'll see that for each item First, we're going to print that item and then we're going to print it in uppercase. Take a look. there you go, that's pretty cool. Well let me explain how the magic happens under the hood. So I'm going to put the caret on the method name and then, now on the top, if you go to the navigate menu, look at the shortcut for declaration, on mac it's command and b, on windows it's probably control and b. So I'm going to press command and b, now you can see the source code of this method. So let's see what's happening here. This method takes a consumer object called first it ensures that this argument is not null, pretty straight forward. Now, look at what is returned here, we're returning a lambda expression, this lambda expression represents a consumer object, so it takes an argument of type t, and in the body of this lambda, first we call the accept method of the current consumer, so we're executing the current operation, and then we're calling the accept method of the second consumer, this is how two operations get executed sequentially. So every time we call this method we get a new consumer, and that means once again we can call and then on that new consumer to chain another task, let me show you. So, back to our demo, once we call and then we get a new consumer object, so here we can call and then and then pass another consumer object, like print. So with this code, we're executing three operations in sequence. Look at the result. There you go. We're printing each item 3 times on the terminal. So this is how we can chain consumers. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


LambdasDemo.java:

package Lambdas;

import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Consumer;

public class LambdasDemo {
    public static void show() {
        List<String> list = List.of("a", "b", "c");
        Consumer<String> print = item -> System.out.println(item);
        Consumer<String> printUpperCase = item -> System.out.println(item.toUpperCase());

        list.forEach(print.andThen(printUpperCase).andThen(print));
    }
}

















10. The Supplier Interface: 

supplier interface is the opposite of the consumer interface. So instead of consuming a value, it supplies a value. So if you look at the documentation you can see, here we have a single abstract method called get, which returns a value of type t. So this interface is also a functional interface and we can represent it using a lambda expression. Let me show you. So let's declare a supplier of double, call it get random, expression. Now this lambda expression should match the signature of this method. So it shouldn't have any parameters and it should return a value of type t, in this case double. So we type empty parenthesis because we don't have any parameters, the lambda operator and then in the body of this lambda I'm going to return math.random. Now because we have a single statement and we're simply returning a value, we can simplify this lambda expression. that is why the return keyword is grayed out. So, we put the caret over here and press alt and enter to replace this with a simpler lambda. Take a look. So, empty parenthesis goes to math.random. We don't need the curly braces, we don't need the return keyword either. Now, this lambda expression represents a function. To call this function, we should type get random.get. So this will return the random value and we can print it over here. So every time we run this program we get a different value. Now, what you need to pay attention to here is that this function is not executed until we explicitly call it. This is called lazy evaluation. So the value is not generated until we explicitly ask for it. Also, just like the consumer interface, this supplier interface has a few specializations for working with primitive values. So we have double supplier, we have int supplier, we have long supplier, and boolean supplier. So if you're working with primitive values, prefer to use these primitive specializations of the supplier interface because you don't pay the cost of auto boxing a primitive value inside a reference type and then unboxing it later on. So that's all about the supplier interface, next we're going to talk about the functional interface. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


The supplier Interface -> https://docs.oracle.com/javase/8/docs/api/java/util/function/Supplier.html



LambdasDemo.java:

package Lambdas;

import java.util.function.Supplier;

public class LambdasDemo {
    public static void show() {
        Supplier<Double> getRandom = () -> Math.random();
        var random = getRandom.get();
        System.out.println(random);
    }
}




















11. The Function Interface: 

Let's talk about the function interface. As the name implies, this interface represents a function or an operation that takes an argument and returns a value. So here we have two generic type parameters. T represents the type of the argument and R represents the type of the result. So over here, you can see that we have four methods in this interface. Three of them have an implementation. The only abstract method is the apply method that takes an argument of type T and returns a value of type R. Now just like the previous interfaces, we have a few specializations of this interface. For example, we have by function that takes two arguments and returns a result. We also have primitive specializations, and these specializations fall into three categories. In the first category, arguments have a specific type, but the return value is parameterized. For example, in function represents a function that takes a primitive integer. Take a look. example, here we have the apply method that takes a primitive integer and it returns a value of type function, you also have long function and double function. In the second category, the argument is parameterized. For example, to int function represents a function that returns a primitive integer. So over here you can see we have a method called apply as int, the argument is parameterized and the return value is a primitive integer. Just like this we have to long function and to double function. Now in the third category, both the argument and the return type are specialized. For example, int long function represents a function that takes a primitive integer and returns a primitive long. Take a look. So we have apply as long, which takes a primitive integer and returns a primitive long. Now let me show you how to use the function interface. So let's declare a function that takes a string and returns an integer. So we type function of string and integer we call it map, now we should set this to a lambda expression that takes a string and returns an integer. So let's say our parameter is str, then we add the lambda operator, and here we just want to return the length of this string. So we type str.length. There is no need to add a code block or use the return keyword. So here we have the map function, now to call it we call map.apply, give it a string like sky this returns the length of the string. So we can print it, and we get 3. So this is how we can use the function interface in java. 


Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

https://docs.oracle.com/javase/8/docs/api/java/util/function/Function.html



LambdasDemo.java: 

package Lambdas;

import java.util.function.Function;

public class LambdasDemo {
    public static void show() {
        Function<String, Integer> map = str -> str.length();
        var length = map.apply("sky");
        System.out.println(length);
    }
}


















12. Composing Functions:  

Let me show you how we can compose small functions to build more complex and interesting functions. So let's say we have a string in this format, key colon value. Now we want to pass this string through two transformations. First we want to replace the colon with an equal sign. So key equals value. That is our first transformation. Now in the second transformation, we want to add braces around this string. So second, add curly braces, and get something like this. So we're going to declare two small functions, and then we're going to compose them to build a larger, more complex function. So, first we declare a function of string and string, we call it replace colon, and set it to a lambda expression like this. str goes to str.replace we want to replace the colon with an equal sign, like this. Now the second function, so function of string and string, let's call this add braces, and set it to a lambda like this. str goes to, first we add the left brace, then we add our string, and then we add the right brace. Pretty straightforward, right? there are two ways to compose functions. Here's one way, we start the replace colon function, then we call and then, exactly like how we chained multiple consumer objects. Here we should pass another function, so we pass add braces, now this returns a new function, we can store it somewhere, or we can directly call the apply method, pass our string, key colon value, and then get the result. as simple as that. So let's print it, and there you go. That's what we get. Beautiful. Now, once again, this is an example of declarative programming. So instead of writing instructions to specify how we should do something, we express what needs to be done. The details are somewhere else. So we have a bunch of small reusable functions, and we're composing them to build something more complex. Now, when writing code like this, it's better to reformat our code like this. So we put each statement on a new line, that reads better, so replace colon, and then add braces, finally apply. Now we also have another method called compose. Compose is similar to and then, but it changes these functions in the reverse order. So if you want to achieve the same result, using the compose method, we should write code like this. We start with the second function, add braces, we compose this with the first function, replace colon, and then we apply key value to get the result, and then print it. Take a look. We get the same result as before. So there are two ways to compose functions. Now if you're curious how these work, put the carrot over here, on the top, from the navigate menu go to declaration, or use the shortcut So here's the implementation of the and then method. So this method takes a function, and it returns another function. Now, in the body of this function, first we make sure that the argument is not null, and here we return another function that is represented using a lambda expression, so we have this parameter of type t, first we call the apply method on this argument, we get the result, and then pass it to the apply method of the second function. Now the compose method is very similar, but it composes these functions in the reverse order. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


LambdasDemo.java:

package Lambdas;

import java.util.function.Function;

public class LambdasDemo {
    public static void show() {
        // "key:value"
        // first: "key=value"
        // second: "{key=value}"
		
        Function<String, String> replacecolon = str -> str.replace(":", "=");
        Function<String, String> addBraces = str -> "{" + str + "}";

        var result = replacecolon
                .andThen(addBraces)
                .apply("key:value");
        System.out.println(result);

        var result2 = addBraces.compose(replacecolon).apply("key:value");
        System.out.println(result2);
    }
}

















13. The Predicate Interface: 

Another type of functional interface we have in Java is the predicate interface, which is extremely useful. It has tons of applications in the real world. We use this interface to filter data. I'll show you how to do that in the next section. So, over here, you can see that this interface has a single abstract method called test. We give it an object of type T and get a Boolean. So with this method we can check to see if this object satisfies some criteria. For example, we can give it a customer object and check to see if that customer has a balance. This is how we use this interface for filtering data. Now, just like the other interfaces, we have a few specializations of this interface, for example, we have by predicate, which takes two arguments and returns a boolean. Take a look. So, here's a test method, it has two arguments of type t and u, and it returns a boolean. We also have primitive specializations of this interface. For example, we have int predicate which takes a primitive integer and returns a boolean. We also have long predicate and double predicate. Now let me show you how to work with the predicate interface in Java. Let's say we have a string like sky, and we want to check to see if this string has more than 5 characters. So we declare a predicate of string, call it is longer than 5. And set it to a lambda expression like this. str goes to str.length is greater than 5. Once again, we don't have a return keyword, we don't have curly braces either. All we have is a boolean expression, because we should return a boolean value. Now, to use this predicate, recall, is longer than 5.test. We give it our input, like sky, and then get the result. Next I'm going to show you how to combine predicates to build more complex predicates.

package Lambdas;

import java.util.function.Predicate;

14. Combining Predicates: 

So we talked about composing functions to build more complex and interesting functions. We can also combine predicates to build more complex predicates. Let me show you. So I'm going to declare two predicates. A predicate of a string called has left brace. So, str goes to str that starts with a left brace. That's a small reusable predicate. call the second predicate, has right brace, and here we call the ends with method, and pass a right brace. Now we can combine these predicates, let me show you. So if you type has left brace dot look, we have and, negate, and, or. These are equivalent to the logical and, the logical or, and the not operator in Java. So, let's say has left brace dot and here we should pass a second predicate, so we pass has right brace, now this returns a new predicate, we can store it over here, and call it has left and right braces, and then just like any other predicate, we can use that to test an object, so we call a test method and pass a string like this, key colon value, this returns the result, in this case the result is true, because this string has both the left and right braces. Now we can also combine two predicates using a logical or, so in this case we get a new predicate, and this returns true if our input string has either the left or the right brace. Now, what about the negate method? That is pretty straightforward. So we call the negate method, and this returns a new predicate does the opposite of the first predicate. So this is how we can combine predicates. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

LambdasDemo.java: 

package Lambdas;

import java.util.function.Predicate;

public class LambdasDemo {
    public static void show() {
        Predicate<String> hasLeftBrace = str -> str.startsWith("{");
        Predicate<String> hasRightBrace = str -> str.endsWith("}");

        Predicate<String> hasLeftandRightBrace = hasLeftBrace.and(hasRightBrace);
        var result = hasLeftandRightBrace.test("{key:value}");
        System.out.println(result);

        Predicate<String> negatehasLeftBrace = hasLeftBrace.negate();
        var result2 = negatehasLeftBrace.test("key:value");
        System.out.println(result2);
    }
}















15. TheBinaryOperator Interface: 

Earlier in this section we talked about this package, java.util.function. This is where we have all these functional interfaces in java. I told you that the list may appear overwhelming, but the concept behind this list is actually very simple. All these interfaces fall into four categories. We have consumers, suppliers, functions, and predicates. So all the interfaces you saw are specializations of these four types of functional interfaces. Now in this video we're going to talk about a special type of function called binary operator. This addition operator we have here is an example of a binary operator because it has two operands and returns a single result. Now we can represent this concept, this operation using the binary operator interface. Now as you can see, this interface extends the by function interface, so the binary operator interface is a special type of function. It's a function that takes arguments of type t and returns a result of type t. Now just like the other interfaces we have primitive specializations of this interface, for example we have int binary operator that takes two primitive integers and returns an integer. Now let me show you how to use the binary operator interface. So let's declare a binary operator of integer, call it add, and set it to a lambda expression like this. Here we need two parameters, so we should wrap them in parenthesis, we can call them a and b, then add the lambda operator and return a plus b, as simple as that. Now, because the binary operator is a special type of the functional interface, here we have the apply method, so we can pass 1 and 2, get the result, and start over here. Now in this example, we are working with primitive integers, so these primitive integers have to be auto-boxed inside instances of the integer class. So if you're dealing with a large number of primitive integers, it is more efficient to use the int binary operator interface. Okay? Now, let's make this more interesting. Let's say we want to represent this operation. We have two arguments, a and b, first we want to add them together, so a plus b, and then we want to get the square of the result. Now to model this, we should have two small functions, each representing one operation, and then compose them. So here's the first function. Now let's create a function to get the square of a number. That is pretty easy. So function of integer and integer, so this function takes an integer and returns another integer. We call it square, and set it to a lambda like this, a goes to a times a. We can use function composition to represent this operation. So first we add these numbers, and then the result. Now, if we print the result we should see 9, there you go. So this is how we can use the binary operator interface in java. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

Consumer, Supplier, Function, Predicate 

LambdasDemo.java: 

package Lambdas;

import java.util.function.BinaryOperator;
import java.util.function.Function;

public class LambdasDemo {
    public static void show() {
        BinaryOperator<Integer> add =  (a, b) -> a + b;
        Function<Integer, Integer> square = a -> a * a;
        var result = add.andThen(square).apply(1,2);
        System.out.println(result);
    }
}















16. The UnaryOperator Interface: 

interface we're going to talk about is the unary operator. So let's see how we can use this interface. I'm going to declare a unary operator of integer called square, and set it to a lambda expression like this, n goes to n times n. we can declare another unary operator of type integer, and call it increment. And set it to a lambda like this, n goes to n plus 1. So, first we can increment a value, and then we can get the square of the result. Now, let's apply this sequence on 1. So we're going to increment 1, we get 2, the square of 2 is 4. Let's get the result and print it to verify that our sequence of operations are working. There you go. So this is how we can use the unary operator in java. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

LambdasDemo.java: 

package Lambdas;

import java.util.function.UnaryOperator;

public class LambdasDemo {
    public static void show() {
        UnaryOperator<Integer> square = n -> n * n;
        UnaryOperator<Integer>  increment = n -> n + 1;
        var result = increment.andThen(square).apply(1);
        System.out.println(result);
    }
}

1. Introduction: 

streams are one of the powerful features of Java that allow us to process a collection of data in a declarative way. Kind of like how we write SQL queries. So we'll start this section with an overview of streams and how they allow us to process a collection of data in a clear and concise way, then we'll talk about various stream operations to build sophisticated queries such as mapping, filtering, slicing, sorting, and reducing, and finally we'll talk about collectors and how we can use them to build more complex queries. So let's jump in and get started.

2. Imperative vs Functional Programming: 

Before we talk about streams, let's look at the problem they try to solve. So let's say we have a list of movies, and we want to count the number of movies that have more than 10 likes. So in this project, in the streams package, I'm going to add a movie class, every movie should have a title and the number of likes. So private string title, and private integer likes. Now let's initialize this using a constructor, so we generate a constructor, and first I'm going to select the title field and then the likes field. Now we have a constructor with two parameters, the first parameter is the title and the second parameter is the number of likes. I also want to generate a getter for the likes field. So we put the caret over here, alt and enter, and create a getter. Pretty straight forward. Now, back to our demo class, let's create a list of movies. so list of movie, we import it, call it movies, and here I'm going to use list of to initialize this list with 3 movies. So here's the first movie, we call it a and give it 10 likes, the name doesn't matter, just something easy to work with, so we have b with 15 likes and c with 20 likes. So we have a list of movies, now we want to count the number of movies that have more than 10 likes. So we need a counter variable, initially we set it to 0, next we need to loop over this list, so for var movie in movies, now here we need an if statement, if movie that get likes is greater than 10, then we're going to increment the count. So this is how we can count the number of movies with more than 10 likes. Now, this style of code is called imperative programming. imperative programming is a programming paradigm or a style of programming. We have so many different programming paradigms like declarative, functional, object oriented, event driven, and so on. In a single application, you might have multiple paradigms. For example, you might implement certain features using the event driven paradigm, but we might use the functional paradigm in other parts of our application. This code that we have over here is an example of imperative programming. In imperative programming, we have statements that specify how something should be done. For example, here we have statements that specify how we should count the number of movies that have more than 10 likes. So first we initialize the count to 0, then we loop over our collection of movies, we have an if statement, and finally we increment the counter. So these statements determine how something should be done. In contrast to imperative programming we have declarative programming where express logic in terms of what needs to be done as opposed to how it should be done. For example, sequel is an example of a declarative language. We write a query to express what we need to get from a database. But we don't write the actual logic for pulling out data and filtering it. Now streams were introduced in Java to allow us process a collection of objects in a declarative or more accurately functional way. Functional programming is a special type of declarative programming it brings some additional concepts. Let me show you an example. So if you want to rewrite this code in a functional style, you would write it like this. We start from our movies collection, now every collection in Java has a method called stream, and this returns a stream of objects. Now what is a stream? Well, a stream is a sequence of objects, but it's not like a collection, it doesn't store data, it's just a way to get data out of a collection. metaphor think of a water tank. The actual water is inside the tank, but we have a bunch of pipes to get the water out of the tank. So a collection is like a water tank. That is where we store some data. A stream is like a pipe. Now we can attach one pipe after another, we can build a pipeline to transform data and get it out of a collection. You're going to see it throughout this section. So, over here, let's get a stream or a pipe from this collection. Now, this stream object has a bunch of useful methods, one of them is filter. With this we can filter data. Now look at the parameter of this method. Here we should pass a predicate of movie. We talked about predicates in the last section. A predicate is a function that takes an object and returns a boolean. So with this predicate we can check to see if this object satisfies some criteria. Now what is the criteria we're looking for here? We're looking for movies with more than 10 likes. So, let's pass a predicate here. movie goes to movie that get likes greater than 10. Now, this function is going to be applied to every movie in our collection, and only movies that satisfy this criteria will be pulled out of our collection. Okay? Now, we have another method that we can chain here called count. And with this we can count the number of movies or objects that come out of this pipe, come out of this stream. So, we get the result and store it in, let's say count 2. What we have here is an example of declarative or more accurately functional programming. Because we have a bunch of functions like filter and count, we're composing these to express a complex logic. As you can see in this example, we don't have instructions that specify how something should be done, we're purely expressing what needs to be done. We're saying we want to filter based on this condition, and we want to count. We don't have statements like setting the counter to 0 and then incrementing it. So this is the difference between imperative and declarative programming. So, with streams we can process a collection of objects in a functional way, and this makes our code cleaner and easier to read. I'm going to show you several examples throughout this section. Next we're going to talk about different ways to create a stream. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

Movie.java: 

package streams;

public class Movie {
    private String title;
    private int likes;

    public Movie(String title, int likes) {
        this.title = title;
        this.likes = likes;
    }

    public int getLikes() {
        return likes;
    }

}



StreamsDemo.java:

package streams;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class StreamsDemo {
    public static void show() {
        List<Movie> movies = List.of(
                new Movie("a", 10),
                new Movie("b", 15),
                new Movie("c", 20)
        );
    //Imperative Programming 
    int count = 0;
    for (var movie : movies) {
        if (movie.getLikes() > 10)
            count++;
    }
    System.out.println(count);
    }
}


Styles of Programming: 

Declarative 
Functional 
Object-oriented 
Event-driven 


Impertative: How
Declarative: What 

Streams: To Process a collection of data in a declarative way 


StreamsDemo.java:

package streams;

import java.util.List;

public class StreamsDemo {
    public static void show() {
        List<Movie> movies = List.of(
                new Movie("a", 10),
                new Movie("b", 15),
                new Movie("c", 20)
        );

    // Declarative(Functional) Programming
    var count2 = movies.stream().filter(movie -> movie.getLikes() > 10).count();
    System.out.println(count2);
    }
}






















3. Creating a Stream: 

have a few different ways to create a stream. We can create a stream from a collection, from an array, from an arbitrary number of objects, we can also generate infinite and finite streams, let me show you. So this collection interface that we have in java, let's say collection of integer, this collection has the stream method that you saw in the last video. That means every class that implements the collection interface has the ability to return a stream. So we can create an array list, array list and call list.stream to get a stream from this array list, okay? Now, what if you have an array? Let's create an integer array called numbers and initialize it to 1, 2, and 3. Now, arrays don't have the stream method, but we have the arrays class, this is a utility class declared in the java.util package, here we have a static method called stream, we can pass our array here, and this will return a stream. Now, every stream has this method for each, with this we can iterate over the stream and get each object. So, look at the parameter of this method, we should pass an int consumer here. This is a primitive specialization of the consumer interface that we talked about in the last section. So the consumer interface represents an operation that takes an object and doesn't return a value. basically consumes that object. The int consumer represents an operation or a method that takes a primitive integer. So here we can say n that's a primitive integer goes to print n. Now when we run we get 1, 2, and 3. We can also create a stream from an arbitrary number of objects. So the stream method has a static factory method called of here we have a var args parameter, so we can pass as many arguments as we want. We can also generate infinite streams, there are actually two ways to do that. We can use stream that generate, now look at the parameter of this method, here we should pass a supplier, that is an operation that supplies or returns a value. So, we can return math.random, and this will generate an infinite stream of random numbers. This is not something that we can do with collections. Because if you want to store an infinite number of objects in a collection, we're going to run out of memory. But with streams, this function is going to get called every time we read a number from this stream. So these numbers are not going to get generated ahead of time. This is what we call lazy evaluation. So if I create this stream and store it over here, and then run our program, nothing happens. No number is generated, we haven't consumed anything. we don't have infinite numbers in the memory. However, if we call stream.foreach and then pass a consumer here, let's say n goes to print n, now this foreach method is going to continuously request a new number from this stream and then print it. So, when we run our program you can see an infinite number of random numbers generated. Now, let's stop this To prevent this we can use the limit method. So, before calling the for each method, we call limit and then pass a maximum size, let's say 3. Now, when we run our program, we only see 3 random numbers. So, this is how we work with streams. We start off by creating a stream, then we apply one or more transformations on that stream, and finally we call an operation that actually terminates that stream. So for each method here, terminates this string, okay? Now we have another method for generating infinite or finite streams, and that is the iterate method. Let me show you. So, stream.iterate, now, this method has two parameters. First we need to pass a seed or an initial value, let's say 1, and then we pass a unary operator that would modify this value. So a unary operator is a function that takes a value and returns a new value. So we can say n goes to n plus 1. So we're starting with 1, and then every time we need to generate a value, we add 1 to the previous value. So once again, this will generate an infinite stream, we can apply a limit, let's say only 10 numbers, and then for each, we're going to print that number on a terminal. Take a look. So we get the numbers One to 10. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

Creating Streams: 

From collections 
From arrays
From an arbirary number of objects 
Infinite/finite streams



CreatingStreamsDemo.java: 

package streams;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.stream.Stream;

public class CreatingStreamsDemo {
    public static void show() {
        // From a collection
        List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>();
        list.stream()
                .forEach(n -> System.out.println(n));

        // From an array
        int[] array = { 1, 2, 3 };
        Arrays.stream(array)
                .forEach(n -> System.out.println(n));

        // From an arbitrary number of objects
        Stream.of(1, 2, 3)
                .forEach(n -> System.out.println(n));

        // Generate from scratch
        Stream.generate(() -> Math.random())
                .limit(3)
                .forEach(n -> System.out.println(n));

        // Generate from scratch
        Stream.iterate(1, n -> n + 1)
                .limit(10)
                .forEach(n -> System.out.println(n));
    }
}















4. Mapping Elements: 

quite often we need to transform the values in a string. To do that we use the map or flat map methods. Let me show you. So over here we have a list of movies, let's say we're only interested in the name of these movies. So instead of having a stream of movie objects, we want to have a stream of strings where these strings are the movie titles. So we start off by getting a stream from this list, movies.stream, now we call the map method to map or transform each movie object a different type. So, here we need to pass a function, this function takes a movie object and returns a different value. So we can pass a lambda like this, movie goes to movie dot, now we need a getter to get the name of each movie. So, real quick, let's go to the movie class, and generate a getter for the title field. So we press command and n to bring up the generate palette, and generate a getter for this field. as I told you before, we should always move these getters and setters down the bottom, that will make our code cleaner and more consistent. Now, back to our demo class, so we call movie.gettitle, and then we can chain the for each method, now look at what we get here. We get a string object. This is the beauty of streams. With streams we can build processing pipelines. So we get a pipe from this data source, then we attach another pipe here, in this pipe we get each movie object and extract its title. Then we pass the title to the next pipe. So here we can print the name of each movie. Take a look. So we get abc. So here's how the map method works. The function that we pass here, this function is going to get applied to every object in this string. So this is all about the map method. We also have primitive variations of this method, like map to int, map to double, and map to long. For example, the map to int method expects a to int function. This is a function that returns a primitive integer. So we can get a movie, and then get the number of likes from this movie. Now we have a compilation error here. Can you tell why? Here's the reason. with this pipe, we're extracting the number of likes of each movie and passing it to the next pipe. So in this pipe, instead of getting a movie object, we're getting an integer. Integers don't have a method called get title. This is the reason why we have a compilation error. Now if you want to get the number of likes of each movie, we delete this line, then we use the for each method to print these values. So this is all about the map method. Now we have another method called flat map, and a lot of people find it confusing, but it's actually very simple. Let me explain it. So I'm going to delete all this code, let's create a stream using the of method, so here we can pass an arbitrary number of objects, I'm going to pass two list objects, list of 1, 2, and 3, and list of 4, 5, and 6. So here we have a stream of list of integers that I'm going to show you. So I'm going to store the result over here, look at the type of this variable, stream of list of integer. So every object in this stream is a list of integers. So now, if we call stream.foreach, look, we should pass a consumer of list of integers. So we get a list here, and then we can print it. Take a look. every object in the stream is a list of integers, okay? Now sometimes we don't want to work with lists, we want to flatten these lists, we want to work with individual numbers, like 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, this is where we use the flat map method. So, before we call the for each method, we call flat map, now, here we should pass a function that takes a list of integers and returns a string. So, this function takes a list of return a stream from this list. How do we do that? Really easy, we call list.stream. So we convert each list to a stream. With this transformation, every object that is passed to the next pipe is going to be an integer as opposed to a list. Take a look. So I'm going to delete this code, here we should pass a consumer of integer. So we get a number and print it. Now let's run the program, we see the numbers 1 to 6. So this is how the flat map method works. With this we can flatten a stream of list of objects to a stream of objects. And it doesn't have to be a list, we could have a set, we could have an array, with flat map we can flatten a stream.

package streams;

import java.util.List;

public class StreamsDemo {
    public static void show() {
        List<Movie> movies = List.of(
                new Movie("a", 10),
                new Movie("b", 15),
                new Movie("c", 20)
        );

        movies.stream()
              .map(movie -> movie.getTitle())
              .forEach(name-> System.out.println(name)); // a b c
    }
}





StreamsDemo.java: 

package streams;

import java.util.List;
import java.util.stream.Stream;

public class StreamsDemo {
    public static void show() {
        var stream = Stream.of(List.of(1, 2, 3), List.of(4, 5, 6));
        stream.flatMap(list -> list.stream()).forEach(n -> System.out.println(n));
    }
}



















5. Filtering Elements: 

We talked about filtering earlier in this section, but let's review it one more time, I want to clarify a few things. So we have a list of movies just like before, we want to filter this list and only get the movies that have more than 10 likes. So we start off by getting a stream, then we call the filter method, now look at the return type of this method, it returns a stream of movies, that is the reason why we can chain other stream methods after calling the filter method. let's pass a predicate here, we get a movie, and check to see if the movie has more than 10 legs. Now, this method returns a stream of movies, so it returns a new pipe, and this is the reason why we can call the filter method again to add a second filter, we can also call the map method to transform each element, or we can call the foreach method. Now look at the return type of the foreach method, this returns This is what we call a terminal operation. So the stream methods fall into two categories. Some of them are intermediate operations, the others are terminal operations. Examples of intermediate operations are map and filter. They return a new stream, a new pipe. So we can continue transforming that pipe to get the data we want. Now, terminal operations are like the for each method. These are the methods that start consuming a stream. If you don't call a terminal operation, nothing is done. For example, if I simply call the filter method without applying a terminal operation, this will return a new stream, we can store it over here, let's call it filtered, now if I run the program, nothing happens, we just have a recipe for getting and filtering movies. The actual filtering happens the moment we call the foreach because this is a terminal operation. Now we get each movie and print its title. Let me remove this variable, and here we can also extract this predicate and store it in a separate variable. Let me show you. So we can have a predicate of movie, call it is popular, and set it to this lambda expression, m goes to m.getlikes greater than 10. Now, we can make this code more readable by passing this predicate over here. So we're getting a stream from our movies collection, we're filtering them based on their popularity and then printing them. What you need to understand here is that our data source, our collection of movies is not affected. So all the water is in this tank, but we're attaching new pipes to get a small amount of water. Next we're going to talk about slicing a string.

StreamsDemo.java: 

package streams;

import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Predicate;

public class StreamsDemo {
    public static void show() {
        List<Movie> movies = List.of(
                new Movie("a", 10),
                new Movie("b", 15),
                new Movie("c", 20)
        );
        
        movies.stream()
              .filter(m -> m.getLikes() > 10)
              .forEach(m-> System.out.println(m.getTitle()));

        Predicate<Movie> isPopular = m -> m.getLikes() > 10;

        movies.stream()
                .filter(isPopular)
                .forEach(m-> System.out.println(m.getTitle()));

    }
}
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
  







6. Slicing Streams:    

talk about slicing streams, we have 4 methods for getting a slice of a stream, we have the limit method which I showed you earlier, with this we can limit the number of elements in a stream, we also have the skip method, with this we can skip a number of elements in a stream, this is useful for pagination, and I will show you an example of this later in this video, we also have take while and drop while which take a predicate, so we take while we can pass a predicate and say, hey, as long as this condition is true, elements from this stream. Let me show you how to work with this method. We have the same list of movies as before, now we get a stream from this list, and call the limit method, look at the return type, it returns a stream, so it's an intermediate operation, we use it for transforming our streams. So let's pass 2 over here, then we call foreach, we get this movie, and print its title. If we limit the elements to 2, we should get only the first two movies, take a look. So we get a and b, beautiful. Now, if we call the escape method and escape the first two items, we're only going to get this movie, take a look. Now, with these two methods we can paginate data. For example, let's say we have 1000 movies, and we want to display 10 movies per page. And right now we're interested in the third page. How many movies should we skip? We should skip 20 movies, right? Now if I want to generalize this into a formula, that formula is skip page minus 1 times size. So the current page is 3, 3-1 is 2, 2 times 10, that is our page size, that gives us 20. So we skip 20 movies, and then we get 10 movies. If you want to generalize this, we would write limit page size. So if you have a collection of movies, and you want to get the movies in the third page, you would write code like this, skip 20 or we can write it in a more general form, then limit 10, and then for each, display their title. Now let's look at the take while and drop while methods. So I'm going to delete these comments. So here's our list of movies. Now, we call take while, here we should pass a predicate. As long as this predicate returns true, this pipe is going to take elements from the previous pipe. if you type m goes to m that get likes less than 30. With this, we're going to get the first two movies. So this pipe is going to stop the moment we get to the third movie. Take a look. a and b. Now you might be wondering, how is this different from the filter method? Well, the filter method iterates the entire data source to find objects that match our criteria. But as the take while method stops, the moment this predicate returns false. So in this example, if I change the number of likes of the second movie to 30, and this one to 20, you will see that this pipe will only get the first movie. even though we have two movies that have fewer than 30 likes. Take a look. so we get a. So the moment this condition becomes false, we stop, okay? So this is the take while method, we also have drop while, which is the opposite of the take while method. So it's going to escape all the elements that match this criteria and then take the rest. In this case, it's going to escape the first movie because it has less than 30 likes, but it will take the other two movies, b and c. Let's verify this. So run, And here's the result. Beautiful. 





Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

limit(n) 
skip(n) 
takeWhile(predicate)
dropWhile(predicate)


StreamsDemo.java: 

package streams;

import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Predicate;

public class StreamsDemo {
    public static void show() {
        List<Movie> movies = List.of(
                new Movie("a", 10),
                new Movie("b", 15),
                new Movie("c", 20)
        );

        movies.stream()
              .limit(2)
                .forEach(m -> System.out.println(m.getTitle()));

        movies.stream()
                .skip(2)
                .forEach(m -> System.out.println(m.getTitle()));

        // 1000 movies
        // 10 movies per page
        // 3rd page
        // skip(20) = skip ( (page - 1) x pageSize)
        // limit(10) = limit(pageSize)

        movies.stream()
                .skip(20)
                .limit(10)
                .forEach(m -> System.out.println(m.getTitle()));

        movies.stream()
                .takeWhile(m -> m.getLikes() < 30)
                .forEach(m -> System.out.println(m.getTitle()));

        movies.stream()
                .dropWhile(m -> m.getLikes() < 30)
                .forEach(m -> System.out.println(m.getTitle()));

    }
}





















7. Sorting Streams: 

By default the elements in a stream come in the same order as the underlying data source. For example, here we have a list of three movies, A, B, and C. If you create a stream from this list, the movies that we get in that stream are going to be in the same order, A, B, C. But we can change the order using the sorted method. So I'm going to change the order of these movies to B, A, and C, and then show you how to sort these movies by their title. First we get a stream, so movies.stream Then we call the sorted method. Now this method is overloaded, one version takes no arguments, the other version takes a comparator object. Do you remember the difference? We talked about this earlier in the course. There are basically two ways to implement sorting in Java. One way is to go to the movie class and implement the comparable interface. This is a generic interface, so here we pass the movie class. Now if we implement the interface, a compared to method in this class. With this method we can determine how these movies should be sorted. Perhaps we can say they're going to be sorted by their title. Now, this works for a lot of scenarios, but it has limitations. What if you want to sort a movie by a different parameter, let's say by the number of likes, or by the release date. That is when we use the comparator interface. So, back to our demo class. This sorted method, if we call the version without any arguments, this will rely on on the movie class itself to determine the ordering, and that means we should implement the comparable interface in our movie class. If you want to have more flexibility, we should use this version, here we should pass an object that implements the comparator interface. These are the methods declared in the comparator interface. Here we have a single abstract method for comparing two objects, so the comparator interface is a functional interface, and we can represent it using a lambda expression. So our lambda expression should match the signature of this method. It should take two objects and return an integer that will determine the ordering. So, over here, we pass a lambda expression with two parameters, let's call them a and b. Now each parameter here is a movie object, so if we type a dot, we see the methods of the movie class. Now let's say we want to compare the movies by their title. So we get the title of the first movie, this returns a string object, strings in java are comparable, so here we can call the compareTo method and pass the title of the second movie, b.getTitle. Now this expression is grayed out because there is a simpler way to rewrite this expression. I'm going to show you how this works in a second. But before we do that, let's call the forEach method and print each movie. So, m getTitle. Now run, So we get a, b, and c, beautiful. Now, how can we rewrite this code in a simpler fashion? Well, back to the documentation of the comparator interface, here we have a static method called comparing that is overloaded. With this we can easily determine the attribute that will be used for sorting data. Let me show you. So, here we call sorted, I'm just going to rewrite this expression down below so we can compare them. We call comparator.comparing, this is a static method in our interface, so we can call it like a static method in our class. Now, here we should pass a function that takes a movie object and returns a different value. That is the value we're going to use for sorting movies. So, we can pass a lambda like this, m goes to m.gettitle. What we have over here is identical to what we have on the top. But we're not done yet, we can even simplify this further. Look at this lambda expression. What are we doing here? All we are doing is calling a method. So this is where we can use a method reference. We can type movie double colon get title without parenthesis. Because we are not going to call that method, we are going to add a reference to it. Now, if you put the caret on this expression and press alt and enter, you can see that IntelliJ also produces the same expression. Now, we don't need one of them, Now, what if you want to sort these movies in descending order? That is very easy. Let's look at the return type of the comparing method. It returns a comparator interface. So right after calling the comparing method, we can call another method in the comparator interface. We have this reverse method, which is one of the default methods in this interface. With this we can change the order of sorting data. Now, let's run our program, we get c, b, and a. So this is how we can sort elements in a string. 





Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


StreamsDemo.java: 

package streams;

import java.util.Comparator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Predicate;

public class StreamsDemo {
    public static void show() {
        List<Movie> movies = List.of(
                new Movie("b", 10),
                new Movie("a", 20),
                new Movie("c", 30)
        );

        movies.stream()
             // .sorted((a,b)->a.getTitle().compareTo(b.getTitle()))
                .sorted(Comparator.comparing(Movie::getTitle).reversed())
                .forEach(m -> System.out.println(m.getTitle()));

    }
}


















8. Getting Unique Elements: 

Sometimes we want to get the unique values in a string. For example, let's say this number over here represents the price of a movie. We don't want to display 10 twice, that is repetitive. Let me show you how to do this. So, first we get a stream of movies, then we map each movie to its price. So, m goes to m that get likes. And here we could use a method reference, because all this lambda is doing is calling a method. So we type the name of the class, double colon, get likes. That is better. So we're mapping a movie to the number of likes. Now, we call the for each method, here we should pass a consumer of integer, so let's say like goes to print like. Once again, we can use a method reference here, because in this lambda expression, we're calling an existing method. So, system.out double colon print line. Let's run the program. 10, 10, 20, 30. This is repetitive. Now, to get the unique values, after we map a movie to the number of likes, we call the distinct method. Now did you see what happened? The moment I added the distinct method, IntelliJ added these tips here to tell us what is each method returning. So the first stream method is returning a stream of movies, the map method is returning a stream of integers, the distinct method is also returning a stream of integers. So if you have more than one intermediate operation, IntelliJ tells us how our data is getting transformed. That's beautiful. Now, let's run our program. So we get the unique list of values.

package streams;

import java.util.Comparator;

9. Peeking Elements: 

while working with complex queries, you may run into issues, make it the wrong result. To troubleshoot these issues, we can use the peek method, let me show you. So we have a list of three movies, just like before, first we create a stream, then we apply a filter, we only want to get movies with more than 10 likes. Next, we apply a map, so we map each movie to its title, and then So, t goes to print t. Now in this lambda expression, we're simply calling an existing method, so we can convert this to a method reference. System.out, double colon, println. The same is true for this other lambda expression, we're only calling an existing method. So, let's simplify this, that is better, so we're mapping each movie to its title. That is more readable. Now, let's run our program So we get b and c. Now let's imagine we expected something different. So one of these operations didn't do its job properly. This is where we can use the peek method to get the output of each operation. So right after filtering data, we call the peek method. This method takes a consumer of movie. So we get this movie and print its title. Movie.getTitle. I would prefer to add a label here, filter, plus, so this peek method is an intermediate operation, we can use it to build a processing pipeline, and that is the difference between this method and the for each method, because the for each method is a terminal operation, and returns void, so after we call this method, we cannot call another method of the stream class, it doesn't work. So, let's fix this code, alright, the map operation, we can pick our data one more time so peek here we should pass a consumer of string, because the previous operation is returning a stream of string objects. So, let's get the title of each movie, and print it. I would prefer to add a label here, mapped. So we know this is the output of the map operation. Okay? Now let's run a program and see what happens. look at this sequence of messages. For each element, we have these three messages. So the first message indicates that this b came out of our filter. So we got this in our first peek, and printed it on the console. Then we pass this to the map method, now the second message indicates that this element came out of the map method. That is the result of the second peek. And finally we have this b over here, that is the result of printing this item. Now we have the same sequence of messages for the second element in our string. So with the peek method we can observe the output of each operation, and this is useful for troubleshooting problems. 


Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


StreamsDemo.java: 

package streams;

import java.util.Comparator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Predicate;

public class StreamsDemo {
    public static void show() {
        List<Movie> movies = List.of(
                new Movie("a", 10),
                new Movie("b", 20),
                new Movie("c", 30)
        );

        movies.stream()
                .filter(m -> m.getLikes() > 10)
                .peek(m -> System.out.println("filtered: " + m.getTitle()))
                .map(Movie::getTitle)
                .peek(t -> System.out.println("mapped: " + t))
                .forEach(System.out::println);

    }
}

Output: 

filtered: b
mapped: b
b
filtered: c
mapped: c
c




















10. Simple Reducers: 

We talked about all the intermediate operations. As you saw, with these operations we can build and customize our processing pipeline. Now, over the next few videos, we're going to look at a group of operations called reducers. They basically reduce a stream of objects to a single object, which is the answer we're looking for. For example, you can use the count method to count the number of elements in a stream. So the count method reduces a stream to the number of elements in that stream. You also have a group of matchers these methods we can see if any or all elements in a stream satisfy a condition. You also have find first and find any for getting an element from a stream, and finally we have max and min for selecting the maximum or minimum element based on a comparator. All these operations are terminal operations, they produce a result, so we cannot call any other operations after them. Now let's see this in action. So we have a list of movies, now we create a stream, the count method, so this returns the number of elements in the stream as a primitive long value. And we also have any match, and this will tell us if you have any elements in the stream that satisfies a condition. So here we have to pass a predicate, we can say, movie goes to movie that get likes greater than 20. So we can ask if you have a movie that has more than 20 likes. Now, this returns a boolean So we get the result, and print it, in this case we get true because we have at least one movie that has more than 20 likes. Now we have a similar method called all match, and this will tell us if every element in the stream satisfies this condition. In this case we're going to get false, because we have two movies that don't satisfy this condition. We have another similar method called non-match, and this will tell us if none of the elements in the stream match this condition. So these are the matchers. Now, we have another method called findFirst, and this will return the first element in the string. Now look at the return type, that is an optional of movie. We'll talk about the optional class later on, but basically this optional class is useful for preventing null pointer exceptions. So we call the findFirst method, this will return an optional of movie, now we can call the get method to get the actual movie object. So we get the movie, and then print its title. There you go. We have a similar method called find any, this will just return any elements in the stream, we also have max and min, this will return the maximum or the minimum element based on a comparator. So, here we should pass a comparator of movie, this will determine on what basis we're going to compare these movies and pick the largest one. So we can call comparator.comparing, here we should pass a function that maps a movie object to a different value. So we can use a method reference, movie colon colon get likes. With this we can find the movie with the maximum number of likes. That is this movie over here. So let's run There you go. You get to see. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:
