14. Thread Signalling with wait() and notify(): 

Sometimes we want one thread to wait until some condition becomes true. For example, over here, the second thread has to wait until the done field becomes true. Now we're implementing this using a while loop with an empty body. The problem with this implementation is that it wastes CPU cycles. Because this while loop is continuously running until the done field becomes true. It may run millions or even trillions of times, so it wastes CPU cycles. Now we can solve this problem by using the wait and notify method. So every object in Java has this wait and notify method, let me show you. So here if you type status dot, take a look. Every object has these methods, notify all, notify, and wait. These methods are inherited from the object class, which is the parent of all classes in Java. So here we call status dot wait, and this will make this thread go to sleep until another thread notifies the thread that the state of the status object is changed. So when we call the wait method, this while loop is not going to run a million times. In the first iteration, this wait method is going to put this thread into sleep until another thread wakes it up. Now here we need to handle the uninterrupted exception just like before, so let's wrap it inside a try catch block. So this is the body of our while loop. Now, the Java virtual machine expects us to call the wait method inside a synchronized block. So we need to wrap this inside the synchronized block, and we're going to use the status object as our monitor. Now let's move the try catch block over here, good. So this is one part of the story. Now we need to go to our first thread, in our download file task class, after we call status.don, we need to notify the other thread that the state of this object is changed. So here we call status.notify. We also have notify all, this is useful if you have multiple threads waiting for a change on this object. With this we can notify them all. Now, just like the wait method, we need to wrap this line inside a synchronized block, otherwise the java virtual machine is going to throw a runtime exception. So let's add a synchronized block, we're going to use the status object as the monitor, and then call the notify all method. Now, with this implementation, the moment the second thread gets started, this while loop is going to start, and in the first iteration, the wait method is going to put this thread into sleep. So the while loop is not going to get executed a million times. Now, the moment the first thread finishes this job, it's going to notify the second thread, so the second thread is going to wake up, and then it's going to print the total bytes. Let's take a look. There you go. So this is a more efficient implementation than a while loop with an empty body that runs a million times. Having said that, wait and notify can get pretty tricky in complex applications. If you don't use them properly, you're going to run into all sorts of weird issues. So I would say don't use them in new code, these days we have better tools for implementing this kind of communication between threads, we'll look at them in the next section. I just wanted to briefly talk about wait and notify methods, in case you have seen them before and you are curious what they are and how they work. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


DownloadFileTask.java: 

package concurrency;

public class DownloadFileTask implements Runnable {
    private DownloadStatus status;
    public DownloadFileTask(DownloadStatus status) {
        this.status = status;
    }

    @Override
    public void run() {
        System.out.println("Downloading a File: "+ Thread.currentThread().getName());

        for(var i=0; i < 1000_000; i++) {
            if(Thread.currentThread().isInterrupted()) return;
            status.incrementTotalBytes();
        }

        status.done();
        synchronized (status){
            status.notify();
        }

        System.out.println("Download Complete.: "+ Thread.currentThread().getName());
    }
}




ThreadDemo.java: 

package concurrency;

public class ThreadDemo {
    public static void show() {
        var status = new DownloadStatus();
        var thread1 = new Thread(new DownloadFileTask(status));
        var thread2 = new Thread(() ->{
            while(!status.isDone()) {
                synchronized (status) {
                    try {
                        status.wait();
                    } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                        throw new RuntimeException(e);
                    }
                }
            }
            System.out.println(status.getTotalBytes());
        });
        thread1.start();
        thread2.start();
    }
}















15. Atomic Objects: 

Another way to achieve thread safety is by using the atomic classes in Java. So, in this package we have a bunch of atomic classes like atomic boolean, atomic integer, atomic long, and so on. With these atomic classes we can perform atomic operations. For example, earlier you learned that incrementing a variable in Java is not atomic, because it involves three instructions, get, increment, and write. With these atomic classes, we can increment or decrement a value in an atomic way, let me show you. So, in our demo class we have the same code as before, we have a single status object that we're sharing across many different threads, over here we're creating 10 download threads, each thread downloads 10,000 bytes, so once we join with all these threads, and print the total bytes, we expect to see 100,000 on the terminal. Take a look, we don't see that, so we have a race condition. to solve this problem, we go to our download status class. So here we are not using synchronization, we are not using locks, we have a very simple class. Now, to solve this problem, we need to change the type of this field from int to atomic integer. And then we set this to a new atomic integer. Now in our getter, we should return total bytes.get, this will return the actual value, and in our increment method, instead of the increment operator, we call increment and get. We also have get and increment, which returns the value first, and then it will increment it. So more accurately, increment and get is equivalent to plus plus a, where a is our variable, and get and increment is equivalent to a plus plus. In this case, we don't want to return this value, we just want to increment it. so it doesn't really matter which method we use. So, I would prefer to call increment and get because the intention is more clear. So that's it, that's all we had to do. Now, multiple threads can modify this field concurrently without waiting for each other. Let's run the program and verify this. So, take a look, there you go, you got 100,000. Now if you're curious how atomic types work, they actually use a technique called compare and swap, which is supported by most CPUs. So most CPUs can execute this operation as a single uninterruptible operation. So when we call the increment and get method, this atomic type is going to compare the current value with the expected value. If they're not equal, it's going to swap them. For example, let's say the current value of total bytes is 0. And we want to increment it. So the expected value is Now this atomic type is going to compare these values, because they're different, it's going to swap them. So 1 and 0. Now this entire operation is natively supported by most CPUs as an atomic operation. So, if you're dealing with counter variables, prefer atomic types to synchronization because they're faster and easier to use. 





Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


DownloadStatus.java: 

package concurrency;

import java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger;

public class DownloadStatus {

    private volatile boolean isDone;
    private AtomicInteger totalBytes = new AtomicInteger(0);
    private int totalFiles;

    public int getTotalBytes() {
        return totalBytes.get();
    }

    public void incrementTotalBytes() {
                totalBytes.incrementAndGet();
    }

    public void incrementTotalFiles() {
            totalFiles++;
    }

    public int getTotalFiles() {
        return totalFiles;
    }

    public boolean isDone() {
        return isDone;
    }

    public void done() {
        isDone = true;
    }
}

        this.status = status;
    }

    @Override
    public void run() {
        System.out.println("Downloading a File: "+ Thread.currentThread().getName());

        for(var i=0; i < 1000_000; i++) {
            if(Thread.currentThread().isInterrupted()) return;
            status.incrementTotalBytes();
        }

        status.done();

        System.out.println("Download Complete.: "+ Thread.currentThread().getName());
    }

import java.util.List;

public class ThreadDemo {
    public static void show() {
        var status = new DownloadStatus();
        List<Thread> threads = new ArrayList<>();

        for (var i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
            var thread = new Thread(new DownloadFileTask(status));
            thread.start();
            threads.add(thread);
        }
        for (var thread : threads) {
            try {
                thread.join();
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {

    }

16. Adders: 

atomic objects are great for implementing counters, but if you have multiple threads updating a value frequently, it's better to use one of the other classes in Java. They're faster than atomic types. So we have long adder for adding long values, and double adder. Let me show you how they work. So we have the same code as before, this is the code that has a race condition. So when we run this program, we see this weird number over here. Let's solve this problem using an adder class. So in the last video we replaced this integer field with an atomic adder. Now we're going to replace this with a long adder. And then we set this to a new long adder. Now, in our getter, we call the int value method, this will return the result as an integer. We also have byte value, short value, double value, and so on. Internally, long adder object keeps an array of counters that can grow on demand. So we don't have a single place in memory where our value is stored. We have a bunch of array cells, each holding a counter value. So different threads can modify these counter variables concurrently. That's why these adder classes are faster than atomic types, because they allow more throughput. So here we can call the int value method, but internally this method is going to call another method called sum, which is going to add up all these counter values and return the result. Now the int value will call this method and then convert the result to an integer. Now in our increment method, we call increment. We also have add for adding a long value, we have decrement, we have reset and so on. So let's call the increment method, that's all we had to do. Let's run our program, the raise condition is gone. if you have multiple threads updating a value frequently, prefer the outer classes to atomic types. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


DownloadStatus.java: 

package concurrency;

import java.util.concurrent.atomic.LongAdder;

public class DownloadStatus {

    private volatile boolean isDone;
    private LongAdder totalBytes = new LongAdder();
    private int totalFiles;

    public int getTotalBytes() {
        return totalBytes.intValue();  // sum()
    }

    public void incrementTotalBytes() {
                totalBytes.increment();
    }

    public void incrementTotalFiles() {
            totalFiles++;
    }

    public int getTotalFiles() {
        return totalFiles;
    }

    public boolean isDone() {
        return isDone;
    }

    public void done() {
        isDone = true;
    }
}

17. Synchronized Collections: 

sometimes we need to share a collection across many threads. So let's create a collection object, collection of integers, we call it collection, and set it to a new array list. Now let's create two threads, and have each thread add three items to this collection. So, thread one, set this to a new thread, and here I'm going to use a lambda expression to represent a runnable object. Because this runnable interface has a single abstract method that is run, so it's a functional interface, and we can represent it using a lambda expression. Now, this method doesn't have any parameters, and returns void. So, over here, I'm going to pass empty parenthesis, an arrow, and a block. Now, in the body of this lambda, we're going to call collection.addAll, here we're going to pass three items, so let's use arrays.addList, and pass 1, 2, and 3. Now, let's duplicate this code, so we create another thread, and call this one thread 2, and with this we're going to add 4, 5, and 6. Next, we start both these threads, so thread 1.start, and thread 2.start, then we join with them, so thread 1.join, and thread 2.start, that we should wrap this inside the try catch block, there you go, let's move this line over here as well. So once they're finished, let's print what we have in the collection. Take a look. So we have 4, 5, 6, one of our insertions was lost, this is because we have a race condition, two threads are racing or competing to modify our collection concurrently. To solve this problem, we can use a synchronized collection, which is a wrapper around a regular collection type in Java. So instead of setting this collection to a new array list, we're going to call collections, this class has a bunch of static factory methods for creating synchronized collections. So we have synchronized collection, let me put this on a new line so you can see clearly, take a look. We have synchronized collection, we have synchronized list, synchronized set, synchronized map, and so on. So let's call synchronized collection, here we should pass an object that implements the collection interface, like an array list. So new array list. Internally our objects are stored inside this array list, but when we call this method, this method is going to wrap this array list inside a synchronized collection, in that synchronized collection, all the methods like add, remove, and so on, they have synchronization code. So a synchronized collection essentially wraps a regular collection like an array list, but it makes it synchronized. Now with this simple change, when we run our program, we can see that two threads modify our collection concurrently. So this is how we can create a synchronized collection. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

ThreadDemo.java: 

package concurrency;

import java.util.*;

public class ThreadDemo {
    public static void show() {
        Collection<Integer> collection = Collections.synchronizedCollection(new ArrayList<>());

        var thread1 =  new Thread(() -> {
            collection.addAll(Arrays.asList(1, 2, 3));
        });

        var thread2 =  new Thread(() -> {
            collection.addAll(Arrays.asList(4, 5, 6));
        });

        thread1.start();
        thread2.start();

        try {
            thread1.join();
            thread2.join();
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            throw new RuntimeException(e);
        }
        System.out.println(collection);


    }
}




















18. Concurrent Collections: 

synchronous collections achieve thread safety by using locks. So when a thread gets access to a synchronous collection, the entire collection gets locked and other threads have to wait. This works well for most cases, but it can have a negative impact on the performance and scalability as the number of threads and concurrent operations increase. In those situations we can use concurrent collections in Java. These classes use the partitioning technique to allow concurrency. So they divide their data into segments threads can concurrently work with different segments, but only one thread at a time can access a given segment. So concurrent collections are faster than synchronized collections because they don't use synchronization. These classes are declared in the java.util.concurrent package. For example, we have concurrent hash map, we have concurrent link deck, which is a double ended queue implemented using a linked list. We have concurrent map and so on. So let's take a look at an example. If you want to create a regular hash map, you write map of integer and string, we call it map and set it to a new hash map. This is a regular hash map where our keys are integers and our values are strings. And here we can call map.put to add an entry like 1 and a. We can call map.get to get the value associated with the key, we can call map.remove and so on. Now this is a regular hash map implementation that is not thread safe. So if it's going to get accessed only by a single thread, it's perfectly fine. But if you want to share this across many different threads, we're going to have problems. Now to solve this, all we have to do is to replace the hash map with the concurrent hash map. That's all we have to do. Everything else in our code stays the same. Because this map is an interface, and hash map and concurrent hash map are two different implementations of this interface. So by programming against interfaces, we reduce the impact of changes in our applications. We simply substitute one implementation with another. Everything else stays the same. Again, I cannot emphasize the importance of interfaces enough. A lot of developers don't understand interfaces, they think they do, but honestly they don't. So if you want to have a better understanding of interfaces and their power, watch the second part of this series. It will change how you think about building software. 





Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

Normal Hash Map :

package concurrency;

import java.util.*;

public class ThreadDemo {
    public static void show() {
        Map<Integer, String> map= new HashMap<>();
        map.put(1,"a");
        map.get(1);
        map.remove(1);
    }
}



ConcurrentHashMap:

package concurrency;

import java.util.*;
import java.util.concurrent.ConcurrentHashMap;

public class ThreadDemo {
    public static void show() {
        Map<Integer, String> map= new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
        map.put(1,"a");
        map.get(1);
        map.remove(1);
    }
}















19. Summary: 

quickly recap the key points in this section. Multi-threaded applications can do more in less time, but they're challenging to build. One of the problems in multi-threaded applications is a race condition, which happens when multiple threads try to modify an object concurrently. We may get wrong results, or our application may crash. Another problem in multi-threaded applications is that a thread can change an object, but the change may not be visible to other threads due to caching. We talked about various strategies prevent these problems. The first strategy was confinement, which means not sharing data across threads. We also talked about synchronization, which involves locking and makes your code run sequentially. So try to avoid it if you can. We also talked about atomic types, these types don't use locks, instead they use the compare and swap technique. They're great for implementing counters. We also talked about partitioning, concurrent collections in java use this technique to allow multiple threads access different segments or partitions concurrently. So that brings us to the end of this section. In the next section we're going to talk about the newer higher level constructs for building concurrent applications in Java. So I will see you next.

The Executive Framework


1- Introduction

Working with threads is complex and error prone. So Java 5 introduced higher level abstractions that simplify building concurrent applications. With these abstractions, we don't need to explicitly work with threads. Instead, we focus on our tasks and let Java take care of thread manipulation. So in this section, we'll be talking about thread pools, executors, the callable and future interfaces, and asynchronous programming with completable futures. So let's jump in and get started. 


Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

Thread Pools
Executors 
Callable and Future interfaces 
Asynchronous Programming 
Completable Futures 

















2. Thread Pools:

Threads are the fundamental building blocks of concurrent applications. But working directly with threads has a couple of problems. The first problem is that we have a limited number of threads available to us. So if you're not careful, you may end up creating too many threads and get out of memory exceptions. So our application will crash. The other issue is that creating and destroying threads is costly. If you have a thousand tasks to execute, like a thousand images to download, and only ten threads available, we have to download ten images using these threads? then once these threads are complete, we have to create another set of 10 threads. And we have to repeat this several times to execute all our tasks. Creating a distraint thread is expensive. So, Java 5 came up with a solution called a thread pool. A thread pool is essentially a pool of threads called worker threads, and this thread can be reused to execute several tasks. When a worker thread finishes, it's returned back to the pool, so it can be reused to execute another task. these threads are not destroyed and recreated, they are always available and are reused to execute many tasks. Also, because a thread pool has a fixed number of threads, we don't have to worry about creating too many threads and running out of memory. For example, we can create a thread pool with 10 threads and submit a thousand tasks to it. The thread pool will take care of assigning our tasks to these threads. If all threads are busy, the new tasks will wait in a queue. As soon as a thread becomes available, it will pick up a task from the queue and execute it. So with this model, we don't have to think about creating threads directly. Instead, we submit our tasks to a thread pool, and let the thread pool take care of thread manipulation. I'll show you how to do that next. 












3. Executors: 

in Java the concept of a thread pool is represented using the executor service interface and its implementations. So we have thread pool executor, which is a typical thread pool implementation, this is the one that we use most of the time. We also have scheduled thread pool executor, with this we can schedule tasks to run after a delay or periodically. For example, we can schedule a task to run 5 hours from now, or every 2 hours. We also have fork join pool, This is a special type of pool that is designed to recursively split a task into smaller tasks, and then combine the results of each sub task to produce the overall result. It's like a divide and concur algorithm. Now let me show you how to create a thread pool. We have this executors class that is declared in the java.util.concurrent package. This class has a bunch of static factory methods for creating an executor service. So with these methods we can create an instance of these implementations. Why shouldn't we create a new instance of thread pool executor directly? We can definitely do that, but it's a little bit difficult, let me show you. So, if you type new thread pool executor, take a look. This constructor has a bunch of parameters like core pool size, maximum pool size, keep a lifetime, and so on. So creating an executor explicitly is a little bit difficult. That is why we use the factory methods on the executors class. So here we can call new single thread executor, this returns an executor with a single thread, not something that we use that often, we also have new fixed thread pool, this will create a thread pool with a given number of worker threads, so that would be an instance of the thread pool executor class. We also have new scheduled thread pool, this will return an instance of the scheduled thread pool executor. So, let's call new fixed thread pool with worker threads. Now we have stored the result in a variable called executor, now look at the type of this variable, that is executor service, so we're dealing with an interface here. At runtime, the type of this object is going to be thread pool executor, let me show you. So, if we print executor.get class, .get name, and run the program, we see thread pool executor, that is a thread pool with a number of worker threads, okay? So, we have an executor, now we can call executor.submit to submit a task to this thread pool. Now this method is overloaded, we can pass a runnable object, we can also pass a callable, that is a task that returns a result, we'll look at that later. So for now let's pass a runnable object here. I'm going to use a lambda expression, so here, let's print the name of the current thread, so thread.currentThread.getName. Now technically we don't need the braces here because we have a single print line statement, but I'm going to keep them here for clarity, because I don't want to put this line over here, otherwise it's going to pop out of the screen. So that's all we have to do to run a task on a separate thread. If you run this program, you can see our task was executed on this thread, full1, thread1. so we didn't have to explicitly create a thread. Now if we have a thousand tasks, we don't have to worry about creating too many threads and running out of memory. We simply submit those tasks to this executor, to this thread pool, and this pool will assign our tasks to worker threads. Let me show you. So here we have a pool with two threads, let's add a for loop for i, we start from 0 to let's say 10, now, in each iteration we submit attached to this pool. So we have only 2 threads available, but we are submitting 10 tasks. Let's see what happens. take a look. So, some tasks are being executed on thread 1, other tasks are being executed on thread 2. See? So internally this executor maintains a queue, every task that we submit goes in this queue and waits for an available thread. Now, let's get rid of this for loop, we don't need it anymore, so we create an executor and submit a task. Now, if I run this program again, we get this dialog box saying that our program is still running. Why is that? because when we start an executor and submit a task, the executor thinks there might be more tasks coming in the future. So it's not going to terminate, it's going to stay in the memory waiting for new tasks. So we have to explicitly shut down an executor to terminate our program. And to do that we call executor.shutdown. We also have shutdown now, the difference is that the shutdown method doesn't stop the current tasks, so it will wait for the completion of those tasks, but it's not going to accept any new tasks. In contrast, if we call shutdown now, this will force the existing tasks to stop, okay? So, let's call the shutdown method, now we run the program, we get this dialog box, because we didn't shut down our executor previously. So let's rerun this program, alright, our program shut down, so if I run it again, we don't get that warning, okay? Now, what if something goes wrong over here? Or we might have some code, and this code throws an exception. With that, we're not going to be able to shut down an executor properly. So as a best practice we should run this inside a try final block. So I'm going to add a try block here, in the try block we submit our task, and in the final block we shut down the executor. With this we'll make sure that no matter what we always down an executor and release it from the memory. So this is the benefit of using the executor framework. We don't have to worry about thread manipulation anymore. We let Java, we let the executor framework take care of all of that. But you have to remember, even when using the executor framework, we still have to worry about the concurrency problems. So if two tasks modify an object concurrently, we're going to run into issues. So the executor framework does not protect us from the concurrency problems we talked about in the last section. it just simplifies thread manipulation. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

Interface ExecutorService in Java: https://docs.oracle.com/javase/8/docs/api/java/util/concurrent/ExecutorService.html


ExecutorsDemo.java: 

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.Executors;

public class ExecutorsDemo {
    public static void show(){
        var executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(2);
        System.out.println(executor.getClass().getName());  // java.util.concurrent.ThreadPoolExecutor
        executor.submit(()->{
            System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName());  // pool-1-thread-1
        });
    }
}




Main.java: 

import executors.ExecutorsDemo;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        ExecutorsDemo.show();
    }
}




ExecutorsDemo.java: 

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.Executors;

public class ExecutorsDemo {
    public static void show(){
        var executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(2);
        System.out.println(executor.getClass().getName());
        try{
            for (var i =0; i <10; i++ ){
                executor.submit(()->{
                    System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName());
                });
            }
        }
        finally{
            executor.shutdown();
        }
    }
}

4. Callables and Futures: 

Sometimes we want to return a value from a task. For example, we may call the twitter API to get the latest tweets for a given user. That's when we use the callable interface. Now if you look at the signature of the submit method, you can see that this method is overloaded. So far, we've been passing a runnable object to this method, but we can also pass a callable object. This callable is an interface very similar to the runnable interface, but this interface represents a task that returns a value. So, If you scroll down, you can see this method has a single abstract method called call, this is similar to the run method of the runable interface, but instead of returning void, it returns v, where v is the generic type parameter here. So, if we return a value in the body of this lambda, now this lambda matches a method with no parameters that returns a value. That is our call method here. So this lambda expression that we have passed here, represent a callable object. Now, we store the result in a variable called future. Let's look at the type of this variable. That is a future of integer. This future is an interface that represents the future result of an operation. An operation that will complete in the future. It may complete in 500 milliseconds or 2 seconds or 5 seconds sometime in the future. So the result of this operation is not going to be calculated immediately using the CPU. So with this future object, we can get the future result of an operation, okay? Now, to make this demo more interesting, let's simulate a long running operation. So, in this package, the executors package, I'm going to add a new class called long task. In this class we're going to add a public static method, simulate, and here we're going to put the current thread to sleep for 3 seconds. So thread sleep for 3 seconds. Now let's handle the interrupted exception, now back in our demo class, in this task, instead of printing the name of the current thread, I'm going to call long task dot simulate. So we're simulating an operation that's going to take 3 seconds. Let's say we're going to call the twitter API to get the latest tweets for a user. Eventually this task is going to return a value, in this case 1. Now, when we call the submit method, the submit method is going to return immediately, so it's not going to wait for 3 seconds. It's going to immediately return a future object. With this future object we can get the result of this operation. So, this object has a bunch of useful methods, get, we can use this to get the value of this operation, like get is overloaded, with this version we can pass a timeout, so if that operation is going to take so long, perhaps we don't want to wait forever, we want to timeout in, let's say 2 seconds. we can also call the get method without a timeout value, we have cancel for canceling an operation, we have is cancelled, with this we can check to see if this operation is cancelled, and is done, which tells us if this operation is complete or not. So, I told you that this submit method is going to return immediately, so here we can do some more work, let's say, do more work, and at some point, you really need the result of this operation, that is when, recall, the get method. Now this method is going to block the current thread and have it wait until the result of this operation is ready. Eventually it's going to return a value. In this case, it's going to return an integer because our task returns an integer. So let's store the result over here, take a look, the type of this variable is integer, okay? Now here we need to handle a couple of exceptions, one of them is interrupted exception that you have seen before, the other is execution exception. And this happens if this task throws an exception. So it can cache this exception over here and do something with it. Now in this demo I'm going to simplify this code and combine these cache blocks. So vertical bar, execution, exception. Now let's get rid of the second cache block. So when I run this program you will see that we immediately see this message on the terminal, because the submit method is not going to wait for the completion of this task. It's going to start this task on a separate thread, and eventually when we call future.get we have to wait for this operation to complete. Now we get the result and print it. Let's see what happens. So, we see do more work, now 1, 2, 3 seconds later, the result is ready, and we print it over here. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


LongTask.java: 

package executors;

public class LongTask {
    public static void simulate() {
        try {
            Thread.sleep(3000);
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            e.printStackTrace();
        }
    }

}




ExecutorsDemo.java: 

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.ExecutionException;
import java.util.concurrent.Executors;

public class ExecutorsDemo {
    public static void show(){
        var executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(2);
        System.out.println(executor.getClass().getName());
        try{
            var future = executor.submit(()->{
                LongTask.simulate();
                return 1;
            });
            System.out.println("Do more work ");
            try {
                var result = future.get();
                System.out.println(result);
            } catch (InterruptedException | ExecutionException e) {
                e.printStackTrace();
            }
        }
        finally{
            executor.shutdown();
        }
    }
}





Main.java: 

import executors.ExecutorsDemo;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        ExecutorsDemo.show();
    }
}




Output: 

java.util.concurrent.ThreadPoolExecutor
Do more work 
1

















5. Asynchronous Programming: 

In the last video I told you that this get method is a blocking method. When we call it, the current thread has to wait until the result of this task is ready. So even though we're executing this task in a separate thread, we're making the current thread wait for the completion of that thread. We're not using our threads properly, we're wasting our threads. This is a very simple application, this is a command line application, but if you had a mobile or a desktop app, this main thread would be responsible for handling the UI events. such as mouse clicks and keystrokes. So if you make the main thread wait for the completion of another thread, it's not going to be able to respond to the UI events. The application window is going to freeze, and the user is not going to be able to resize or move it. So to get the most out of our threads, we should write code in a non-blocking way. And that is what we call asynchronous programming. Asynchronous is one of those fancy computer science terms, but basically it means non-blocking. This code that we have here is synchronous code. It's blocking. You want to write code in a non-blocking way. As a metaphor, think of a restaurant. When you go in a restaurant and order your food, the waiter comes, takes your order, and passes it to the kitchen. He doesn't have to sit there, wait for the kitchen to prepare your food before serving another guest. He can go serve someone else in the meantime. So that waiter, that resource is like a thread in our application. We don't want to make our main thread or another thread wait for the completion of another thread as much as possible. So we want to orchestrate our tasks such that when a task completes, another task can get executed asynchronously. In 2014, Java gave us a new tool for implementing this kind of applications, asynchronous applications. We're going to look at that next. 










6. Completable Futures: 

Throughout the rest of this section we're going to talk about a very important type called completable future. As you can see this class implements the future interface, so every completable future is a future object. Now the reason this is called completable future is that with this we can explicitly complete a future object. This is not something that we can do with the future interface. Now this class also implements another interface called completion stage, if you look at the documentation it says that this interface represents a stage of a possibly asynchronous operation. What does this mean? Well, in the real world, quite often an asynchronous operation involves many steps. For example, we may call a remote API to get some data, that is one step, then perhaps we want to transform that data into a different structure, that's another step, and finally we want to write that data into a database, that's another step. This interface represents a step or a stage of an asynchronous operation, and it gives us a bunch of methods for combining and composing these steps in a declarative way. Kind of like how we use the streams api to build a complex query in a declarative way. Earlier we used the map, filter, and reduce methods to build a complex query. Now we can do the same thing with this completion stage interface. I'm going to show you how to do that later in this section. So, over the next few videos, we're going to talk about the completable future class. I'm going to show you how to use this class to build complex asynchronous operations. But first, let me show you how to create a completable feature object. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

CompletableFuture in java: https://docs.oracle.com/javase/8/docs/api/java/util/concurrent/CompletableFuture.html













7. Creating a Completable Future: 

This completable future class has a couple of methods that we use for creating a completable future object. If you want to run a task that doesn't return a value, we call run async, this basically says run this task in an asynchronous or non-blocking fashion. Here we can pass a runnable object, or a runnable plus an executor. If you don't pass an executor, this method is going to run our task on a common pool. Let me show you. So here we have this class, fork join pool, this is one of the implementations of the executor service interface. Now here we have a static method common pool, and this returns the pool that is used by the completable future class. So if you don't supply a pool, this is the pool that is going to be used under the hood. Now this pool is well aware of the number of available threads. Earlier I told you how to get that, so we have this class, runtime, we call it getRuntime, and then available processors. So on my machine I have four cores, and each core has two threads. So the common pool is based on the number of available threads. Now, if this doesn't fit your application, you can always create a custom pool with fewer or more threads. Okay? Now, to simplify this, I'm not going to pass a pool, I'm just going to pass a runnable object. So, let's create a runnable object called task, and here we just want to print something. Now we can pass the task over here, now this returns a completable future object, now look at the type of this object, it's a completable future of void, because our task is not returning a value, okay? Now, with this single line of code, we can execute a task in an asynchronous fashion, we didn't have to create an executor and then submit a task to it and then shut it down, we only call completable future that run async. if our task should return a value, instead of calling the run async method, we call the supply async method. Now look at the signature of this method. Here we can pass a supplier, that is a functional interface, we talked about it before, it has a single abstract method called get that returns a value. Now similar to the run async method, here we can pass a supplier, or a supplier plus an executor. If we don't supply an executor, the common pool will be used. Okay? So I'm going to change this task to a supplier of integer. And here, let's just return This will return a completable future of integer, let me show you. So, the type of this future object is completable future of integer. Now, if you want to get this value, we call future.get, because this is a future object. So all the methods that you learn about future objects also exist here. As I told you before, this get method is a blocking method, so it's going to block the current thread until the result is ready, but in this completable future object, have a bunch of other methods that allow us to build complex asynchronous operations. We'll talk about them as you go through this section. Now, let's call the get method and store the result over here. We should wrap this with a try catch block, and then print the result. There you go. So, this is how we can create a completable feature object. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

CompletableFuturesDemo.java:

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;
import java.util.concurrent.ExecutionException;
import java.util.function.Supplier;

public class CompletableFuturesDemo {
    public static void show(){
        // ForkJoinPool.commonPool()
        // CompletableFuture.runAsync()

        Runnable task = () -> System.out.println("a");
        var future = CompletableFuture.runAsync(task);  // a

        Supplier<Integer> task2 = () -> 1;
        var future2 = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(task2);

        try {
            var result = future2.get();
            System.out.println(result); // 1
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            throw new RuntimeException(e);
        } catch (ExecutionException e) {
            throw new RuntimeException(e);
        }

    }
}




Main.java: 

import executors.CompletableFuturesDemo;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        CompletableFuturesDemo.show();
    }
}





















8. Implementing an Asynchronous API: 

Let me show you how to create an asynchronous API, this is a very powerful technique that you should start using in your applications right away. So, in this project I'm going to add a new class, mail service, we're going to use this class to send emails to our users. Now, here we need a method like public void send, now in a real application we should pass a mail message object, but let's not worry about that, instead, we're just going to print a message like mail was sent. Now sending an email is a long running operation because we have to go over the network. Basically, anytime we have to touch the file system or the network we're dealing with a long running operation. And we shouldn't run these operations on the main thread, we should run them on a separate thread. So to simulate a long running operation, I'm going to use our long task class that we created in the last section. Here we call the simulate method, which adds a 3 second delay to this method. in our main method, I'm going to create a new mail service, and then call service.send. Now, this is a synchronous or blocking operation, so if you print another message here like hello world, we're not going to see this until this method returns, let me show you. So, run, you're waiting for the mail to be sent, 3 seconds later, the mail is sent, and now we see the hello world message. So this is the problem with synchronous or blocking code. Now, anytime we have a long running operation like querying a database, calling a remote service, working with the file system, we should run these operations asynchronously. Let me show you how to do that. So we go back to our mail service class, I'm going to re-implement this method in an asynchronous fashion. So we add another method, public, instead of returning void, we're going to return a completable future of void. if this method returned an integer, here we would use completable future of integer. Now we call this method send async, so by convention we add the async suffix to this method. Now, here we should return a completable future object, you learned how to do that in the last video. So we return completable future, because we're not going to return a value, we should call the run async method. Now here we should pass a runnable object, So we use a lambda, and in the body of this lambda we simply call our synchronous method. As simple as that. Now with this single line of code, we can execute this method in an asynchronous fashion. Because when we call the run async method, the task that we pass here is going to be executed on a separate thread on the common thread pool. So if you have an existing synchronous method, you can simply convert it to an asynchronous method by wrapping it inside completable future object. Now, back to our main method, here we call sendAsync, now because this is an asynchronous method, it's not going to block the current thread, so the moment I run this program, we're going to see this hello word message immediately, then we wait 3 seconds for the mail to be sent. Take a look, so run, we see the hello word message, but what happened to our mail? Well, here we have a command line program, and this program terminated so quickly that we didn't see the result of this task that was executed on a separate thread. We don't have this problem in a mobile or a desktop app, because these apps are continuously running until the user terminates them. So to prove to you that our mail was being sent on a separate thread, I'm going to put a delay over here, let's say thread.sleep for 5 seconds, we should wrap this in a try catch block, now we run our program we see the hello world message, 3 seconds later the mail was sent, and then 2 seconds later our program finished. So this is the benefit of asynchronous apis, they don't block the current thread and allow us to better exploit our parallel hardware.

        service.send();

Making it to run asynchronously:


MailService.java: 

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;

public class MailService {
    public void send() {
        LongTask.simulate();
        System.out.println("Mail was sent.");
    }

    public CompletableFuture<Void> sendAsync() {
        return CompletableFuture.runAsync(() -> send());
    }
}




Main.java: 

import executors.CompletableFuturesDemo;
import executors.ExecutorsDemo;
import executors.MailService;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var service = new MailService();
        service.sendAsync();
        System.out.println("Hello World");

        try {
            Thread.sleep(5000);
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            throw new RuntimeException(e);
        }
    }
}










9. Running Code on Completion: 

Quite often we need to execute some code when an asynchronous operation completes. For example, we may want to display the result or store it in the database. Let me show you how to do that. First, let's create a completable future object. Here we call the supply async method and pass a lambda that returns 1. Now this returns a completable future object, so let's store it over here. Now here we have a method called then run, this is one of those methods that is provided by the completion stage interface. So if you type completion stage x and then type x dot, here we can see all the methods declared in this completion stage interface. Earlier I told you that this interface represents a stage or a step in an asynchronous operation. So here we have a bunch of methods for modeling complex asynchronous operations, a lot of these methods start with then, that means when this task is finished, then do something else. So here we're going to call future.then run, that means we're going to run some code once this task is finished. Now, here we need to pass a runnable object, so we pass a lambda and print a message like done. Let's run our program, here's our message, we also have then run async, this method is like the run async method that you saw earlier. It's basically saying run or execute this task in an asynchronous fashion. So the task that we pass here, this runnable object is going to be submitted to the underlying executor and executed asynchronously. Let me show you. So I'm going to change this lambda, let's add a code block, here we print our done message, but before that let's print the name of the current thread. So, thread current thread.get name. Now, let's run our program, you can see that this task was executed on this thread, fork join pool.comment pool worker 3. In contrast, if we call then run, this task is executed on our main thread. So that is the difference between then run and then run async. Now we also have another pair of methods called then accept, this is useful if you want to get the result of this completable feature. Let me show you. So delete, look at the signature of this method. Here we should pass a consumer object. We talked about consumers earlier in the course. So this consumer interface has a single abstract method called accept. It takes an object and doesn't return a value. So, here we can pass a lambda like this, result, and in the body of this lambda, we're going to print the current thread, so thread.currentThread.getName, and then we're going to print the result. Let's see what happens. So, this task was executed on the main thread, and here's the result of this task. similarly we have then accept async, and this will submit this task to be executed asynchronously. Take a look. Now this task was executed on this thread, but we don't see the result. This happened because our main thread finished earlier than this other thread. So if we put a delay in our main thread, we can see the result. Let's put this in sleep for let's say 2 seconds, and run the program again, there you go. So here's our thread and here's the result. So this is how we can execute some code upon completion of an asynchronous task.

    public static void show(){
        var future = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 1);
        future.thenRun(() -> System.out.println("Done"));
    }
}

        future.thenRunAsync(() -> {
            System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName());
            System.out.println("Done");
        });
    }

10. Handling Exceptions: 

We learned how to consume the result of an asynchronous task. But what if this task throws an exception? For example, let's say we try to call a remote weather service to get the current weather in a given city. Now, this remote call may fail for various reasons. So let's see how we can handle exceptions and recover by transforming an exception into a value. So, I'm going to create a completable future object using the supply async method. In the body of this lambda, first we print a message like getting the current weather, and then we throw a new illegal state exception. The type of the exception doesn't matter, I just want to throw an exception. Now, this returns a completable future, so let's run our program and see what happens. We didn't see anything, because this exception was thrown on a different thread. To get this exception, we have to call get method of the future interface. So if you look at the documentation of the future interface, you can see that the get method returns a value of type v, and it may throw an exception of type interrupted exception or execution exception. So if an exception is thrown on the thread that is executing our task, the get method is able to propagate that exception and bring it into our main thread. Let me show you. So, here we call future.get wrap this inside a try catch block, then run our program, now we get the exception. So, you are trying to get the current weather, but we got an execution exception. This execution exception is caused by an illegal state exception. So the exception that we threw in our lambda is wrapped inside an execution exception. So, when we call the get method, we may get an interrupted exception, this happens if the thread is sleeping but we try to interrupt it, or we may get an execution exception. This happens if something goes wrong during the execution of our asynchronous task. Now we can get the actual cause of this exception by calling e.get cause. This will return the exception that we threw here. Now, we don't want our application to crash, so what should we do here? in this case we can recover and provide a default value like the last temperature that we successfully read before. Let me show you how to do that. So first I'm going to delete this line. Before we call the get method, we call another method called exceptionally. Now look at the signature of this method. It takes a function that maps a throwable to a different type. We talked about throwables earlier in the section about exceptions. So this throwable class is the base or parent of all errors and exceptions in java. So here we need to pass a lambda that maps this exception to a different type. So we can get this exception and map it to a numeric value like 1. Let's imagine this is the last temperature that we read successfully. Now, when we call the get method, we get the result, that is the last temperature, so let's store it over here, and then print it. we didn't get an exception this time. So this is the benefit of using the exception method. Now one thing you need to understand is that this method will return a new completable future. So this completable future is different from the one we originally created, with this method we're programming this completable future to return a default value if an exception is thrown. So when we call the get method on this new completable future, we'll get that default value. If you call the get method on the original completable future, we'll get an exception. So this is something that you're going to see again and again throughout this section. A lot of methods in the completable future class return a new completable future. With this we can build a recipe for our asynchronous operations.

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.ExecutionException;

public class CompletableFuturesDemo {
    public static void show(){
        var future = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() ->{
            System.out.println("Getting the current Weather");
            throw new IllegalStateException();
        });

        try {
            future.get();
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            throw new RuntimeException(e);
        } catch (ExecutionException e) {
            throw new RuntimeException(e);
        }
    }
}

11. Transforming a Completable Future: 

Sometimes we need to transform the result of an asynchronous task. For example, our weather service may return a temperature in a complex data structure, but perhaps we're only interested in the temperature as a numeric value. So we want to map or transform the result into a different type. Or as another example, imagine the weather service returns the temperature in Celsius, but we want to print it in Fahrenheit. So let me show you how to do that. First I'm going to create a completable future using the supply async method. here we pass a supplier that returns the temperature in Celsius. Let's say 20 degrees. Now we get a completable future and store it over here. This future object has a method called then apply. So as I told you before, whenever you see then that means execute this piece of code after this task or this future is complete. So with the apply method we can convert or map the result to a different type. We also have then apply async, which will execute this code asynchronously. So it will submit it as a new task to the underlying executor. Okay? So, let's call then apply, here we should pass a function that takes an integer and maps it to a different type. So, we get a result in Celsius, and we can convert it to Fahrenheit using this formula. Celsius times 1.8 plus 32. just like the exceptionally method that you learned about in the last video, this method returns a new completable feature. So if we call the get method on this completable feature, we'll get the result in Fahrenheit. Let me show you. So we call get and store the result over here. Now to make our code cleaner, it's nicer to put each method on a new line. That is better. Now let's wrap this inside a try catch block. So we get the result in Fahrenheit and then print it. So that is 68 degrees, beautiful. Now, we can make this code better, instead of passing this formula as a lambda, we can encapsulate this inside a method. So, I'm going to add a static method in this class, public static integer 2 F, a bit hard to spell, we give it a parameter called Celsius, and here we return Celsius times 1.8 plus 32. Now here we have a compilation error, because the result of this expression is a double, but we're expecting an integer. We can solve this by casting this to an integer, the problem is gone. Now, we can replace this lambda with a method reference, so the name of our class is completable futures demo, we type completable futures demo, colon colon 2 farenheit. That is better. Now, here we don't have to call method, earlier I showed you how to run code upon completion of a task, we talked about two methods, then run for executing a task, and then accept for executing a task but also passing the result to that task. So I'm going to simplify this code. Let's grab what we have inside this try block, cut it, we don't need the try block anymore, so here we create a completable future, our remote api, then we transform the result to fahrenheit, now instead of calling the get method we call then accept, because we want to get the result, the result here is going to be temperature in fahrenheit. So f goes to print f. Now, we don't need the print statement anymore, and we don't need this variable either. So, build a completable future, then we transform it, and finally we accept or consume the result. Let's run our program, there you go, so here's the temperature in Fahrenheit. So this is the benefit of completable futures. With these completable futures, we can build complex asynchronous operations in a declarative way. 



Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


CompletableFuturesDemo.java: 

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;
import java.util.concurrent.ExecutionException;

public class CompletableFuturesDemo {
    public static void show(){
        var future = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 20);
        try {
            var result = future.thenApply(celsius -> (celsius * 1.8) + 32).get();
            System.out.println(result); // 68.0
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            throw new RuntimeException(e);
        } catch (ExecutionException e) {
            throw new RuntimeException(e);
        }
    }
}


Main.java: 

import executors.CompletableFuturesDemo;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        CompletableFuturesDemo.show();
    }
}




CompletableFuturesDemo.java:


package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;

public class CompletableFuturesDemo {
    public static int toFahrenheit(int celsius){
        return (int) (celsius * 1.8) +32;
    }

    public static void show(){
        var future = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 20);
        future.thenApply(CompletableFuturesDemo::toFahrenheit).thenAccept(f -> System.out.println(f));
    }
}




Main.java:

public class Main {

        CompletableFuturesDemo.show();
    }

12. Composing Completable Futures: 

often we want to start a task upon completion of another task. For example, let's say we have the idea of a user, now we want to get the user's email, so we have to go to our database and read that user record, with this we can get their email. Now, we want to pass this email to one of those music streaming websites where people have their own playlist, so we pass the email and get the playlist for a given person. So here we have two tasks, two asynchronous operations, we want to start the second task completion of the first task. Completable futures make it really easy to implement these kind of scenarios in a declarative and efficient way. Let me show you. So first we're going to create a completable future for getting the email of a user. So, completable future, that's supply async, you pass a lambda, and here we just want to return the email for this user. In a real world application, in the body of this lambda, we're going to query a database. But here I just want to return a string. This returns a completable future of string. Right? So completable future of string. Now, we want to start the second task upon completion of the first task. To do that we call future. then compose. Now here we should pass a function that maps a string to a completion stage object. That means from this function we should return a new completable future. That completable future a new asynchronous task, right? So what is the string that we get here? That is the email that is returned from our first task. So we get the email, now we want to pass this to a second task. So here we should return a new completable future object. That is very easy, so completable future dot supply async, here we should call that remote service to get the playlist for this user. So we pass another supplier object, with this supplier, I'm going to return this string, playlist. Now, in a real application, we're going to return a playlist object, or a list of songs, but here I don't want to waste time creating different classes, I just want to return a string. All I want you to understand is that our first task returns an email, then we pass the email to our second task, and get the playlist. Okay? So, when we call then compose, we get a new completable future, so here we can chain another method, like then accept, we get the playlist and print it on the terminal. As simple as that. Now, let's reformat our code, so let's put each method call on a new line, that is better, we don't need the comments, let's run our program, so we get the playlist, beautiful. Now, let's improve this code further. technically we don't need this future variable, we can immediately call this method on the object that is returned over here. So let's get rid of the future variable. So we call completable future.supply async, this is better, but we can make this even better. What is this lambda representing? It's representing a task for getting a user object from a database. So in a real application, you would probably have a method somewhere like public, in this case static, in a real application we would return a user object, but here I want to return a string. We call this method get user email, I told you that querying a database is a long running operation, so we should make our APIs asynchronous, so instead of returning a string, we should return a completable future of string. Now, inside this method we're going to have some code for querying a database and getting someone's email. So we return completable future dot supply async and return this email. Okay? Now, with this method, we no longer need this piece of code over here, we can simply call get user email. Also, as I told you before, it's better to use the async suffix here to indicate that this is an asynchronous method. So let's rename this to get user email async. going to apply the same technique over here for the second task that gets an email and returns a playlist asynchronously. So in a real application somewhere we're going to have another method for getting the playlist of a given user. So public static, we have a long running operation, so we should return a completable future of in this case string, and we call that method getPlaylistAsync. Now here we need a parameter of type string called email, so this method gets the email of a user and calls a remote API to get the playlist for that user in an asynchronous fashion. Now here we should return a completable future, that's supply async, and we return this playlist. Right? Now with this refactoring, we no longer need this lambda over here, we can use a method reference. So the name of this class is completable futures demo, I'm going to type that here, completable futures demo, then we have colon colon and finally get playlist async. Now look at the end result. We're getting the email of a user in an asynchronous fashion, then we're composing this with another operation for getting the playlist, and finally we print the playlist. This is the beauty of completable futures. We can build this kind of complex asynchronous operations in a declarative way. 




Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

CompletableFuturesDemo.java: 

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;

public class CompletableFuturesDemo {
    public static void show(){
        // id -> email
        // email -> playlist

        var future = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "email");
        future.thenCompose(email -> CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "playlist"))
                .thenAccept(playlist -> System.out.println(playlist));

    }
}





Main.java: 

import executors.CompletableFuturesDemo;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        CompletableFuturesDemo.show();
    }
}


Output: 
playlist 




Making CompletableFuturesDemo Class more simpler:  

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;

public class CompletableFuturesDemo {
    public static CompletableFuture<String> getUserEmailAsync() {
        return CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "email");
    }

    public static CompletableFuture<String> getPlaylistAsync(String email) {
        return CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "playlist");
    }

    public static void show(){
        getUserEmailAsync().thenCompose(CompletableFuturesDemo::getPlaylistAsync).thenAccept(playlist -> System.out.println(playlist));

    }
}












13. Combining Completable Futures: 

In my opinion, one of the most powerful features of completable futures is the ability to start two tasks asynchronously and then combine the results. For example, let's say we call a remote service to get the price of a product, and that service returns the price in US dollars. So we get 20 US dollars. Now at the same time, in parallel, we want to call another service to get the exchange rate between US dollars and Euros. So that service is going to return Now, we don't want to call the second service upon completion of the first task. We want to start this task concurrently, and then wait for both of them to complete in order to calculate the result. So, let me show you how to do this. First we want to create a completable future to return the price of a product. So, completable future.supply async, and here we return 20. That is our first task. Now, in our second task, we're going to call another service to get the exchange rate between US dollars and euros. So one more time, completable future, dot supply async, here we return 0.9. This is our second task. Now we call first dot then combine. With this we can combine the result of two asynchronous operations. So we can wait for both of them to complete to calculate the final result. But here's the interesting part. calling this method is not going to block the current thread, we're basically building a processing pipeline, we're building a recipe, we're telling the executive framework, hey, go ahead and start these two tasks concurrently, when they're both complete, start a new task to do something else. All of this is happening asynchronously, okay? So, look at the signature of this method. Here we have two parameters, the first parameter is a completion stage object, that is our second task. So let's pass it over here. Now the second parameter is a by function. That is a function with two parameters that returns a value. Look at the type of these parameters. The first parameter is an integer, the second is a double. So these parameters represent the results of our tasks. So the first task returns a price in US dollars, and the second task returns the exchange rate. Exchange rate. So this is our by function. Now we get these two values, and based on these we're going to calculate a new value. So here we can return price times exchange rate. Okay? Now, this method, then combine is going to return a new completable future, so after that we can call then accept, here we get the result and print it. Now, let's reformat our code So run, so the price is $18. Now let's make this example more interesting. Let's imagine that the first task returns the price as a string, 20 us dollars. Now we have a compilation error, because we cannot multiply a string by a number. So, in our first task, we should transform the result. So right after calling the supply async method, we call then apply. Here we should pass a function that takes a string and returns a new value, in this case an integer. So I'm going to reformat this code, first we call supply async, and then apply. So here we get a string, now we call str.replace, I'm going to replace us dollars with an empty string, we can also use regular expressions, it doesn't really matter, this is a very basic transformation. So we get that, that gives us the price, now we call integer.parseInt and pass the price. And finally return the result. So here's our first task, then we have our second task, we combine them and wait for both of them to complete before displaying the result. Now all of this is happening asynchronously, so none of this code is going to block the current thread.

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;

public class CompletableFuturesDemo {
    public static void show(){
       // 20 USD
       // 0.9
       var first = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 20);
       var second =  CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 0.9);

       first.thenCombine(second, (price, exchangeRate) -> price * exchangeRate)
               .thenAccept(System.out::println);
    }
}

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;

public class CompletableFuturesDemo {
    public static void show(){
       // 20 USD
       // 0.9
       var first = CompletableFuture
               .supplyAsync(() -> "20USD")
               .thenApply(str -> {
                    var price = str.replace("USD", "");
                    return Integer.parseInt(price);
               });
       var second =  CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 0.9);

       first.thenCombine(second, (price, exchangeRate) -> price * exchangeRate)
               .thenAccept(System.out::println);
    }
}


















15. Waiting for Many Tasks to Complete: 

sometimes we need to wait for the completion of many tasks before doing something else. Let me show you how to do this. So I'm going to create 3 completable futures to represent 3 asynchronous tasks. Here's the first one, we set this to completable future, that's supply async, and here we return 1. Here's our second task, we return 2, and here's the third task. Now, we want to wait for all these tasks to complete before doing something else. To do that we call completable future dot all of. Now look at the signature of this method. Here we have completable future dot dot dot, this is called var args which means a variable number of arguments. We can pass zero or more arguments. So I'm going to pass first, second, and third. Now this will return a new completable future that will complete when all of these completable futures complete. So, let's store the result over here, look at the type of this variable, that is a completable future of void. Why is it void? Because each completable future may return a different data type, one might return a string, another completable future might return an integer, so there is no way for us to know what is the final result. That is why this is a completable future of void. It's like a task that will complete when many other tasks complete. So, here we can write all that then run, here we pass a runable object, and then print all tasks completed successfully. Let's run our program, so here's the message, but what if you want to get the result of each of these completable future objects? Well, that is very easy, we can call first that get, this will return the result of the first completable future, but in this case, this method is not going to block the current thread, because this code, this task is going to get executed asynchronously when all these tasks are complete. So at this point, when we call the get method on any of these completable futures, we get the result immediately, because the result is ready. So, we get the result of the first task and store it here. First result. we should wrap this inside a try catch block, so we get the first result and then print it. So here's the first result and our final message. 


Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:


CompletableFuturesDemo.java: 

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;

public class CompletableFuturesDemo {
    public static void show(){
        var first = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 1);
        var second = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 2);
        var third = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 3);

        var all = CompletableFuture.allOf(first, second, third);
        all.thenRun(() -> {
            System.out.println("All tasks completed Successfully");
        });
    }
}

CompletableFuturesDemo.java: 


package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;
import java.util.concurrent.ExecutionException;

public class CompletableFuturesDemo {
    public static void show(){
        var first = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 1);
        var second = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 2);
        var third = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 3);

        var all = CompletableFuture.allOf(first, second, third);
        all.thenRun(() -> {
            try {
                var firstResult = first.get();
                System.out.println(firstResult);
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                throw new RuntimeException(e);
            } catch (ExecutionException e) {
                throw new RuntimeException(e);
            }
            System.out.println("All tasks completed Successfully");
        });
    }
}










15. Waiting for the First Task: 

let's say there are two ways to get the current weather in a given city. Perhaps we have two different remote services, but sometimes one of these services responds a little bit slower. So we want to call these services concurrently, and as soon as we get a response, we're going to display it to the user. Let me show you how to do that. So, I'm going to create two completable futures. Here's the first one, completable future, that's supply async, let's say this is our slow link. So, in the body of this lambda I'm going to call long task, that's simulate, and then return 20. That is the current weather, let's say in New York City. Now we create a second task, complete about future, that's supply async, now this simulates a fast link, so here we return 20 immediately. Now we're going to call both these services concurrently, and as soon as one of them responds, we're going to print the result on the terminal. So, here we call completable future, in the last video you learned about all of, with this we can wait for the completion of all tasks, we have another method called any of, this returns a new completable future that will complete as soon as one of these completable futures that we pass here completes. So, just like before, we have var args here, we can pass any number of completable futures, I'm going to pass first and second, this will give us a new completable future, let's store it in this variable, fastest, look at the type of this variable, it's a completable future of object. Now, right after we get the result, we can call then accept, we're going to consume the result, so we get the temperature and print it on a terminal. let's reformat our code, that is better, so run, see, we got the result immediately, we didn't have to wait 3 seconds to get the current weather from our slow service. 


Codes and Other Notes Referred in this Discussion:

package executors;

import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;

public class CompletableFuturesDemo {
    public static void show(){
        var first = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> {
            LongTask.simulate();
            return 20;
        });

        var second = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 20);

        CompletableFuture.anyOf(first, second)
                .thenAccept(temp -> System.out.println(temp));

    }
}
